📁 Week05/ChallengeLab.md
Title: “Detect, Break, and Harden: Real-World IaC Security Scenarios”

🎯 Goal
This lab simulates what happens when:

A team member adds insecure changes to infrastructure

A bad IaC policy goes unnoticed in a PR

Cloud resources are manually changed (drift)

You accidentally commit secrets

You’ll be the DevSecOps engineer responsible for catching and fixing these before deployment or audit.

🧩 Scenario Setup
You’ve already built secure infrastructure in Terraform, CDK, and CloudFormation.
Now you’ll:

Introduce intentional vulnerabilities

Detect them with security tools

Enforce policy gates before deploy

Simulate cloud drift and reconcile changes

🧱 Section 1: Terraform Misconfiguration + Detection
🔧 A. Inject Insecure Config
Open ecs.tf and add the following security vulnerability:

resource "aws_security_group" "open_all" {
  name        = "open-all-sg"
  description = "Opens all ports (bad)"
  vpc_id      = module.vpc.vpc_id

  ingress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 65535
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  egress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 0
    protocol    = "-1"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}
What’s wrong:

Opens all TCP ports to the world (0.0.0.0/0)

Egress is unrestricted

Violates principle of least privilege and PCI/ISO/OWASP rules

🔍 B. Run Checkov
checkov -d .
✅ Expected output:

Check: CKV_AWS_260: "Security group allows unrestricted ingress"
Result: FAILED
File: ecs.tf:1
...
💡 Why Checkov?
It codifies security policies into rules. You can enforce this in your CI pipelines to block bad merges or deploys.

🛠 C. Fix It
Update the security group:

ingress {
  from_port   = 443
  to_port     = 443
  protocol    = "tcp"
  cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/16"] # internal only
}
Run Checkov again:

checkov -d .
✅ Should pass now.

🧱 Section 2: CloudFormation Secret Leak + Linting
🔧 A. Inject Secret in Template
In stack.yaml, simulate a mistake:

DBPassword:
  Type: AWS::SSM::Parameter
  Properties:
    Name: /notestream/db_password
    Type: String
    Value: 'super-secret-plaintext-password'
🔍 B. Run cfn-lint and cfn_nag
cfn-lint stack.yaml
Then:

cfn_nag_scan --input-path stack.yaml
✅ Expected warnings:

W58: Parameter DBPassword should not use plain-text values
🧠 Use NoEcho: true and encrypted SSM parameters to store real secrets. Never commit plaintext values.

🧱 Section 3: AWS CDK Insecure Defaults + cdk-nag
🔧 A. Add Open SG in CDK
In infra_stack.py:

sg = ec2.SecurityGroup(self, "BadSG", vpc=vpc, allow_all_outbound=True)
sg.add_ingress_rule(ec2.Peer.any_ipv4(), ec2.Port.tcp(22), "Open SSH access")
🔍 B. Add cdk-nag to Detect It
Install:

pip install cdk-nag
Update your stack:

from cdk_nag import AwsSolutionsChecks
Aspects.of(self).add(AwsSolutionsChecks())
Run:

cdk synth
✅ Should show something like:

[Warning] AwsSolutions-EC23: Security group allows ingress from 0.0.0.0/0 to port 22
🛠 C. Fix It
Update rule:

sg.add_ingress_rule(ec2.Peer.ipv4("10.0.0.0/16"), ec2.Port.tcp(22), "Internal only")
🧱 Section 4: Drift Detection with Terraform
🐛 A. Simulate Drift
Go to the AWS Console → change a subnet name manually (or add an extra tag).

🔍 B. Detect Drift
terraform plan
✅ Output:

~ aws_subnet.public[0]
      tags.Name: "old" => "new"
🧠 Why this matters:
Manual changes break IaC consistency. Plan should always reflect infra state.

🛠 C. Reconcile
Option 1: Accept drift

terraform refresh
Option 2: Enforce source-of-truth

terraform apply
🧱 Section 5: Commit Secrets Accidentally + Detect
🔧 A. Simulate a Leaked Secret
echo "AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=1234567890" >> .env
git add .env
git commit -m "oops"
🔍 B. Run GitLeaks
gitleaks detect --source .
✅ Output:

Secret detected: AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY
🔐 Add gitleaks to pre-commit or CI to block merge on exposed secrets.

✅ Challenge Completion Checklist
Challenge	Status
Insecure SG in Terraform	✅ Detected with Checkov & tfsec
Plaintext secret in CF	✅ Flagged with cfn-lint & cfn_nag
Overly open rule in CDK	✅ Caught with cdk-nag
Secret committed to Git	✅ Detected with gitleaks
Manual infra drift	✅ Detected with Terraform plan
📘 Summary: DevSecOps IaC Maturity Practices
Practice	Benefit
Scan infra code pre-deploy	Catch CVEs & misconfigs early
Block secrets in source code	Prevent long-term breaches
Detect drift	Enforce single source of truth
Use tools across IaC flavors	Terraform, CDK, CFN all need enforcement
Shift-left via pipelines	Ensure Dev & Ops stay aligned with security

📁 Week05/Security.md
Title: “Securing Infrastructure as Code: Risks, Threats & Controls”

🧠 Why IaC Security Matters in DevSecOps
In traditional ops, a misconfigured port or admin role is a one-off mistake.
In IaC, that same mistake replicates across all environments, automates insecurity, and becomes auditable risk.

🚨 Common IaC Threat Scenarios
Threat Scenario	Description
Open networking	SGs or NACLs expose 0.0.0.0/0 to ports like 22 or 80 — used in lateral movement, initial access
Excessive IAM roles	Admin privileges granted to ECS tasks, Lambdas, CI/CD roles
Public S3 buckets	Accidental BlockPublicAccess = false via IaC
Hardcoded secrets	Passwords/API keys in template.yaml, .tf, app.py
Unscanned AMIs/images	Use of base images with CVEs or log4j
Resource drift	Infra modified outside code (console edits, quick fixes)
Unapproved changes merged	Lack of code reviews/policy enforcement allows insecure commits
🔐 Layered Security Controls for IaC
Control Type	Tools / Techniques
Static Analysis (SAST)	checkov, tfsec, cfn-lint, cdk-nag scan IaC pre-deploy
Secrets Detection	gitleaks, truffleHog, GitHub secret scanning
Policy-as-Code	OPA, conftest, tflint rules, Rego policies enforce org guardrails
CI/CD Gates	Require scans to pass in pipelines before terraform apply or cdk deploy
IAM Linting	Tools like iam-policy-validator, cloudsplaining, parliament
Cloud Drift Detection	terraform plan, cdk diff, CloudFormation Drift detection
Versioning & Rollback	All infra code in Git; use tags, changelogs, PR approvals
Least Privilege	ECS/Lambda roles get only s3:GetObject, not s3:*
Enforced Inputs	Avoid inline values — use variables.tf, Parameters blocks, or env vars
Environment Isolation	Use per-env folders or workspaces (dev, qa, prod) with restricted roles per env
✅ Secure Defaults to Always Enforce in IaC
Resource Type	Secure Default
VPC	Private subnets, NAT-only egress, no internet gateway unless needed
S3 Buckets	BlockPublicAccess = true, encryption enabled
ECS Tasks	Use execution roles with minimum permissions
Lambda	No wildcard IAM, use managed policies or scoped inline ones
Secrets	Use AWS Secrets Manager / SSM Parameter Store — never hardcode
Logs	Enable retention + encryption (CloudWatch, S3 access logs)
ECR	Enable scan_on_push, use immutable tags
Subnets	No public IP auto-assign unless explicitly needed
API Gateway	Use auth (JWT, API Key, Cognito) — default is open unless locked
🧪 Security Testing You Should Automate
Phase	What to Test	Tools
Pre-Commit	Secrets in code, insecure configs	gitleaks, tflint, pre-commit, checkov
CI	Full IaC scan, approval gates	checkov, tfsec, OPA, GitHub/GitLab runners
Pre-Deploy	terraform plan, cdk diff, manual approval	Manual gate + audit logs
Post-Deploy	Runtime drift, policy scan, runtime CVEs	AWS Config Rules, drift detection, trivy on container runtime
Audit	IAM policies, security groups, logging	cloudsplaining, Prowler, AWS IAM Analyzer
🔁 Secure IaC Lifecycle in DevSecOps
Git Commit ──▶ Pre-commit Checks (gitleaks, lint) ──▶ PR Review (OPA, tfsec) ──▶ 
CI Pipeline (Terraform Plan + Security Gate) ──▶ Manual Approve or Auto Merge ──▶ 
Deployment (Apply, Deploy, Drift Monitor) ──▶ Alerts, Metrics, Logs ──▶ Feedback Loop
🏗️ Threat Modeling Your Stack (Example)
Component	Risk	Control
vpc.tf	Exposes subnet to public	Use map_public_ip_on_launch = false
ecs.tf	IAM role has *:* access	Use scoped permissions like ssm:GetParameter
secrets.tf	Secrets injected inline	Use Terraform variables and AWS Secret Manager
CDK app.py	SG allows SSH from 0.0.0.0	Block in cdk-nag rule
CloudFormation	No retention policy on logs	Add RetentionInDays: 14 to log groups
SAM	Lambda assumes admin role	Use Policies: SecretsManagerReadOnly only
CI/CD Pipeline	Applies Terraform without approval	Use terraform plan + manual apply step gated by pipeline
🧠 Final Takeaways
IaC is code — so treat it like code:

Review it

Scan it

Test it

Secure it

Use security tooling that developers don’t hate

Automate feedback loops (pre-merge, pre-deploy, post-deploy)

Use Git as your source of truth, not the AWS console

🛡️ This week’s challenge has shown that IaC is one of the most powerful enablers — or risks — in DevSecOps.

Title: “From Manual to Intentional: Lessons in Securing Infrastructure as Code”

🧠 What You Learned

➜ to

Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) with security-first mindset.

You explored and compared multiple tools:

Tool	Purpose	Strength

AWS CDK	Imperative, code-driven IaC	Reusable in Python/TS, IDE-friendly

Policy scans (checkov, tfsec, cdk-nag)

Secrets detection

Cloud drift awareness

Least privilege enforcement

🔄 Why This Week Matters in DevSecOps
DevSecOps is not about “slapping security on at the end.”
It’s about building secure defaults into how infrastructure is created and changed.

This module puts security into the earliest part of the SDLC:

SDLC Phase	Security Injection
Plan	Threat modeling IaC misconfig
Code	Static analysis, secrets scanning, policy-as-code
Build	Template validation, IAM sanity checks
Deploy	Manual gates, drift detection
Run	Retention, logging, least privilege enforcement

Inject secrets into containers via Secrets Manager

Enforce security pre-deploy and during CI/CD

This is the “infrastructure” behind your microservices — and it’s now secure, testable, and version-controlled.

You’d be responsible for guardrailing IaC pipelines

You’d enforce policy-as-code and block insecure merges

Concept	Reflection
IaC is a source of risk and control	Treat it with the same rigor as app code
Declarative vs Imperative IaC	Choose based on team skill, audit needs, and cloud target
Drift is real	Don’t rely on docs — enforce real state
Pre-commit checks catch the bad stuff early	Fast feedback = fewer bugs and breaches
Secrets belong in secure systems	Not in your .tf files, .env, or Git history
Policy-as-Code scales security	OPA, tfsec, and Checkov give you automated guardrails
✅ You're now capable of owning secure infrastructure delivery from design to deploy.

📁 Week06/Overview.md
Title: Secure CI/CD Pipelines & Artifact Management
Theme: Automating secure builds, artifact scanning, and safe multi-environment deployments

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you’ll have:

Built secure CI/CD pipelines using GitLab and GitHub Actions

Integrated infrastructure + app deployment (Terraform, CDK, SAM)

Enforced pre-merge and pre-deploy security gates (e.g., checkov, trivy, gitleaks)

Implemented branch-to-environment promotion (Dev → QA → Stable → UAT)

Published signed, scanned artifacts to ECR and S3

Used OpenID Connect (OIDC) for secure deploys without long-lived credentials

Set up multiple CI agents to handle different app components securely (Python, Node.js)

🔍 Why This Matters in DevSecOps
DevOps accelerates delivery — but without security, it accelerates risk.
DevSecOps ensures that automation enforces your security policies, not bypasses them.

This module shifts security left by:

Blocking misconfigurations before merge (checkov, trivy, gitleaks)

Preventing secrets or vulnerable code from entering production

Requiring approval for high-trust actions like terraform apply

Making security posture observable in the pipeline itself

🧱 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🔄 CI/CD Lifecycle in DevSecOps
Stage	Example Tasks	Security Layer
Commit	Lint, unit test	Pre-commit checks, secrets scanning
CI	Build, test, scan	SAST, IaC scan, SBOM generation
Artifact	Push to ECR/S3	Sign image, scan again (Trivy)
CD	Deploy infra/app	Gate with plan, require manual approval
Post-deploy	Monitor	Drift detection, runtime scanning (later week)
2. 📦 Artifact Security
Artifacts are deployable units — containers, Lambda packages, zip files.
A secure pipeline:

Scans them before push (CVEs, licenses)

Signs them with a key

Stores them in immutable registries (ECR, S3)

Tags them semantically (v1.2.0-dev, qa-rc1, uat-prod-candidate)

Tracks provenance via Git commit or SBOM (later)

3. 🔐 Secrets & Credential Management in Pipelines
You’ll:

Use GitLab & GitHub OIDC to deploy to AWS without hardcoded secrets

Avoid plaintext environment variables (use GitHub/GitLab Secret Stores)

Manage access by environment — UAT deploys need reviewer approval, dev does not

4. 🌱 Environment Promotion (Branch-to-Env Mapping)
Git Branch	Environment	Access Policy
develop	dev	Auto-deploy, fast feedback
qa	QA	Deploy on merge + SAST/IaC approval
stable	staging	Requires pipeline pass + reviewer
uat	UAT	Controlled deploy from stable only
This will set the foundation for RBAC, audit trails, and deployment hygiene.

🛠️ Tooling Breakdown
Tool	Role
GitLab CI/CD	Declarative pipelines (YAML) with security jobs
GitHub Actions	Event-based CI/CD, great for OpenID and fine-grained workflows
Terraform + CDK + SAM	Infrastructure as Code delivery
Trivy	Container, IaC, and artifact scanner
Gitleaks	Detect hardcoded secrets in commits
Checkov / tfsec	IaC misconfiguration detection
AWS ECR / S3	Secure artifact registry with image scanning
AWS OIDC	Deploy to AWS from GitHub/GitLab without long-term AWS keys
🔄 Application Evolution This Week
We're now evolving our monorepo into a full DevSecOps-ready application.

You'll:

Containerize both the Python backend and Node.js frontend

Push signed/scanned images to ECR

Deploy via CI/CD using approved infrastructure plans

Route each environment (dev, qa, staging, uat) with namespace isolation (ECS Fargate)

Lay the foundation for SBOMs and runtime policies (future modules)

✅ By End of Week 6, You Will Be Able To:
Build secure CI pipelines for microservices + infrastructure

Auto-deploy safely to multiple environments

Block insecure merges/deploys using pipeline policy gates

Securely manage pipeline credentials (OIDC vs access keys)

Run IaC, container, and secrets scans as part of every PR and commit

📁 Week06/GuidedLab.md
Title: Build Secure CI/CD for App + Infrastructure + Artifact Management

🧠 What You’ll Do
Set up secure GitHub Actions and GitLab pipelines

Automate secure deployment of:

Python backend (as container)

Node.js frontend (as container)

ECS infra (via Terraform/CDK)

Add security gates:

checkov, trivy, gitleaks, cfn-lint

Push signed & scanned images to AWS ECR

Implement OIDC for secretless AWS deploy

Use Git branches to promote between dev → qa → stable → uat

🧱 PART 1: Prep Your Repositories (Monorepo Setup)
repo/
├── .github/workflows/        ← GitHub Actions (or .gitlab-ci.yml)
├── infrastructure/
│   ├── terraform/            ← ECS, VPC, ALB, etc.
│   ├── cdk/
│   └── sam/
├── backend/                  ← Python Flask or FastAPI
├── frontend/                 ← Node.js (e.g. Vite + React)
└── scripts/ci/               ← Shared scan/publish scripts
🔐 PART 2: Secure GitHub → AWS OIDC Authentication
✅ Why use OIDC?
No AWS access keys.
No secret rotation.
No storage of credentials.
Only valid builds from your repo can assume roles.

Step 1: Create OIDC IAM Role
aws iam create-role --role-name GitHubDeployRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://trust-policy.json
trust-policy.json:

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": {
      "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::<account_id>:oidc-provider/token.actions.githubusercontent.com"
    },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": {
        "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com",
        "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub": "repo:<owner>/<repo>:ref:refs/heads/dev"
      }
    }
  }]
}
🎯 This allows only the dev branch in your repo to assume the role.

Step 2: Attach Minimal Policies
aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name GitHubDeployRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonEC2ContainerRegistryFullAccess

aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name GitHubDeployRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AWSCloudFormationFullAccess
Later, use custom least-privilege roles per environment.

🤖 PART 3: GitHub Actions CI/CD for Python + Node + Infra
📦 Step 1: Secure CI for Backend
Create .github/workflows/backend.yml

name: Backend CI/CD

on:
  push:
    branches: [dev, qa, stable, uat]

permissions:
  id-token: write
  contents: read

jobs:
  build-and-push:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest

    steps:
    - uses: actions/checkout@v4

    - name: Set up Docker Buildx
      uses: docker/setup-buildx-action@v3

    - name: Login to ECR
      uses: aws-actions/amazon-ecr-login@v2

    - name: Build backend
      run: |
        docker build -t backend:${{ github.sha }} ./backend
        docker tag backend:${{ github.sha }} ${{ secrets.ECR_REGISTRY }}/backend:${{ github.sha }}

    - name: Trivy Image Scan
      uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
      with:
        image-ref: ${{ secrets.ECR_REGISTRY }}/backend:${{ github.sha }}

    - name: Push to ECR
      run: docker push ${{ secrets.ECR_REGISTRY }}/backend:${{ github.sha }}

    - name: Assume OIDC Role
      uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
      with:
        role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::<account>:role/GitHubDeployRole
        aws-region: us-east-1
🧪 Step 2: Secure CI for Infra
Add .github/workflows/infra.yml

name: Terraform CI

on:
  pull_request:
    paths:
      - 'infrastructure/**'

jobs:
  validate:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
    - uses: actions/checkout@v4

    - name: Install tools
      run: |
        curl -sSL https://install.terraform.io | bash
        pip install checkov

    - name: Terraform Init & Plan
      run: |
        cd infrastructure/terraform
        terraform init
        terraform plan

    - name: Checkov IaC Scan
      run: checkov -d infrastructure/terraform
🔐 Step 3: Add Secrets Scan
Add to all CI jobs:

    - name: Secrets Scan
      uses: zricethezav/gitleaks-action@v2
📤 Step 4: Promote Environments via Branches
Branch	Auto Deploy?	Approval Needed?
dev	✅ yes	❌ no
qa	✅ yes	✅ after SAST, IaC pass
stable	✅ yes	✅ with reviewer
uat	✅ yes	✅ tagged artifact only
🛠️ PART 4: GitLab CI/CD Sample for Frontend
Add .gitlab-ci.yml:

stages:
  - lint
  - test
  - build
  - scan
  - deploy

lint:
  stage: lint
  script:
    - npm install
    - npm run lint

test:
  stage: test
  script:
    - npm test

build:
  stage: build
  script:
    - docker build -t $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE/frontend:$CI_COMMIT_SHORT_SHA ./frontend
    - docker push $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE/frontend:$CI_COMMIT_SHORT_SHA

scan:
  stage: scan
  script:
    - trivy image $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE/frontend:$CI_COMMIT_SHORT_SHA

deploy:
  stage: deploy
  only:
    - dev
  script:
    - aws ecs update-service --cluster dev-cluster --service frontend --force-new-deployment
🔐 Add secret variables (e.g. AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, OIDC_ROLE) in GitLab settings.

✅ You’ve Now Covered:
✅ Secure builds for both backend & frontend
✅ Scan containers, IaC, and secrets in every pipeline
✅ Push only scanned artifacts to AWS ECR
✅ Promote between environments using Git branch control
✅ Deploy apps + infra securely without long-term secrets

Branching errors (QA gets a dev feature)

📁 Week06/ChallengeLab.md
Title: “Catch Me If You Can: CI/CD Incidents and Security Enforcement”

🧠 Scenario
Your company has CI/CD pipelines for its Python backend, Node.js frontend, and infrastructure (Terraform/CDK).
This week, the following events occur:

Incident	Description
🔥 Secret pushed to Git	A dev commits AWS credentials by mistake
❌ Insecure Terraform Merge	A team member bypasses security group validation
🐛 Vulnerable container built	Outdated image with CVEs is pushed
🚫 Deployment to UAT from wrong branch	A feature from dev accidentally gets to UAT
🌀 Drift between ECR and deployed task	Deployed version differs from tagged artifact
🤖 Developer tries to deploy manually from laptop	Bypassing CI/CD — no traceability
Your job is to simulate, detect, and block these before they cause damage.

🧪 Challenge 1: Secret Leak in Git Commit
Step 1: Simulate the Mistake
echo 'AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=abcd1234' >> backend/.env
git add backend/.env
git commit -m "oops: testing db fix"
Step 2: Run Gitleaks Manually
gitleaks detect --source .
✅ You should see output like:

Secret detected: AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY
🎯 Fail the pipeline on this detection.

Step 3: Add Gitleaks to CI
GitHub:

- name: Secrets Scan
  uses: zricethezav/gitleaks-action@v2
GitLab:

secrets:
  stage: scan
  script:
    - gitleaks detect --source .
🔥 Challenge 2: Insecure Terraform Code Merged
Step 1: Add Misconfig
In infrastructure/terraform/ecs.tf, add:

resource "aws_security_group_rule" "bad_rule" {
  type        = "ingress"
  from_port   = 0
  to_port     = 65535
  protocol    = "tcp"
  cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  security_group_id = aws_security_group.main.id
}
Step 2: Checkov Should Detect This
checkov -d infrastructure/terraform
✅ Output:

[CKV_AWS_260] Security group allows unrestricted ingress from 0.0.0.0/0
Step 3: Enforce in CI
Block the merge unless checkov passes.

- name: Checkov Scan
  run: checkov -d infrastructure/terraform
🐳 Challenge 3: Vulnerable Image Gets Deployed
Step 1: Build Image with Vulnerable Package
In Dockerfile:

FROM python:3.8
RUN pip install flask==1.0.0
Flask 1.0.0 has known CVEs

Step 2: Run Trivy Locally
trivy image backend:vuln
✅ You should see:

[HIGH] Flask 1.0.0 has XX vulnerabilities...
Step 3: Enforce in CI
Add to pipeline:

- name: Trivy Image Scan
  uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
  with:
    image-ref: backend:${{ github.sha }}
    severity: HIGH,CRITICAL
    exit-code: 1
🛡️ If HIGH or CRITICAL CVEs are found, the job fails.

🌀 Challenge 4: Wrong Branch Deployed to UAT
Step 1: Simulate Branch Confusion
A dev merges a feature to dev, but QA approves it accidentally into uat.

Step 2: Validate ref Before Deploying
Update CI job to block unauthorized deploy:

if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/uat' && contains(github.event.head_commit.message, '[uat-approved]')
🔐 Tag or annotate commits to uat with required approvals.

🧾 Challenge 5: ECR Image != Deployed Version
Step 1: Push Image
docker build -t backend:v1.2.0 .
docker push <ecr>/backend:v1.2.0
Step 2: Deploy Incorrectly Tagged Task
Manually deploy the wrong image version (v1.0.0).

Step 3: Add ECR Drift Check Job
Use ECR CLI or script:

aws ecs describe-tasks ... | jq '.[] | .containers[].image' 
Compare with Git tag or commit hash.

✅ Add pipeline check:

if deployed_image != tagged_artifact:
   echo "Drift detected!"
   exit 1
👨‍💻 Challenge 6: Manual Deploy from Dev Machine
Step 1: Simulate
Run terraform apply or aws ecs update-service directly from a dev laptop.

Step 2: Catch Unauthorized Role Use
Use AWS CloudTrail to log role usage:

aws cloudtrail lookup-events --lookup-attributes AttributeKey=Username,AttributeValue=DevUser
✅ This should alert if deploys bypass CI OIDC or IAM roles.

✅ Checklist
Incident	Tool	Detection
Secrets in Git	Gitleaks	Pre-commit, CI scan
Insecure IaC	Checkov, tfsec	Fail merge
Vulnerable containers	Trivy	Fail build
Wrong env promotion	Git branch policy	Tag validation
Artifact drift	ECS + ECR compare	Alert and rollback
Manual deploys	IAM Role usage + CloudTrail	Alert and block

📁 Week06/Security.md
Title: “Securing the Pipeline: Shifting Left in CI/CD”

🧠 Why CI/CD Security is Core to DevSecOps
CI/CD is the heartbeat of DevOps — it touches:

Your code

Your infra

Your credentials

Your production environments

If an attacker compromises your pipeline, they can bypass all runtime controls — making CI/CD a critical security boundary.

🔍 Key Threats in CI/CD Pipelines
Risk	Description
Secrets in repos or env vars	Hardcoded keys in code or exposed to builds
Unscanned IaC/app code	Misconfigurations go live without detection
Artifact tampering	Images pushed without scanning, tagging, or validation
Manual deploys from dev machines	Bypasses audit logs and policy gates
Unapproved code merged to secure branches	No human in the loop for prod
Over-permissive CI roles	CI can modify anything in AWS/GCP without restrictions
Shadow pipelines	Old/insecure pipelines with outdated runners
🔐 Essential CI/CD Security Controls
Layer	Control
Source Control	Protected branches, required PR approvals, code owners
Pre-Commit	Hooks with gitleaks, checkov, trivy config scan
CI Security Gates	Fail pipelines on SAST/IaC/secrets/CVE violations
Artifact Control	Immutable ECR/S3, signed tags, image scanning on push
IAM Access	OIDC role assumption (no long-lived AWS secrets)
Manual Approvals	Required for terraform apply, prod deploy
Multi-Env Isolation	Git branch + env mapping (dev/qa/stage/uat/prod)
Audit Logging	CloudTrail on assumed roles and deploy actions
RBAC in CI/CD	GitLab groups, GitHub environments with role policies
✅ Secure CI/CD Architecture Pattern
             +------------------------+
             |        Source Code     |
             |    (GitHub/GitLab)     |
             +------------------------+
                       |
                 (Webhook Trigger)
                       |
               +-----------------+
               |     Pipeline    |
               |  (GitHub/GitLab)|
               +-----------------+
             /       |         \
            /        |          \
     +---------+ +----------+ +-----------+
     | IaC Scan| | Secrets  | | Container |
     | Checkov | | Gitleaks | | Trivy     |
     +---------+ +----------+ +-----------+
            \         |          /
             \        |         /
              \       |        /
              +----------------+
              |    Approval    |
              |  (Manual Gate) |
              +----------------+
                       |
                  +-----------+
                  | Deploy to |
                  |   AWS     |
                  +-----------+
                       |
               (OIDC AssumeRole)

🔧 Secure Pipeline Practices by Tool
Tool	Best Practices
GitHub Actions	Use OIDC with least-privilege roles; protect environments; use required reviewers
GitLab CI/CD	Store secrets in CI/CD Variables; use environments + approval jobs; enable pipeline policies
Terraform	Run plan + checkov before apply; avoid auto-apply for prod
SAM/CDK	Scan templates with cfn-lint, cdk-nag; require diffs in MR
Docker	Use trivy, sign/tag images; fail builds on critical CVEs
AWS Deploys	Use IAM roles per environment; deny non-CI access via policy
ECR/S3	Mark artifacts immutable; require scanOnPush for ECR
🧪 Detecting & Blocking CI/CD Attacks
Threat	Detection	Control
Secret leaked in commit	gitleaks, GitHub secret scan	Fail PR, alert dev
Insecure SG in Terraform	checkov in CI	Block merge
CVE in backend image	trivy in CI	Block build step
Dev deploys manually	IAM policy audit, CloudTrail	Limit role access to CI only
Wrong branch to UAT	github.ref check	Require annotated commit or PR approval
🧠 Final Principles
CI/CD is not just automation — it’s a security boundary

Shift security left — run scans at every phase (commit, build, deploy)

Replace “hope-based” deploys with policy-enforced pipelines

Monitor drift between intended state (CI) and deployed state (AWS)

🔐 Your New Security Responsibilities
As a DevSecOps Engineer, you are now responsible for:

Responsibility	Impact
Designing pipelines that block insecure deploys	Protects prod from human error
Implementing guardrails, not gates	Empowers devs with safe defaults
Managing role-based CI/CD access	Limits blast radius of credential misuse
Observing and alerting on pipeline anomalies	Reduces dwell time of pipeline attacks
Teaching others to scan before they build	Culture of secure engineering
✅ CI/CD is now secured, monitored, and enforced — not just scripted.
You're operating at real-world DevSecOps level.

📁 Week07/Overview.md
Title: Secure Container Runtime & Image Provenance
Theme: From scanning at build-time to verifying at runtime

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you will:

Enable runtime threat detection with Amazon Inspector (ECS Fargate)

Generate and track Software Bills of Materials (SBOMs)

Sign and verify your containers with Sigstore/Cosign

Enforce only signed, scanned, and approved images can run

Restrict container networking per environment using ECS Service Connect

Detect runtime drift between what's deployed vs. what's approved

🧠 Why This Week Matters in DevSecOps
DevSecOps isn’t just about shifting left.
It’s about full-spectrum coverage — from commit to runtime to incident response.

While you’ve secured your CI/CD pipelines and artifacts:

There’s still risk after deployment: image swaps, runtime vulnerabilities, lateral movement, config drift.

This week helps you lock runtime down with real-time verification, network controls, and container provenance.

🧱 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🔍 Runtime Scanning vs. Build-Time Scanning
Build Time	Runtime
Trivy, Checkov, Gitleaks	Amazon Inspector, Falco, CloudTrail, ECS event stream
Known before deploy	Catches issues introduced after deploy
Validates before push	Detects CVEs, malware, or changes in prod
Static	Dynamic (behavioral)
⚠️ You can pass a Trivy scan today, and be vulnerable tomorrow.

2. 📜 Software Bill of Materials (SBOM)
SBOM is like a nutritional label for your container — listing every library, version, and origin.

Helps detect vulnerable transitive deps

Required for government and regulated environments

Generated using tools like Syft, Trivy, Docker SBOM

Example output:

{
  "name": "flask",
  "version": "2.2.5",
  "purl": "pkg:pypi/flask@2.2.5"
}
3. 🔐 Signing Containers with Cosign (Sigstore)
Container signing ensures:

Only verified, unaltered images run

You can trace the image to a Git commit, pipeline run, or dev

You’ll use:

Cosign: Sign container with GitHub Actions identity (cosign sign)

Policy: Only run images that are signed + match commit hash

4. 🕵️‍♂️ Drift Detection
"Drift" is when your deployed state no longer matches your approved build.

You’ll:

Detect containers running unscanned or unsigned images

Alert if ECS task uses a SHA or tag that was never deployed via CI/CD

5. 🌐 Runtime Access Control via ECS Service Connect
You’ll implement:

Per-environment namespaces (dev/qa/staging/uat)

Cross-environment communication rules:

dev-backend ❌ cannot call dev-backend

dev-service-a ✅ can call staging-service-b

uat isolates everything — allow-listed communication only

This allows:

Secure testing across environments

Prevention of lateral movement

Zero-trust-by-default in container communication

🔧 Tooling Breakdown
Tool	Purpose
Amazon Inspector v2	Runtime CVE scanner for ECS/Lambda
AWS Systems Manager	Pull runtime metadata
Cosign	Sign/verify OCI images
Trivy	Generate SBOMs
Syft	Advanced SBOM generator
ECS Service Connect	Enforced service-to-service controls
IAM Roles for Tasks	Runtime identity enforcement
AWS CloudTrail + ECR Events	Detect unauthorized image changes
GitHub Actions / GitLab CI	Signing + SBOM generation pipeline integration
🧩 How This Evolves the App
This Week	App Evolution
Signatures & SBOMs	Every image built by CI will be traceable and verified at runtime
Runtime CVE alerts	If new CVEs affect running images, you’ll be notified immediately
Environment network policy	You enforce who can talk to who in each namespace
UAT hardening	UAT will now block unregistered services from even making a connection
Secure registries	Only signed, approved images will be pulled from ECR
✅ By End of Week 7, You Will Be Able To:
Sign container images automatically and verify them before deploy

Generate, store, and inspect SBOMs for your services

Detect runtime CVEs and container drift with Inspector

Enforce per-environment container isolation (via ECS Service Connect)

Build defense-in-depth: scan before build, sign before deploy, verify at runtime

📁 Week07/GuidedLab.md
Title: Runtime Integrity, Provenance & Environment Isolation

🧠 Lab Goals
By the end of this lab, you'll have:

Signed your container images via CI/CD using Cosign

Generated SBOMs using Trivy and Syft

Enabled Amazon Inspector runtime scanning for ECS

Verified container signatures before ECS deployment

Enforced network isolation policies via ECS Service Connect

Detected drift between signed image and deployed runtime task

🧱 Lab Structure
Stage	Tools
Image Signing	Cosign + GitHub OIDC
SBOM Generation	Trivy + Syft
Runtime Scanning	Amazon Inspector
Signature Verification	Cosign + deploy gating
ECS Isolation	ECS Service Connect, namespaces
Drift Monitoring	ECR tags + ECS task metadata comparison
🛠️ PART 1: Container Signing with Cosign
🧾 Step 1: Install Cosign Locally (for testing)
brew install cosign  # macOS
# OR
curl -sSL https://github.com/sigstore/cosign/releases/latest/download/cosign-linux-amd64 \
  -o /usr/local/bin/cosign && chmod +x /usr/local/bin/cosign
🔐 Step 2: Sign Image via GitHub Actions
Add to your pipeline (backend.yml or frontend.yml):

- name: Sign Docker image with Cosign
  run: |
    cosign sign --yes \
      --key env://COSIGN_PRIVATE_KEY \
      ${{ secrets.ECR_REGISTRY }}/backend:${{ github.sha }}
  env:
    COSIGN_PRIVATE_KEY: ${{ secrets.COSIGN_PRIVATE_KEY }}
    COSIGN_PASSWORD: ${{ secrets.COSIGN_PASSWORD }}
🔐 Store the COSIGN_PRIVATE_KEY and COSIGN_PASSWORD in GitHub Secrets.

Use GitHub Actions OIDC instead of a static key (recommended for prod):

cosign sign --yes --identity-token $ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN <image>
✅ Step 3: Verify Image Signature (Before Deploy)
cosign verify $ECR_REGISTRY/backend:<tag>
❌ CI fails if image is unsigned or modified.

📦 PART 2: Generate SBOMs
Step 1: Generate SBOM with Trivy
Add to pipeline:

- name: Generate SBOM
  run: |
    trivy image --format cyclonedx \
      --output sbom-backend.json \
      ${{ secrets.ECR_REGISTRY }}/backend:${{ github.sha }}
🎯 Store this in S3, SSM Parameter Store, or attach to build artifacts.

Step 2: Generate SBOM with Syft
syft $ECR_REGISTRY/backend:${{ github.sha }} -o json > sbom-backend.json
🔍 PART 3: Enable Amazon Inspector for Runtime CVE Scanning
Step 1: Enable Inspector in Region
aws inspector2 enable --resource-types EC2 ECR
ECS on Fargate automatically integrates via ECR scanning.

Step 2: Enable Scan on Push (ECR)
aws ecr put-image-scanning-configuration \
  --repository-name backend \
  --image-scanning-configuration scanOnPush=true
Step 3: View Inspector Findings
aws inspector2 list-findings \
  --filter criteria='{"repositoryName":[{"value":"backend","comparison":"EQUALS"}]}'
🚦 PART 4: Gate ECS Deployments Based on Signature Verification
Update deployment pipeline:

- name: Verify Image Signature
  run: |
    cosign verify $ECR_REGISTRY/backend:${{ github.sha }}
❌ Block deploy if unsigned or tampered.

Add optional signature check in ECS task definition label or tag:

"imageTag": "backend:git-sha-verified"
🌐 PART 5: ECS Service Connect Isolation Rules
Step 1: Enable Namespaces Per Environment
Create namespaces:

aws servicediscovery create-namespace --name dev --type HTTP
aws servicediscovery create-namespace --name qa --type HTTP
Assign services to namespace:

service_connect_configuration {
  enabled = true
  namespace = "dev"
  services {
    port_name = "backend"
    client_alias {
      port = 8080
    }
  }
}
Step 2: Block Same-Service Calls in Dev
In ECS Task Security Groups:

# Allow frontend to backend
ingress {
  from_port = 8080
  to_port = 8080
  protocol = "tcp"
  security_groups = [frontend_sg]
}

# Deny backend → backend
Step 3: Allow Dev Service A → Staging Service B
Use Service Connect + hardcoded aliasing:

client_alias {
  dns_name = "staging-b.backend.staging"
  port = 9090
}
🌀 PART 6: Detect Container Drift
Step 1: Get Running Task Image
aws ecs describe-tasks --tasks <id> \
  --cluster <cluster> \
  --query "tasks[].containers[].image"
Step 2: Compare with Signed Tag
Use:

cosign verify <ECR_IMAGE_URI>
Fail if:

Image doesn’t match latest signed SHA

Container was launched manually or from unsigned pipeline

✅ You’ve Now Secured the Runtime
Layer	Secured How
Build → Deploy	Cosign signing + Trivy SBOM
Registry	Amazon Inspector scans, scanOnPush enabled
Runtime	ECS + Inspector alerts + ECS task metadata
Networking	ECS Service Connect with namespace isolation
Drift Detection	Signature check vs running task hash

📁 Week07/ChallengeLab.md
Title: “Runtime Under Siege: Drift, Versions, and Secure Communication”

🧠 Challenge Overview
This lab simulates real-world runtime security threats across the following categories:

Category	Scenario
🧪 Image Drift	Someone swaps out a signed container in ECS
🔓 Unsigned Deploy	An image is deployed without Cosign signature
❌ CVE Discovered Post-Deploy	CVE appears in running container after initial scan
🔄 Version Hijack	Dev service talks to wrong version of staging service
🌐 Network Violation	Dev backend makes unauthorized call to another dev backend
🛂 Signature Verification Bypass	Deployment skips Cosign check
🔧 Pre-requisites
SBOMs and signed images generated in CI (cosign, trivy)

ECS Fargate with Service Connect enabled

Inspector v2 enabled in AWS account

Services deployed in at least dev and staging environments

⚠️ Challenge 1: Image Drift in ECS
🔁 Step 1: Simulate Drift
Push a different image manually:

docker build -t backend:latest-drft .
docker tag backend:latest-drft <ECR_REPO>/backend:manual-push
docker push <ECR_REPO>/backend:manual-push
Update the ECS task manually:

aws ecs update-service \
  --cluster dev \
  --service backend \
  --force-new-deployment \
  --task-definition new-def-using-manual-image
🔍 Step 2: Detect Drift with Cosign + ECS Metadata
Script to detect:

IMAGE=$(aws ecs describe-tasks --tasks <task-id> --cluster dev \
  --query 'tasks[].containers[].image' --output text)

cosign verify $IMAGE || echo "⚠️ Image drift detected!"
✅ Action: Block this drift and roll back to last signed version.

🔒 Challenge 2: Deploy Unsigned Image to UAT
Step 1: Bypass Signing in CI
Comment out the cosign sign step and deploy.

Step 2: Add a Signature Verification Check
In GitLab or GitHub:

- name: Cosign Check
  run: |
    cosign verify ${{ secrets.ECR }}/backend:${{ github.sha }}
✅ Expect failure: no signature, deploy should be blocked.

🐞 Challenge 3: CVE Emerges in Runtime Container
You deployed a scanned image, but later a new CVE appears.

Step 1: Let Amazon Inspector Catch It
After push, monitor via:

aws inspector2 list-findings \
  --filter criteria='{"repositoryName":[{"value":"backend","comparison":"EQUALS"}]}'
✅ Expected: Inspector reports new CVE affecting Flask, libcurl, etc.

Step 2: Automate Slack/Jira Notification
Use EventBridge to trigger on CVE detection:

{
  "source": ["aws.inspector2"],
  "detail-type": ["Inspector2 Finding"]
}
🔄 Challenge 4: Version-Aware Cross-Environment Call
Dev Service A must explicitly call version 2 of Staging Service B

Step 1: Deploy Staging Service B v2
docker build -t service-b:v2 .
docker tag service-b:v2 <ECR>/service-b:v2
docker push <ECR>/service-b:v2
ECS Task Definition:

"containerDefinitions": [
  {
    "name": "service-b",
    "image": "<ECR>/service-b:v2",
    "environment": [{"name": "SERVICE_VERSION", "value": "v2"}]
  }
]
Step 2: Call Versioned Service Using Alias
In Dev Service A:

const response = await axios.get("http://staging-v2.service-b.staging:8080/health");
Service Connect Configuration (Dev):

client_alias {
  dns_name = "staging-v2.service-b.staging"
  port     = 8080
}
✅ This enforces calling only a specific version of the service.

Step 3: Block Call to Wrong Version
Test bad call:

const badCall = await axios.get("http://staging.service-b.staging:8080/health");
Expected: DNS fails or network blocked due to missing alias or firewall.

🔐 Challenge 5: Dev Backend Calls Another Dev Backend (Violation)
Step 1: Simulate Violation
From dev-service-A, make a call:

const response = await axios.post("http://dev-backend.dev:8080/api/internal");
Step 2: Detect and Block with ECS SG Rules
In Terraform:

resource "aws_security_group_rule" "deny_dev_backend_to_dev_backend" {
  type        = "ingress"
  from_port   = 8080
  to_port     = 8080
  protocol    = "tcp"
  cidr_blocks = [] # deny all
  security_group_id = aws_security_group.dev_backend.id
}
✅ Expect: timeout or connection refused.

🔁 Challenge 6: Simulate Signature Verification Bypass Attempt
Dev modifies the pipeline to skip Cosign check and tries to deploy unsigned image.

Step 1: Disable Cosign Check (simulated bad commit)
# comment out this
# - name: Cosign Verify ...
Step 2: Add Audit Trail to CI Config
- name: Detect CI Bypass
  run: |
    if ! grep -q "cosign verify" .gitlab-ci.yml; then
      echo "Pipeline missing security check"
      exit 1
    fi
✅ Fail the job if Cosign check is missing in CI config.

✅ Outcomes
Challenge	Detection/Prevention
Image drift	Cosign + ECS metadata
Unsigned deploy	Cosign verify gate
Runtime CVEs	Amazon Inspector alerts
Version hijack	DNS aliasing per version
Network violation	ECS SG + namespace
CI bypass	Self-check in pipeline
🔐 You’ve now simulated and hardened against real-world runtime threats using:

AWS native tools (Inspector, Service Connect, IAM)

Container provenance tooling (Cosign, Trivy, Syft)

Pipeline enforcement strategies (CI config validation)

You said:
The call with axios seems incorrect because it’s the service we versioned not the namespace. Also how do we enforce https calls within service connect? 
ChatGPT said:
Excellent questions — let's unpack and correct both points.

✅ Correction: Versioning the Service, Not the Namespace
You're absolutely right.

In ECS Service Connect, namespaces map to environments (e.g., dev, staging, uat).
But versioning should happen at the service name or endpoint level, not the namespace.

✅ Correct Pattern:
If you're running service-b v1 and v2 in the staging namespace, they should each register as distinct services in Service Connect.

🔧 Solution: Split Services per Version (same namespace)
# ECS Task Definition or Terraform ECS Service Module
service_connect_configuration {
  enabled = true
  namespace = "staging"

  services {
    port_name = "http"
    client_alias {
      dns_name = "service-b-v2"
      port     = 8080
    }
  }
}
Now your dev service can call:

const response = await axios.get("http://service-b-v2.staging:8080/health");
✅ service-b-v2 is now a version-specific ECS service name registered in Service Connect, inside the staging namespace.

🔐 Enforcing HTTPS in ECS Service Connect
By default, Service Connect uses plaintext HTTP internally over the VPC, unless you take extra steps.

🔐 Option 1: TLS Termination at Each Service (Recommended)
Each ECS service container runs with HTTPS support enabled (self-signed or ACM certs).

Service Connect uses TLS passthrough — it does not modify traffic.

🔧 Example
If backend uses Express with HTTPS:

https.createServer({ key, cert }, app).listen(443);
And client service calls:

axios.get("https://service-b-v2.staging:443/secure-endpoint", {
  httpsAgent: new https.Agent({ rejectUnauthorized: false }) // for dev/self-signed
});
✅ You enforce service-level encryption — even inside the VPC.

🔐 Option 2: Use Envoy Proxy for mTLS Between Services
For stricter security, run a sidecar proxy (Envoy) configured with:

mTLS certificates per service/environment

Traffic policies per identity

This would resemble a lightweight service mesh, and ECS Service Connect supports this pattern.

🔐 Option 3: Use AWS Private CA + ACM TLS for Internal Services
Use AWS ACM Private CA to issue internal TLS certs.

Attach ACM certs to services (with ALB or NLB in front).

Use internal load balancer DNS inside ECS.

✅ Final Notes
Concern	Solution
Incorrect versioning via namespace	✅ Use versioned service names in the same namespace
HTTPS within Service Connect	✅ Terminate TLS at service or use mTLS via Envoy
Enforce version-specific DNS	✅ service-b-v2.staging resolves to version 2 only
Prevent fallback to HTTP	✅ Enforce HTTPS-only calls via internal certs and listeners

🔄 [Updated Challenge 4]
Version-Aware Cross-Environment Call (Service B v2 in Staging)
Dev Service A must explicitly call v2 of Staging Service B — not default or latest.

✅ Step 1: Deploy Staging Service B v2 as a Separate ECS Service
Use a separate ECS service name (e.g. service-b-v2) in the staging namespace.

service_connect_configuration {
  enabled   = true
  namespace = "staging"

  services {
    port_name = "http"
    client_alias {
      dns_name = "service-b-v2"
      port     = 8080
    }
  }

This registers service-b-v2.staging as a unique DNS endpoint in ECS Service Connect.

✅ You now have separate service registration for each version.

In dev-service-a:

const response = await axios.get("http://service-b-v2.staging:8080/health");

// Calls unversioned or latest service (not approved)

Remove service-b registration

Block it via security group (if routed via ALB)

Add pipeline or code checks to validate DNS targets

📁 Week07/Security.md
Title: “Runtime, Registry & Network Hardening”

🔐 1. Container Signing: Trust But Verify
Cosign Signing (CI)

Every container built in CI is signed using GitHub/GitLab OIDC identity

Stored in OCI registry (ECR)

Verified at deploy via pipeline policy

Why It Matters:

Guarantees container provenance — no tampering between CI and runtime

Detects image swaps or untrusted sources

cosign sign --yes <image>
cosign verify <image>
📜 2. SBOMs: Audit-Ready from Day One
Trivy / Syft SBOM Generation

Captures exact software components (libs, deps, licenses)

Stored alongside artifacts (S3 or GitHub release)

Why It Matters:

Mandatory for regulated industries (FedRAMP, SOC2, DoD)

Enables instant CVE lookup if a zero-day hits a dependency

trivy image --format cyclonedx -o sbom.json <image>
🕵️ 3. Amazon Inspector Runtime Scanning
Enabled for ECS (Fargate)

Automatically scans running container layers

Reports CVEs even after build time

Best Practices:

Enable scanOnPush in ECR

Automate notifications to Slack, Jira, or SIEM via EventBridge

aws inspector2 enable --resource-types EC2 ECR
🔄 4. Drift Detection Between CI and Runtime
Compare:

ECS task image vs CI signed image hash

Why It Matters:

Detects unauthorized deploys or manual hotfixes

IMAGE=$(aws ecs describe-tasks --query 'tasks[].containers[].image')
cosign verify $IMAGE
🌐 5. Network Isolation (Service Connect Namespaces)
Per-Environment Namespaces

dev, qa, staging, uat all have isolated namespaces

Prevents cross-talk (e.g., dev-backend ↔ dev-backend)

Enforced via:

ECS Task Security Groups

Service Connect scoped DNS

🛡️ 6. HTTPS Enforcement Within ECS Service Connect
By default, ECS Service Connect uses HTTP over the VPC.
To enforce HTTPS (TLS) between services, use one of these options:

✅ Option 1: TLS Termination at the Service (Recommended)
Backend runs with TLS enabled (self-signed or ACM)

https.createServer({ key, cert }, app).listen(443);
Frontend or client services call:

await axios.get("https://service-b-v2.staging:443/secure", {
  httpsAgent: new https.Agent({ rejectUnauthorized: false }) // dev/test only
});
✅ Ideal for secure intra-VPC encryption and RBAC via certs.

🔐 Option 2: mTLS with Envoy Proxy Sidecar
Each ECS task runs Envoy or AWS App Mesh proxy

Certs issued via ACM Private CA

Enforced per-source/destination identity

Why It Matters:

Strongest security model: mTLS, policy enforcement, identity-bound traffic

🔒 Option 3: Internal ALBs with TLS & SNI Routing
Expose each ECS service via internal ALB with TLS listener

Attach ACM certificate

Use SNI or path-based routing to split versions

Useful if Service Connect DNS isn't flexible enough for your HTTPS model.

✅ Best Practice Summary:
Approach	TLS?	Auth?	Use When...
TLS inside app	✅	❌ (unless mTLS in app)	Simple HTTPS
TLS + ACM certs	✅	✅	Internal trust model
Envoy sidecar	✅✅	✅✅	Full mTLS, policy-driven
No HTTPS (default)	❌	❌	Not recommended
✅ Bonus: Version-Aware Service Routing Security
Register each service version separately in Service Connect (e.g., service-b-v2)

Limit each service to only talk to specific version endpoints

Use DNS patterns like service-b-v2.staging and reject fallback calls

axios.get("https://service-b-v2.staging:443")
✅ Prevents shadow deployments, version confusion, and data inconsistencies.

🔚 Summary
Area	Security Practice
Build pipeline	Cosign signature + SBOMs
Registry	Inspector scan-on-push
Runtime	Inspector + drift detection
Network	SG rules + Service Connect namespaces
Transport	HTTPS via in-app TLS or Envoy
Versioning	DNS-based routing + client enforcement

📁 Week08/Overview.md
Title: Per-Service Security Pipelines & Runtime Monitoring

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you will:

Build dedicated security pipelines per microservice

Apply fine-grained IAM roles per ECS task

Enable runtime anomaly detection using GuardDuty and CloudWatch

Distinguish security controls between Fargate and EC2 workloads

Enforce ownership boundaries (RBAC, audit, and alerting per service)

Integrate SBOM + signature lineage in your service metadata

Design per-service security policies: image trust, role least-privilege, runtime checks

🧠 Why This Week Matters
In a microservices DevSecOps architecture, each service is a unit of trust and responsibility.
This week helps you move from global security policies to per-service ownership.

You’ll answer questions like:

How do I restrict which service can assume which IAM role?

How can I alert only the owning team if Service A behaves abnormally?

Can I prevent Service B from pulling unscanned images?

How can I track what code, SBOM, and signature a specific service is running right now?

🧱 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🛠️ Per-Service Security Pipelines
Instead of one global CI/CD pipeline:

Each service gets its own hardened security CI/CD flow.

Each pipeline:

Scans and signs its own containers

Generates its own SBOMs

Pushes metadata to a central registry or database

Is tagged with its responsible team or service owner

/backend/.gitlab-ci.yml
/frontend/.github/workflows/frontend.yml
/notifications/.gitlab-ci.yml
2. 🔐 IAM Role Per Service (Fargate & EC2)
You will:

Create one IAM task role per ECS service

Limit permissions to only what's needed

Use IAM condition keys to allow only deploys from trusted pipelines

"Condition": {
  "StringEquals": {
    "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:ecs:...:service/backend"
  }
}
🔐 For EC2 tasks: bind IAM role to instance profile + container task override
🔐 For Fargate: use executionRole and taskRole explicitly

3. 🧠 Runtime Monitoring (Fargate + EC2 Differences)
Tool	ECS Fargate	ECS EC2
Amazon Inspector	✅ Built-in	✅ Requires agent
GuardDuty ECS Integration	✅	✅
Falco	❌ not directly supported	✅ Native via DaemonSet
CloudWatch Metrics	✅	✅
ECS Exec Logs	✅	✅ (with SSM agent)
📊 Use CloudWatch, GuardDuty, and Inspector for real-time visibility.
🧪 Add custom anomaly rules per container (e.g., memory spikes, outbound DNS, unusual ports).

4. 🔍 Track Provenance Per Service
Each service registers:

Image SHA

Signature identity (Cosign signer, CI origin)

SBOM link (e.g., S3 path or GitHub artifact)

Deployment tag (Git tag or semantic version)

Store this in:

DynamoDB (per service)

S3 (signed metadata manifest)

GitHub/GitLab releases (SBOMs + signatures)

5. 📛 Ownership Enforcement and Alerts
For each service:

Define owner (backend-team, frontend-team)

Route findings from Inspector or GuardDuty only to relevant Slack/Jira

Label builds and tasks with owner and service-name tags

🎯 No more "catch-all" security. Every service has its own alerts, responders, and policy.

6. 🔒 Service Hardening by Track (Fargate vs EC2)
Control	Fargate	EC2
Host Access	❌ No host access	✅ Needs host protection (e.g., SELinux, Auditd)
Runtime Agents	Limited	Full (Falco, Datadog, CrowdStrike)
IAM Role Binding	Per task	Per instance or per container
mTLS Sidecars	Supported	Native with App Mesh or Envoy
Egress Filtering	Via task SG	Via host-level IPTables or eBPF
Runtime Alerting	Inspector/CloudTrail	Inspector + custom Linux tooling
🧩 How This Evolves the App
Evolution	Result
Split pipelines	Each service has isolated build, scan, and deploy flow
Least-privilege IAM	Services only access the resources they truly need
SBOM lineage	You can answer: “What is service-A running in prod right now?”
Runtime alerting	You catch misbehaviors or anomalies fast — and alert the right team
Audit readiness	You have per-service provenance, audit, and CVE history on demand
✅ By End of Week 8, You Will Be Able To:
Design and implement security-as-code per microservice

Harden ECS services by runtime type (Fargate vs EC2)

Alert the correct team on runtime anomalies (service-aware monitoring)

Maintain full provenance of images, SBOMs, and deploy history

Treat services like independent security tenants — not just containers

🔜 Next: GuidedLab.md — Set up real per-service CI/CD pipelines with IAM isolation, SBOM lineage, and runtime anomaly alerting.

📁 Week08/GuidedLab.md
Title: Per-Service Pipelines, IAM Isolation & Runtime Monitoring

🧪 Lab Scenario
You’re expanding your secure note/task manager into microservices:

auth-service

notes-service

notification-service

This week, you’ll:

Create dedicated GitLab/GitHub CI/CD pipelines per service

Generate and track SBOMs + signed containers per service

Assign IAM roles per ECS task

Enable runtime security (Inspector, GuardDuty)

Store and trace lineage metadata (SBOM link, Git SHA, signer identity)

🧱 Step 1: Create Per-Service Pipelines
Split your CI/CD:

/services/
  ├── auth-service/
  │    └── .gitlab-ci.yml
  ├── notes-service/
  │    └── .github/workflows/deploy.yml
  └── notification-service/
       └── .gitlab-ci.yml
Each pipeline should include build, SBOM, sign, and deploy steps.

📋 GitLab Example: auth-service/.gitlab-ci.yml
stages:
  - build
  - sign
  - deploy

build:
  stage: build
  script:
    - docker build -t $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHA .
    - docker push $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHA

sbom:
  stage: sign
  script:
    - trivy image --format cyclonedx -o sbom.json $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHA
    - cosign sign --yes $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHA

deploy:
  stage: deploy
  script:
    - ecs deploy via CDK/Terraform or shell
✅ Include:
SBOM output saved in S3 or Git artifact

cosign signature with pipeline identity

Git tag or commit hash for traceability

🔐 Step 2: Assign Per-Service IAM Roles (ECS Fargate & EC2)
Use separate IAM Task Roles per service:

resource "aws_iam_role" "auth_service_task_role" {
  name = "auth-service-task-role"

  assume_role_policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17",
    Statement = [{
      Effect = "Allow",
      Principal = {
        Service = "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com"
      },
      Action = "sts:AssumeRole"
    }]
  })
}

resource "aws_iam_role_policy" "auth_secrets_access" {
  name = "auth-access"
  role = aws_iam_role.auth_service_task_role.id

  policy = jsonencode({
    Statement: [{
      Effect: "Allow",
      Action: ["secretsmanager:GetSecretValue"],
      Resource: ["arn:aws:secretsmanager:...:secret:auth-*"]
    }]
  })
}
✅ ECS Service Definition
"taskRoleArn": "arn:aws:iam::...:role/auth-service-task-role"
🔐 Principle: no shared roles between services. Least privilege per task.

🧠 Step 3: Enable Runtime Monitoring (Inspector + GuardDuty)
✅ Amazon Inspector (ECR + ECS Fargate)
aws inspector2 enable --resource-types ECR ECS
✅ Inspector will:

Scan images at push

Monitor tasks for runtime CVEs

Send findings to Security Hub / EventBridge

✅ GuardDuty Runtime Anomaly Detection
Enable ECS integration:

aws guardduty update-organization-configuration \
  --detector-id <detector_id> \
  --data-sources '{"Kubernetes": {"AuditLogs": {"Enable": true}}, "ECSRuntimeMonitoring": {"Enable": true}}'
Use GuardDuty findings to detect:

DNS exfiltration

Unexpected port listening

Credential harvesting

Route alerts to Slack via EventBridge + Lambda handler or SNS

🧬 Step 4: Register SBOM + Signature Metadata
For each deployed version, register:

Key	Example Value
service_name	auth-service
image_digest	sha256:4a92...
cosign_subject	gitlab-ci@project
sbom_url	s3://sboms/auth-service/sbom-abc123.json
git_sha	abc123def456
deployed_at	2025-07-24T16:40:00Z
You can:

Store in DynamoDB per service

Log to OpenSearch or ELK

Push to Security Hub

Optional: Output metadata from CI pipeline
echo "{ \"service\": \"auth\", \"image\": \"$IMAGE\", \"sbom\": \"$SBOM_URL\" }" > lineage.json
aws s3 cp lineage.json s3://service-metadata/auth-service/
🔁 Step 5: Git Tag + Version Traceability
Every successful pipeline:

Tags the service repo with vX.Y.Z or commit SHA

Includes SBOM + signature in the release

- name: Tag on successful deploy
  run: |
    git tag v1.3.2
    git push origin v1.3.2
✅ Success Criteria
Component	What You Should See
Per-service pipelines	Individual build → scan → sign → deploy flows
Isolated IAM roles	No shared permissions between services
Runtime monitoring	GuardDuty & Inspector detecting behavior
Metadata lineage	SBOMs + image digests traceable per deploy
Audit trail	Git tags, signatures, and SBOMs archived
🧩 Real-World Value
By the end of this lab, you’ll be able to:

Prove what's running in production and where it came from

Create pipeline gates per service (block if not signed)

Investigate runtime alerts down to the commit + SBOM

Route alerts to the correct service owner/team

Build zero-trust DevSecOps pipelines per microservice

Title: Secure Autonomy: Locking Down the Microservices Supply Chain

🧪 Scenario
You’ve successfully split your CI/CD into per-service pipelines. Now leadership is asking you to:

Prove every deployed service is signed, scanned, and traceable

Ensure services cannot assume each other’s IAM roles

Route runtime alerts to only the team responsible for that service

Monitor both ECS Fargate and EC2 microservices for anomalies

You must complete the following challenges in under an hour per team.

🔐 Challenge 1: Enforce Signature Verification Before Deploy
Update your deployment pipeline to:

✅ Fail deploy if the image isn't signed by your org's identity
✅ Log signed image digest to S3 with a Git tag for traceability

cosign verify --key cosign.pub <image> || exit 1
Bonus: Deny ECS deploys via terraform apply unless signature is verified.

🔄 Challenge 2: Add Image Provenance Metadata per Service
Push a metadata JSON file per service deploy:

{
  "service": "auth-service",
  "image_digest": "sha256:...",
  "sbom_link": "s3://sboms/auth/sbom-abc123.json",
  "signed_by": "ci-pipeline@org",
  "git_tag": "v1.3.2"
}
✅ Store it in:

DynamoDB (per service)

or S3 (per tag)

or ElasticSearch (searchable lineage)

🧰 Challenge 3: Create Runtime Alerts per Service
Enable the following for each service (Fargate or EC2):

✅ GuardDuty ECS Runtime Monitoring

✅ Amazon Inspector (linked to ECR and ECS)

✅ Alert routing: Service A → Team A’s Slack, Service B → Team B’s Jira

Use EventBridge filters based on resource.service.name.

Bonus: Add container-level anomaly rules with CloudWatch (e.g., CPU spike, unexpected outbound DNS).

🔐 Challenge 4: Harden IAM Role Per Service
Create a least-privilege IAM role for each microservice:

Service	IAM Role Actions
auth-service	SecretsManager:GetSecretValue (auth only)
notes-service	DynamoDB:GetItem/PutItem
notifications	SES:SendEmail
✅ Ensure:

No service can assume another's role

Roles are only used within ECS task definitions

Terraform/CloudFormation defines roles, not humans manually

🔒 Challenge 5: Prevent Runtime Drift
Enable automatic image scan on push to ECR, and compare digests at deploy:

EXPECTED=$(cosign verify --key cosign.pub <image> | jq '.digest')
ACTUAL=$(aws ecs describe-tasks ... | jq '.containers[].image')

if [[ "$EXPECTED" != "$ACTUAL" ]]; then
  echo "Image drift detected!" && exit 1
fi
✅ Prevent ECS from running unsigned or unverified containers.

🧩 Challenge 6: Multi-Track Monitoring (Fargate vs EC2)
Task	Fargate	EC2
Inspector Runtime	✅ Enabled	✅ Enabled (with agent)
Falco	❌ Not supported	✅ Installed as daemonset
GuardDuty alerts	✅	✅
IAM Isolation	Per-task	Per-container (via EC2 IAM policies or IRSA)
✅ Document differences and tradeoffs in your team runbook.

✅ Success Criteria
Task	Validation
Image signing enforced	Cosign fails pipeline if not verified
SBOM uploaded	Stored and linked per version
IAM isolation	Each service uses distinct role
Runtime alerting	GuardDuty + Inspector enabled per task
Drift detection	Deployed image matches verified hash
Slack/Jira routing	Findings alert only owning team

📁 Week08/Security.md
Title: Locking Down the Service Plane: Role Isolation, Runtime Defense, and Provenance

🔐 1. IAM Role Isolation per Microservice
Each ECS service must operate with its own least-privilege IAM role:

auth-service: Only access SecretsManager for auth-specific secrets

notes-service: Only access DynamoDB for note operations

notification-service: Only access SES or SNS to send messages

"Effect": "Deny",
"Action": "*",
"Resource": "*",
"Condition": {
  "StringNotEquals": {
    "aws:ResourceTag/Service": "notes-service"
  }
}
✅ Enforced in infrastructure-as-code (CDK/Terraform)
✅ Tagged roles prevent assumption by unauthorized services
✅ Avoids blast radius when credentials are leaked or misused

🧱 2. Secure Metadata Provenance per Service
For each deploy, you store:

✅ image_digest: cryptographic image hash

✅ cosign_subject: identity of the signing CI/CD pipeline

✅ sbom_url: path to CycloneDX or SPDX document

✅ git_sha: version of source that produced this image

Why it matters:

You can reconstruct any deploy for audit, rollback, or breach analysis

Meets FedRAMP, ISO, and SBOM mandates

🧠 3. Runtime Threat Monitoring: GuardDuty + Inspector
🔍 GuardDuty (ECS Runtime)
Enables runtime threat detection at the container level:

Unexpected port binding

Credential stealing behavior

Unusual DNS (exfiltration, C2 domains)

Enforce per service:

Alerts tagged by ECS service-name

Route only to owning team’s alert channel (Jira, Slack, PagerDuty)

🛡️ Amazon Inspector
CVE scanning of images at rest (ECR)

CVE scanning of containers in runtime (Fargate and EC2)

Automatically integrated with ECS tasks

Policy:

No deploy if critical CVEs are present

Logs forwarded to Security Hub or ELK

❌ 4. Drift Prevention: Runtime ≠ CI/CD
You enforce post-deploy drift detection by comparing:

Source	Check
GitLab/GitHub CI pipeline	Cosign signature + SBOM
ECS Runtime Task	Image SHA
Deployed Metadata	S3 / DB record
If the ECS container doesn’t match the last signed image → alert + rollback.

🧬 5. SBOMs as Living Documentation
Each service SBOM is versioned and stored with metadata

Used in CI to detect:

Unwanted libraries (log4j, openssl bugs)

License violations (GPL, LGPL in commercial services)

Trivy/Grype flags CVEs with links, so you can automate approvals or block deploys.

🔒 6. Advanced Threat Mitigation
Threat	Countermeasure
Lateral movement between services	Service Connect namespace isolation + IAM role binding
Privilege escalation	Separate taskRole vs executionRole + SCPs
Image tampering	Cosign signature verification
CVE exploitation	Amazon Inspector + runtime scan blocks
Unauthorized changes	Git tag signing + deploy metadata log
Alert fatigue	GuardDuty alerts scoped per service
🔄 7. ECS-Specific Hardening (Fargate vs EC2)
Concern	Fargate	EC2
Host access	❌	✅ (needs OS hardening)
mTLS sidecar	✅ with Service Connect	✅ with App Mesh / Envoy
Runtime agents	Limited (Inspector only)	Full (Falco, eBPF)
File system auditing	Not supported	✅ (Auditd)
egress filtering	SG only	IPTables + VPC routing
✅ Key Practices Enforced This Week
Control Type	Technique
Identity Trust	Cosign container signing + signer enforcement
Access Control	IAM per-service with deny-by-default
Runtime Defense	Inspector + GuardDuty ECS integration
Auditability	SBOM versioning + image traceability
Ownership	Alert routing based on ECS service name or tags

Title: “Security as a Microservice: Trust Is Now a Team Sport”

🧠 What You Learned

into

Area	Capability

Your secure notes app now behaves like a real-world microservice platform:

Feature	Now Secured By
Auth Service	Least-privilege IAM + runtime signature enforcement
Notes Service	SBOM generation + GuardDuty monitoring
Notification Service	Role-bound SES access + runtime anomaly detection
Deployment History	Git-tagged metadata with SBOM + image hash

🔐 DevSecOps Concepts That Clicked
Concept	Realization
Security isn’t centralized	Each team/service must own and enforce its own security responsibilities
Least privilege isn’t optional	IAM per service enforces true zero trust
CI pipelines are security boundaries	Only trusted pipelines can sign, push, and deploy
Runtime ≠ Build	Inspector and GuardDuty give you live protection and drift detection
SBOMs are living contracts	They’re not just documents — they’re enforcement tools and audit assets
🚀 Real-World Value You Now Offer

Refactor a monolithic pipeline into isolated, signed per-service flows

Apply fine-grained IAM roles to ECS services or Kubernetes workloads

Track runtime provenance and detect container drift

Route runtime threat alerts to the correct on-call rotation per team

Build a signed, traceable container supply chain from CI to ECS task

You now think and act like a secure platform engineer, not just a DevOps implementer.

💡 Key Takeaways
Principle	Practice
Every service is its own security perimeter	IAM + CI pipeline + SBOM + runtime scope
Ownership must be enforced, not implied	You tag, sign, and alert by service
Runtime matters more than release	Inspector and GuardDuty protect you post-deploy
Provenance is power	SBOM + signed image = proof of what's really running
Trust is intentional	Every deploy, every call, every alert is now attributable and validated

📁 Week09/Overview.md
Title: Advanced Policy-as-Code + Automated Risk Gates

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you will:

Write and enforce custom security policies using Rego/OPA

Integrate Conftest with GitLab and GitHub CI/CD

Block deployments with high CVEs, disallowed licenses, or missing metadata

Tailor policies by environment (e.g., dev vs prod rules)

Produce and verify attestations tied to policy outcomes

Apply SBOM-aware gates across multiple services

🧠 Why This Week Matters
Scanning a container for CVEs is helpful.
Blocking its deployment unless it passes a security policy is DevSecOps.

Security tools give you findings.
Policy-as-Code gives you control.

This week, you move from "monitor and notify" to "validate and enforce."
Your services will now carry proof of policy compliance into runtime.

📚 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🔍 What is Policy-as-Code?
Policy-as-Code is the practice of:

Expressing security rules in machine-readable formats

Validating infrastructure, code, and metadata before deployment

Embedding those checks into the CI/CD pipeline

Frameworks:

✅ OPA/Rego for flexible logic

✅ Conftest to run Rego against YAML/JSON

✅ Kyverno or Gatekeeper for runtime Kubernetes validation

2. 🛠️ OPA + Conftest: The Core Tools
You’ll use OPA Rego policies + Conftest to enforce security checks in CI.

Example Rego:

package sbom

deny[msg] {
  input.licenses[_] == "GPL-3.0"
  msg = "GPL-3.0 licensed code is not allowed"
}
Run it with:

conftest test sbom.json --policy ./policies
3. 🚦 CI/CD Risk Gates (GitHub/GitLab)
Integrate policy checks into your CI:

- name: Run SBOM policy checks
  run: |
    conftest test sbom.json --policy policies
CI gates should:

✅ Fail on high-severity CVEs

✅ Block disallowed licenses (GPL, LGPL)

✅ Require signed images and SBOMs

✅ Enforce mandatory metadata: owner, service, env

4. 🧬 SBOM-Aware Enforcement
With SBOMs now generated per-service (from Week 7–8), you’ll:

Parse SBOM files with Conftest

Define policies like:

"Disallow vulnerable OpenSSL versions"

"Ensure all services use MIT/BSD-licensed packages"

"Reject deploys missing SPDX compliance headers"

SBOM becomes a gate, not just a report.

5. 📜 Attestations and Provenance
You’ll use tools like:

Cosign attestations

in-toto metadata

SLSA framework

To generate and verify:

Who built the image

Which policy passed

What was enforced in CI/CD

When and how it was deployed

Attestations become immutable receipts of security compliance.

6. 🔄 Environment-Specific Policies
Different policies per environment:

Rule	Dev	QA	Prod
Allow high CVEs	✅	❌	❌
Allow GPL packages	✅ (test)	❌	❌
Require SBOM + signature	Optional	✅	✅
Require deployment tag & version	Optional	✅	✅
Implemented via:

Branch-based conditions

GitLab/GitHub environment variables

Rego input.env context

🧩 How This Evolves the App
Evolution	Result
Policies as pipelines	Every service must now pass automated risk gates
SBOM is enforced	No more “I forgot the SBOM” — it's required
CI/CD becomes zero-trust	Only signed, proven builds get deployed
Audit becomes trivial	Attestations prove what was run and why
Teams become accountable	Owners must fix or justify policy failures before merge or deploy
✅ By End of Week 9, You Will Be Able To:
Write custom Rego policies to protect your services

Scan and enforce SBOM + license + CVE policies

Fail builds that don’t meet compliance criteria

Generate and verify signed attestations tied to deploys

Apply different rules per environment (dev vs prod)

🔜 Next: GuidedLab.md — you’ll build real enforcement pipelines with Rego + Conftest that block policy violations in CI, validate SBOM content, and generate signed attestations per service.

📁 Week09/GuidedLab.md
Title: Enforce the Rules: SBOM Policies, CI/CD Risk Gates & Attestations

🧪 Lab Scenario
You’re now required to enforce the following before any service can be deployed:

Block high CVEs in the SBOM

Disallow GPL-3.0 and LGPL-3.0 licenses

Require signed images with Cosign

Enforce service metadata (owner, service_name, env)

Record signed attestations of every pass/fail decision

You’ll apply this across your auth-service, notes-service, and notification-service CI pipelines.

📦 Prerequisites
SBOM generation already integrated via Trivy

Images signed using Cosign

GitHub/GitLab runners enabled

conftest, cosign, and jq installed in your CI runners

🔧 Step 1: Write Rego Policies
🗂️ Create policy directory: policies/sbom.rego
package sbom

deny[msg] {
  input.vulnerabilities[_].severity == "HIGH"
  msg = "High severity CVEs are not allowed"
}

deny[msg] {
  input.licenses[_] == "GPL-3.0"
  msg = "GPL-3.0 licenses are disallowed"
}

deny[msg] {
  not input.metadata.owner
  msg = "Missing 'owner' in metadata"
}
You can extend with rules like:

deny[msg] {
  input.metadata.environment == "prod"
  not input.metadata.signed
  msg = "Production services must be signed"
}
🧪 Step 2: Sample SBOM Input File
Trivy SBOM (CycloneDX format) → convert to JSON:

trivy image --format cyclonedx -o sbom.json your-image:tag
Append metadata (or merge it in CI):

{
  "vulnerabilities": [{ "severity": "HIGH" }],
  "licenses": ["MIT", "GPL-3.0"],
  "metadata": {
    "owner": "auth-team",
    "service_name": "auth-service",
    "environment": "prod",
    "signed": true
  }
}
🚦 Step 3: Conftest in CI/CD
🧪 GitHub Actions
- name: Enforce SBOM Policies
  run: |
    conftest test sbom.json --policy ./policies
🧪 GitLab CI
sbom-policy:
  stage: validate
  script:
    - conftest test sbom.json --policy ./policies
❗ CI should fail the job if any deny rule triggers

🔐 Step 4: Cosign Signature Verification
Before deploy, verify signature:

cosign verify --key cosign.pub ghcr.io/org/auth-service@sha256:abcd... || exit 1
In CI:

- name: Verify Image Signature
  run: |
    cosign verify --key cosign.pub $IMAGE_REF
Only signed images pass.

📜 Step 5: Create and Attach Attestation
Cosign supports attestations with your own predicates:

cosign attest --predicate sbom.json --type cyclonedx --key cosign.key $IMAGE_REF
This creates an in-toto statement bound to your signed image:

Who signed it

When

What policy passed

SBOM content

You can later verify it:

cosign verify-attestation --key cosign.pub --type cyclonedx $IMAGE_REF
🔁 Step 6: Environment-Specific Policy Rules
Modify sbom.rego:

deny[msg] {
  input.metadata.environment == "prod"
  input.vulnerabilities[_].severity == "MEDIUM"
  msg = "No medium CVEs allowed in production"
}
Use CI environment variables:

GitHub:

echo "{ \"environment\": \"prod\" }" > meta.json
GitLab:

script:
  - echo "{\"environment\": \"$CI_ENVIRONMENT_NAME\"}" > meta.json

Merge this into `sbom.json` dynamically if needed.

---

## ✅ Success Criteria

| Item | Validation |
|------|------------|
| SBOM policy | Denies GPL-3.0 or high CVEs |
| Signature | Cosign blocks unsigned deploys |
| Metadata | `owner`, `env`, `service_name` required |
| CI/CD gate | Build fails if policy fails |
| Attestation | Attached to image with SBOM |
| Environment-sensitive | Stricter for `prod` vs `dev` |

---

## 🧩 Real-World Outcomes

| Practice | Value |
|---------|-------|
| Policy in code | Auditable, reviewable security gates |
| Attestations | Immutable proof of compliance |
| Fail-fast in CI | No bad containers reach production |
| SBOM-aware | Attack surface minimized |
| Zero-trust builds | No deploy unless signed + verified |
| Team accountability | Failing policies trigger secure feedback loops |

📁 Week08/Overview.md
Title: 🔐 Secrets Management with Vault, SOPS, and Sealed Secrets

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you will:

Understand the risks of hardcoded, unencrypted secrets in pipelines and deployments

Learn how to manage secrets securely at rest, in transit, and in use

Use HashiCorp Vault for centralized, dynamic secret management

Use Mozilla SOPS for GitOps-friendly encryption

Use Bitnami Sealed Secrets for Kubernetes-native encrypted secrets

Inject secrets securely into CI/CD, ECS, and Kubernetes workloads

🧠 Why This Week Matters
Most security breaches start with one thing: exposed credentials.

In DevSecOps, secrets are a top attack vector if mishandled:

Leaked API keys in Git history

Over-permissive long-lived tokens in CI

Unencrypted secrets in Helm charts or Terraform

Static database passwords in environment files

This week, we go beyond best practices. You’ll engineer secrets into the system: discoverable, revocable, auditable, and encrypted — across CI/CD, IaC, ECS, and Kubernetes.

📚 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🔐 What Is a Secret?
Any sensitive value that must be kept confidential:

Type	Examples
Static Secrets	API keys, DB passwords, JWT signing secrets
Dynamic Secrets	Short-lived AWS STS tokens, signed certificates
Encrypted Secrets	Sealed Secrets, SOPS-encrypted YAML
Environment Secrets	Variables injected into containers or runners
In DevSecOps, we care about secret lifecycle, not just encryption:

Who created it?

Where is it stored?

Who can access it?

How long does it live?

Can we revoke or rotate it?

2. 🧰 HashiCorp Vault: Centralized Secrets Engine
Vault solves:

✅ Secret storage (static)

✅ Dynamic secret generation (e.g., DB creds, AWS STS)

✅ Access control (policies, tokens, JWT-based auth)

✅ Auditing and rotation

Features:

Key-Value Secrets Engine

Dynamic Secrets (e.g., per-pod DB creds)

AppRole, GitHub, OIDC Auth Methods

Audit Logging and Policy-Based Access Control

Use Cases:

Inject secrets at runtime (e.g., ECS task or Kubernetes pod)

Rotate credentials automatically (e.g., PostgreSQL passwords)

Enable GitHub Actions or GitLab to fetch secrets securely

3. 🔐 Mozilla SOPS: Secrets-as-Code with GitOps
SOPS (Secrets OPerationS) allows encrypting secrets inline in your YAML/JSON files, so you can safely store them in Git.

Key features:

Encrypt only the values — not the whole file

Use KMS, PGP, or GCP/AWS keys

Compatible with Terraform, Kubernetes manifests, etc.

Works well with GitOps workflows (e.g., ArgoCD, Flux)

Example:

apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: my-secret
data:
  password: ENC[AES256_GCM,data:encrypted==]
Use sops -e and sops -d to encrypt/decrypt.
This keeps secrets version-controlled without exposing plaintext.

4. 🛡️ Bitnami Sealed Secrets
Sealed Secrets are a Kubernetes-native way to:

Encrypt Secret manifests using the cluster's public key

Allow storing encrypted secrets in Git

Automatically decrypt them on deploy (via Sealed Secrets controller)

Benefits:

✅ Works with CI/CD and GitOps

✅ Supports RBAC enforcement

✅ Protects secrets even if repo is public

You’ll learn:

How to generate a sealed secret

How the controller decrypts them securely

How to rotate or re-seal secrets for different clusters

5. 🔁 Injecting Secrets into Pipelines, ECS, and K8s
You’ll see how to securely inject secrets into:

System	How
GitHub Actions	secrets: or Vault via JWT/OIDC
GitLab CI	secret variables, Vault integration
ECS (Fargate/EC2)	SSM Parameter Store, Secrets Manager, or Vault agent sidecar
Kubernetes	Kubernetes Secrets, SOPS, or Sealed Secrets
You’ll also learn how to rotate, revoke, and audit access to secrets — even across environments (dev, qa, prod).

🧩 How This Evolves the App
Your secure note/task manager app will now:

Upgrade	Outcome
Vault integrated	Dynamic DB credentials per service
SOPS-encrypted manifests	Git-safe app configs
Sealed Secrets in K8s	Safe GitOps deploys with secrets
CI secrets injection	No secrets in repo or runner logs
✅ By End of Week 8, You’ll Be Able To:
Use Vault to manage dynamic secrets per service/environment

Encrypt and store secrets in Git using SOPS

Use Sealed Secrets for Kubernetes-native secret injection

Inject secrets securely into ECS tasks and CI/CD jobs

Apply RBAC, TTLs, and policies to all secrets

Audit and rotate secrets without redeploying everything
