📁 Week08/GuidedLab.md
Title: Centralized Secrets Without Compromise: Vault, SOPS & Sealed Secrets

🧪 Scenario
You’ve been asked to modernize secret handling for your evolving note/task management app. The app runs across ECS and Kubernetes. Secrets currently live in unencrypted .env files and plaintext Helm charts.

You’ll now:

Set up Vault for centralized secrets

Encrypt GitOps-compatible secrets using SOPS

Deploy Kubernetes secrets using Sealed Secrets

Inject secrets securely into GitLab/GitHub CI

Enforce access control across environments (dev, staging, prod)

🧰 Prerequisites
Docker or K8s dev environment (minikube or ECS dev cluster)

GitLab and/or GitHub repo access

Tools installed: vault, sops, kubeseal, jq, helm, kubectl

🔧 Part 1: Setup & Use Vault
🔹 Step 1.1: Start a Dev Vault Server
In production, use HA mode + integrated storage. Here we’ll use dev mode.

vault server -dev
You'll see:

Root Token: s.xxxxxxxx
Export the token:

export VAULT_ADDR='http://127.0.0.1:8200'
export VAULT_TOKEN='s.xxxxxxxx'
🔹 Step 1.2: Enable KV Secrets Engine (Version 2)
vault secrets enable -path=secrets kv-v2
🔹 Step 1.3: Add a Secret
vault kv put secrets/dev/db password='devpass123' username='devuser'
🔹 Step 1.4: Read Secret
vault kv get secrets/dev/db
🔹 Step 1.5: Configure App Access (GitHub Auth Example)
vault auth enable github

vault write auth/github/config organization="YourOrg"

vault write auth/github/map/teams/dev team="dev" value="default,dev"

vault policy write dev - <<EOF
path "secrets/data/dev/*" {
  capabilities = ["read"]
}
EOF
Use GitHub token to authenticate:

vault login -method=github token=YOUR_GITHUB_TOKEN
✅ This limits access to specific secret paths per team/environment.

🔐 Part 2: Use SOPS for Git-Safe Secrets
🔹 Step 2.1: Generate a GPG Key (or use AWS KMS / GCP KMS)
gpg --full-generate-key
gpg --list-keys
Copy your key ID (e.g. A1B2C3D4).

🔹 Step 2.2: Create a Secret File
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: db-secret
type: Opaque
data:
  password: myplaintextpassword
🔹 Step 2.3: Encrypt with SOPS
sops --encrypt --pgp A1B2C3D4 secret.yaml > secret.enc.yaml
Check the output — values are encrypted inline.

🔹 Step 2.4: Decrypt
sops --decrypt secret.enc.yaml
✅ Now you can commit the encrypted file to Git — safe for GitOps workflows.

🛡️ Part 3: Sealed Secrets in Kubernetes
🔹 Step 3.1: Install Sealed Secrets Controller
kubectl apply -f https://github.com/bitnami-labs/sealed-secrets/releases/download/v0.23.0/controller.yaml
🔹 Step 3.2: Get Public Key
kubeseal --fetch-cert \
  --controller-namespace kube-system \
  --controller-name sealed-secrets-controller \
  > pub-cert.pem
🔹 Step 3.3: Create & Seal a Secret
kubectl create secret generic my-secret --from-literal=password='SuperSecret' --dry-run=client -o json > secret.json

kubeseal --cert pub-cert.pem -o yaml < secret.json > sealed-secret.yaml
✅ Now sealed-secret.yaml can be safely committed to Git. Only the cluster with the private key can decrypt it.

🔹 Step 3.4: Apply Sealed Secret
kubectl apply -f sealed-secret.yaml
Verify the decrypted Secret was created.

🤖 Part 4: Inject Secrets in CI/CD
🔹 GitHub Actions
jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    env:
      DB_PASSWORD: ${{ secrets.DB_PASSWORD }}
Or use OIDC to retrieve from Vault dynamically:

vault write auth/jwt/login role=github \
     jwt=${GITHUB_TOKEN}
🔹 GitLab CI
In .gitlab-ci.yml:

variables:
  VAULT_ADDR: https://vault.mycorp.com
  VAULT_TOKEN: ${VAULT_CI_TOKEN}

script:
  - vault kv get -field=password secrets/dev/db
✅ Success Criteria
Task	Validation
Vault set up	Secrets stored and scoped to envs
SOPS working	Secret YAML encrypted inline
Sealed Secrets	Secrets sealed, committed, and deployed safely
CI/CD injection	Secrets retrieved dynamically or from env
Access controls	Limited by environment, service, or auth
Ready to move on to ChallengeLab.md, where you’ll build your own full secrets handling flow across Vault, GitOps, and deployment pipelines?

📁 Week08/ChallengeLab.md
Title: No Secrets Left Behind: Encrypt, Inject & Rotate Across Environments

🧪 Scenario
You’ve onboarded 4 services in your note/task manager platform:

auth-service

notes-service

notification-service

analytics-service

You now need to:

Build a multi-environment secret strategy

Use Vault for dynamic database secrets

Encrypt GitOps secrets using SOPS

Deliver Sealed Secrets to Kubernetes securely

Inject secrets into CI/CD pipelines

Enforce RBAC and TTL policies

🛠️ Challenge 1: Vault for Dynamic Secrets
Create a dynamic secrets engine in Vault for PostgreSQL:

vault secrets enable database

vault write database/config/my-postgres-db \
  plugin_name=postgresql-database-plugin \
  allowed_roles="readonly" \
  connection_url="postgresql://{{username}}:{{password}}@db.internal:5432/postgres?sslmode=disable" \
  username="admin" \
  password="adminpass"
Define a dynamic role:

vault write database/roles/readonly \
  db_name=my-postgres-db \
  creation_statements="CREATE ROLE \"{{name}}\" WITH LOGIN PASSWORD '{{password}}' VALID UNTIL '{{expiration}}'; GRANT SELECT ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO \"{{name}}\";" \
  default_ttl="1h" \
  max_ttl="24h"
Test the dynamic secret:

vault read database/creds/readonly
✅ Output should be a short-lived credential.

🛠️ Challenge 2: SOPS-Encrypted GitOps Secrets
Create a YAML manifest with environment-specific values:

apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: analytics-api-keys
type: Opaque
data:
  stripe_key: sk_test_12345
Encrypt it with SOPS using AWS KMS or GPG:

sops --encrypt --kms arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:123456789012:key/abcd... secret.yaml > encrypted.yaml
Commit encrypted.yaml to your GitOps repo.

Decrypt in CI to render the full manifest:

sops -d encrypted.yaml > manifest.yaml
✅ Manifest ready for Kustomize/Helm or direct kubectl apply.

🛠️ Challenge 3: Kubernetes Sealed Secrets
Create a Kubernetes secret for auth-service JWT signing key:

kubectl create secret generic auth-jwt \
  --from-literal=jwt_key='super-secure-secret' \
  --dry-run=client -o json > secret.json
Seal it:

kubeseal --cert pub-cert.pem -o yaml < secret.json > sealed-auth-jwt.yaml
Apply to cluster:

kubectl apply -f sealed-auth-jwt.yaml
✅ Should automatically decrypt to usable secret within cluster.

🛠️ Challenge 4: Secrets in CI/CD
🔹 GitHub Actions
Retrieve a secret from Vault using JWT OIDC:

vault write auth/jwt/login role=github jwt=${ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN}
Fetch secret for deploy:

vault kv get -field=stripe_key secrets/prod/analytics
🔹 GitLab CI
Use Vault environment variables or secret variables.

Store VAULT_TOKEN as masked variable.

Pull dynamic DB credentials for notes-service.

🛠️ Challenge 5: Access Policies per Environment
Create Vault policies for dev, qa, prod:

# dev-policy.hcl
path "secrets/data/dev/*" {
  capabilities = ["read"]
}
vault policy write dev dev-policy.hcl
Assign access based on GitHub team, CI OIDC claim, or service name.

✅ Success Criteria
Task	Validation
Dynamic DB credentials	vault read returns valid temporary creds
Encrypted GitOps secret	SOPS-encrypted YAML decrypts and applies cleanly
Sealed Kubernetes secret	Encrypted secret deploys and is usable
Vault CI/CD integration	Secrets retrieved in pipeline without storing plaintext
Scoped access	Dev team cannot access prod secrets
🧠 Bonus (Optional)
Rotate the Vault DB root credentials using a rotation command

Trigger Slack or email on secret read from Vault

Store audit logs from Vault to CloudWatch or ELK

Configure Vault agent injector into K8s sidecars

Let me know when you're ready to move on to the Security.md — we’ll cover the threats this model protects against (like secret sprawl, Git leaks, insider threats, and over-permissioned access).

📁 Week08/Security.md
Title: The Secrets We Keep: Threats Mitigated by Vault, SOPS, and Sealed Secrets

🎯 Objective
This week’s security goal is to prevent:

Hardcoded secrets in code or CI/CD

Git-leaked credentials

Long-lived or overly permissive tokens

Cross-environment secret access

Unauthorized reads or silent misuse

🔐 Key Threat Vectors and How We Mitigate Them
1. 🛑 Hardcoded Secrets in Code
Threat:

Secrets like DB passwords, API keys, or JWT signing keys are often hardcoded into:

config.js, .env, .tfvars, values.yaml

Git history (even if later removed)

Risk:

Compromise through Git leaks, insider access, or log exposure.

Mitigation:
✅ Use Vault to generate and inject secrets dynamically at runtime.
✅ Use SOPS to encrypt secrets before storing them in Git.
✅ Use Sealed Secrets to store encrypted Kubernetes secrets.

2. 📤 Secrets Leaked via Git or GitOps
Threat:

Secrets committed to GitOps manifests, Helm charts, or kustomize overlays.

Risk:

Public repos or poorly configured GitHub Actions runners may expose secrets.

Mitigation:
✅ SOPS encrypts only secret values (not entire manifest) — keeps Git readable + safe.
✅ Sealed Secrets ensure encrypted secrets are useless without cluster’s private key.
✅ CI/CD decrypts secrets only at runtime, never in the repo.

3. ⏳ Long-Lived Credentials
Threat:

Tokens or passwords that live indefinitely in .env, CI/CD, or cloud IAM.

Risk:

Breaches persist longer, credential reuse in multiple places, difficult revocation.

Mitigation:
✅ Vault issues short-lived dynamic secrets (e.g., 1h PostgreSQL login).
✅ You can revoke or rotate without restarting apps or pipelines.
✅ Use policies to auto-expire secrets based on role/environment.

4. 👤 Cross-Environment Secret Exposure
Threat:

Dev environment secrets accidentally access prod data (e.g., shared keys).

Risk:

Testing code in dev leaks or modifies production systems.

Mitigation:
✅ Vault secrets are namespaced by environment (secrets/data/dev/*)
✅ Policies ensure team members and services can only access what they need
✅ Sealed Secrets and SOPS can encrypt separately per environment

5. 📓 Lack of Auditability
Threat:

No logs on who accessed a secret, when, or why.

Risk:

No way to detect misuse, leaks, or insider access.

Mitigation:
✅ Vault logs every auth and secret read/write event
✅ Integration with CloudWatch, ELK, or SIEM for real-time detection
✅ Can alert on secret reads from CI/CD unexpectedly

6. 🔓 Exposed Secrets in CI/CD Logs or Artifacts
Threat:

Secrets printed in CI/CD output logs or stored in artifact bundles

Mitigation:
✅ Mask secrets using pipeline variables ($VAULT_TOKEN, $JWT_SECRET)
✅ Fetch secrets at runtime and never echo values
✅ Seal or encrypt secrets before storing them anywhere

📦 Defense-in-Depth Summary
Attack Vector	Mitigation
Secrets in Git	SOPS, Sealed Secrets
Overused creds	Vault dynamic secrets
Broad access	Vault policy-based access
Secrets in logs	CI masking + controlled injection
No revocation	TTL-based rotation + vault revoke
Cross-env access	Env-scoped secret paths & policies
Lack of audit	Vault audit logs + SIEM integration
🔒 Zero Trust in Practice
Secrets should not be trusted by default — even within your own code or team.

This week’s model follows these Zero Trust principles:

No secret is static unless encrypted at rest

No one gets secrets unless explicitly granted

Secrets are generated just-in-time and revoked automatically

CI/CD doesn’t “own” secrets — it leases them

✅ Let me know when you’re ready for the Week 8: Reflection.md to wrap it up and capture takeaways, challenges, and how this impacts your architecture moving forward.

📁 Week09/Overview.md
Title: 🐳 Container Image Security & SBOMs – Know What You Ship

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you will:

Generate SBOMs (Software Bills of Materials) for your container images

Scan images for vulnerabilities using tools like Trivy, Grype, and Docker Scout

Automate CVE scanning in GitLab CI, GitHub Actions, and CodeBuild

Enforce vulnerability gates in your pipeline

Understand how to sign and verify container images for trust and provenance

🧠 Why This Week Matters
Most modern applications are built on top of dozens of base images and open-source layers, which can include:

Vulnerable libraries (e.g., Log4Shell)

Misconfigured packages (e.g., SSH enabled in Alpine)

Outdated dependencies with public CVEs

Binaries you didn’t even know were included

You can’t secure what you don’t know you’re shipping.

That’s why we use SBOMs and image scanners — to illuminate the supply chain and block bad builds before they hit production.

📚 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🧾 What Is an SBOM?
A Software Bill of Materials is a manifest of every package, binary, and dependency that exists inside your application or image.

It answers:

What’s in this container?

Where did it come from?

Is it vulnerable or outdated?

Example tools:

Syft (by Anchore)

Trivy

Docker Scout

Formats:

SPDX

CycloneDX

Syft JSON

2. 🚨 CVE Scanning: Detecting Vulnerabilities in Images
CVE (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures) are published weaknesses in software libraries.

Container scanners work by:

Analyzing the file system and installed packages

Comparing against CVE databases (NVD, GitHub Advisory, etc.)

Producing severity-based reports (Critical, High, Medium, Low)

Popular scanners:

Tool	Features
Trivy	Fast, covers OS + language deps, SBOM + CVE
Grype	SBOM-first, good CycloneDX/SPDX support
Docker Scout	Built-in to Docker, easy for quick checks
Clair	Integrates with registries, good for Harbor
Snyk	SaaS-focused, Git integration
3. 🔄 Image Signature & Provenance
Image signing ensures that:

The image hasn’t been tampered with

The image was built and pushed by a trusted party

Tooling:

Cosign (part of Sigstore)

Docker Content Trust

Notary v2

You’ll explore:

How to sign your image during build

How to verify signature at deploy

How to enforce only signed images can be deployed

4. 🛡️ Policy Gates in CI/CD
You’ll enforce security gates like:

Gate	Enforcement
No Critical CVEs	Block pipeline
All images signed	Require Cosign verify
SBOM uploaded	Required for artifacts
Approved base image	Dockerfile check or whitelist
Version drift detected	Notify or fail
Example: GitLab CI + Trivy gate

script:
  - trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL my-image:latest
🧩 How This Evolves the App
Your secure note/task app now builds images that are:

Upgrade	Benefit
SBOM included in pipeline	Full supply chain traceability
Trivy & Grype scans	Vulnerabilities caught early
Image signing (Cosign)	Tamper-proof deployment pipeline
Deployment gating	No unscanned or unsigned images reach prod

Fail builds if critical vulnerabilities are found

Sign and verify your own container images

Build pipelines that only deploy known-safe images

📁 Week09/GuidedLab.md
Title: From Build to Trust: SBOMs, CVE Scans & Signed Containers

🧪 Scenario
Your app services (auth, notes, notifications, analytics) are containerized and deployed via GitLab CI, GitHub Actions, and AWS CodePipeline. Security requires that:

Every image must have an SBOM

All images must be scanned before they reach staging or prod

Images must be signed to verify provenance

Pipelines must block critical CVEs automatically

You’ll now implement this end to end using Syft, Grype, Trivy, and Cosign.

🧰 Prerequisites
Docker installed and running

GitHub CLI or GitLab CI access

Tools installed:

syft, grype (Anchore)

trivy (Aqua Security)

cosign (Sigstore)

jq (JSON parsing)

DockerHub or GitHub Container Registry

🛠️ Step 1: Build a Sample Image
git clone https://github.com/your-org/noteapp-notes-service.git
cd noteapp-notes-service
Check your Dockerfile — ensure it uses a minimal base image (e.g., python:3.11-slim).

Build the image:

docker build -t noteapp-notes:dev .
🧾 Step 2: Generate SBOM with Syft
syft noteapp-notes:dev -o json > sbom.json
syft noteapp-notes:dev -o spdx-json > sbom.spdx.json
✅ You now have machine-readable manifests of all dependencies, system packages, and libraries in the image.

🚨 Step 3: Scan Image for Vulnerabilities
🔹 Option A – Using Grype (SBOM-driven)
grype sbom:sbom.json --fail-on critical
If any critical CVEs are found, it will exit with non-zero status.

🔹 Option B – Using Trivy
trivy image --scanners vuln --severity HIGH,CRITICAL noteapp-notes:dev
To gate builds:

trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL noteapp-notes:dev
✅ Trivy will fail the pipeline if a critical CVE is found.

📩 Step 4: Upload SBOM to CI/CD Artifacts
In your CI pipeline (GitHub Actions or GitLab CI), add this:

artifacts:
  paths:
    - sbom.json
    - sbom.spdx.json
✅ SBOMs are now part of the build history, useful for audits and compliance.

🔏 Step 5: Sign Your Image with Cosign
🔹 Generate a key pair (first time only)
cosign generate-key-pair
This creates:

cosign.key

cosign.pub

You can check it into your secret manager or CI pipeline.

🔹 Push the image to a registry
docker tag noteapp-notes:dev yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
docker push yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
🔹 Sign the image
cosign sign --key cosign.key yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
✅ Image signature is now embedded in the registry metadata.

🔍 Step 6: Verify Image Signature in Pipeline
Add a validation step before deploy:

cosign verify --key cosign.pub yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
✅ If the image was not signed or tampered with, this will fail.

🛡️ Step 7: Add to CI/CD (GitHub or GitLab Example)
🔹 GitHub Actions
jobs:
  scan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Build Docker image
        run: docker build -t noteapp-notes:dev .
      
      - name: SBOM
        run: |
          syft noteapp-notes:dev -o json > sbom.json

      - name: Vulnerability Scan
        run: trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL noteapp-notes:dev

      - name: Sign Image
        run: |
          docker tag noteapp-notes:dev yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
          docker push yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
          cosign sign --key ${{ secrets.COSIGN_KEY }} yourusername/noteapp-notes:dev
🔹 GitLab CI
stages:
  - scan

scan_image:
  stage: scan
  script:
    - docker build -t noteapp-notes:dev .
    - syft noteapp-notes:dev -o json > sbom.json
    - trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL noteapp-notes:dev
    - docker push $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHA
    - cosign sign --key $COSIGN_KEY $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHA
  artifacts:
    paths:
      - sbom.json
✅ Success Criteria
Task	Verification
SBOM Generated	sbom.json exists and shows image contents
CVE Scan Run	Scanner detects (or passes) known CVEs
Signature Created	cosign sign succeeds, registry shows signature
Signature Verified	cosign verify passes in CI
Build Gated	Pipeline fails if CRITICAL CVEs found

📁 Week09/GuidedLab.md
Title: From Build to Trust: SBOMs, CVE Scans & Signed Containers

🧪 Scenario Summary
You’ll containerize notes-service, generate an SBOM, scan the image for CVEs, sign it with Cosign, and enforce gates in GitHub Actions and GitLab CI. These practices prepare your app for secure production pipelines.

🛠️ Part 1: Generate an SBOM with Syft
🔹 Step 1.1: Install Syft
curl -sSfL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anchore/syft/main/install.sh | sh -s -- -b /usr/local/bin
Verify:

syft version
🔹 Step 1.2: Build Your Service Image
Assume you're in services/notes-service/ with a working Dockerfile.

docker build -t notes-service:dev .
🔹 Step 1.3: Generate SBOM in JSON format
syft notes-service:dev -o json > sbom-notes-service.json
✅ Now you have a complete software bill of materials for this image.

🔹 Step 1.4: Convert to CycloneDX format (optional)
syft notes-service:dev -o cyclonedx-json > sbom-notes-service.cdx.json
Why? CycloneDX is more interoperable with external scanners and policy engines like Anchore Enterprise, OWASP Dependency-Track, etc.

🧠 Why SBOM?
Shows exact dependencies (base image packages, OS libs, node/python deps)

Required for supply chain compliance (NIST, FedRAMP, SLSA)

Enables drift detection, image diffing, and CVE correlation

🛠️ Part 2: Scan for CVEs
🔹 Option 1: Trivy (recommended)
brew install aquasecurity/trivy/trivy  # macOS
Scan for all vulnerabilities:

trivy image notes-service:dev
Scan only critical:

trivy image --severity CRITICAL notes-service:dev
Scan with SBOM:

trivy sbom sbom-notes-service.json
🔹 Option 2: Grype (Anchore)
curl -sSfL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anchore/grype/main/install.sh | sh -s -- -b /usr/local/bin

grype notes-service:dev
✅ Use Grype when you already have an SBOM — it integrates deeply with Syft.

🛠️ Part 3: Sign Your Image with Cosign
🔹 Step 3.1: Install Cosign
brew install cosign  # or use GitHub releases
🔹 Step 3.2: Generate a key pair (or use keyless signing)
cosign generate-key-pair
This creates:

cosign.key (private key)

cosign.pub (public key)

🔹 Step 3.3: Tag & Push to Registry
docker tag notes-service:dev ghcr.io/<your-org>/notes-service:dev

docker push ghcr.io/<your-org>/notes-service:dev
🔹 Step 3.4: Sign the Image
cosign sign --key cosign.key ghcr.io/<your-org>/notes-service:dev
🔹 Step 3.5: Verify the Signature
cosign verify --key cosign.pub ghcr.io/<your-org>/notes-service:dev
✅ Ensures the image is exactly what was signed — no tampering post-push.

🛠️ Part 4: Add Security Gates in CI/CD
🔹 GitHub Actions .github/workflows/build.yml
jobs:
  scan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - name: Scan with Trivy
        run: |
          docker build -t notes-service:dev .
          trivy image --severity CRITICAL --exit-code 1 notes-service:dev
🚫 Fails the build if any CRITICAL CVEs are found.

🔹 GitLab CI .gitlab-ci.yml
scan:
  stage: test
  image: aquasec/trivy:latest
  script:
    - docker build -t notes-service:dev .
    - trivy image --severity CRITICAL --exit-code 1 notes-service:dev
🔒 Add cosign verify as a step after build to enforce image trust.

✅ Success Criteria
Task	Goal
SBOM created	Generated with Syft or Trivy
CVE scan	Completed with Trivy/Grype
Build gating	CI fails on critical vulnerabilities
Image signing	Signed and verified with Cosign
SBOM published	Artifact attached or stored

📁 Week09/ChallengeLab.md
Title: Security Gates Up: SBOMs, CVEs, and Signed Images Across the Pipeline

🧪 Scenario
You've been tasked with:

Enforcing a production deployment policy:
No unsigned images and no critical vulnerabilities allowed in any container.

To meet this standard, you'll build and validate all 4 services (auth, notes, notifications, analytics) using:

✅ SBOMs (Syft or Trivy)

✅ CVE scanning (Trivy, Grype)

✅ Image signing (Cosign)

✅ Build failure on critical vulnerabilities

✅ Registry trust enforcement

🔧 Setup
All services live in services/:

services/
├── auth-service
├── notes-service
├── notification-service
└── analytics-service
Ensure all services have a valid Dockerfile and can be built locally.

🛠️ Challenge 1: Generate SBOMs for Each Service
Loop through each service and create SBOMs:

for service in auth-service notes-service notification-service analytics-service; do
  docker build -t noteapp/$service:dev ./services/$service
  syft noteapp/$service:dev -o json > sbom-$service.json
done
✅ Store these SBOMs as build artifacts or attach to releases.

🛠️ Challenge 2: Vulnerability Scanning and Gating
Scan each image:

for service in auth-service notes-service notification-service analytics-service; do
  echo "Scanning $service..."
  trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL noteapp/$service:dev || echo "$service failed security gate!"
done
✅ Any CRITICAL CVE should fail the build.

🛠️ Challenge 3: Image Signing with Cosign
Generate or reuse your cosign.key and cosign.pub.

Push and sign each image:

for service in auth-service notes-service notification-service analytics-service; do
  docker tag noteapp/$service:dev ghcr.io/<your-org>/$service:dev
  docker push ghcr.io/<your-org>/$service:dev
  cosign sign --key cosign.key ghcr.io/<your-org>/$service:dev
done
✅ Each image is now signed in the registry.

🛠️ Challenge 4: Signature Enforcement (Verification Step)
Before deploy (in CI/CD or manual step), verify each signature:

for service in auth-service notes-service notification-service analytics-service; do
  cosign verify --key cosign.pub ghcr.io/<your-org>/$service:dev
done
✅ CI fails if the signature is missing or invalid.

🛠️ Challenge 5: CI/CD Integration (Example for GitHub Actions)
Use a matrix strategy to run a reusable job across services:

jobs:
  scan-sign:
    strategy:
      matrix:
        service: [auth-service, notes-service, notification-service, analytics-service]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - name: Build & Scan
        run: |
          docker build -t ${{ matrix.service }}:dev ./services/${{ matrix.service }}
          syft ${{ matrix.service }}:dev -o json > sbom-${{ matrix.service }}.json
          trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL ${{ matrix.service }}:dev
      - name: Push & Sign
        run: |
          docker tag ${{ matrix.service }}:dev ghcr.io/<your-org>/${{ matrix.service }}:dev
          docker push ghcr.io/<your-org>/${{ matrix.service }}:dev
          cosign sign --key ${{ secrets.COSIGN_KEY }} ghcr.io/<your-org>/${{ matrix.service }}:dev
✅ Reusable across all microservices.

🛡️ Bonus Challenge: Approved Base Image Enforcement
Create a bash check to block Dockerfiles using disallowed base images:

grep "^FROM " services/*/Dockerfile | grep -v "python:3.11-slim"
✅ Fails pipeline if a Dockerfile uses an unapproved base.

✅ Success Criteria
Checkpoint	Passes If…
SBOM exists for each service	sbom-*.json is present
Trivy scan blocks critical CVEs	--exit-code 1 halts on high risk
Cosign signature present	verify command passes for each image
All services covered	Auth, Notes, Notifications, Analytics
CI/CD gates enforced	No unsigned or vulnerable images pass

Title: Ship with Confidence: How SBOMs, CVE Scans & Signing Protect the Supply Chain

🎯 Security Objectives This Week
This week’s focus was on supply chain integrity:

Goal	Description
🧾 SBOM	Know everything that’s inside your images
🚨 CVE Scanning	Detect known vulnerable components
🔏 Image Signing	Ensure only trusted images are deployed
🔐 Gated Deployments	Block builds that fail security checks
🛡️ Real-World Threats You’re Now Mitigating
1. 💥 Vulnerable Base Images or Open-Source Dependencies
Threat:

Using an outdated base image with known CVEs (e.g. Ubuntu, Python, Node.js)

Or adding a vulnerable library like log4j-core without knowing it

Real Incidents:

Log4Shell (2021) → thousands of apps compromised via hidden Java lib

Mitigation:
✅ You now scan every image with Trivy or Grype
✅ You can fail builds on CRITICAL CVEs
✅ SBOMs give you visibility into the entire dependency tree

2. 🧟‍♂️ “Zombie Dependencies” and Transitive Risks
Threat:

Image contains packages you didn’t explicitly install (e.g., cron, netcat, curl, telnet)

These can be abused in RCE attacks

Mitigation:
✅ SBOM exposes all packages, not just what you install
✅ You can compare SBOMs between versions and detect drift

3. 🧬 Image Tampering in Registry
Threat:

Attacker pushes a malicious image to your registry using your tag (:prod)

Or image is modified post-build (supply chain attack)

Real Incidents:

SolarWinds (build system compromise)

DockerHub hijacked tags (crypto miners)

Mitigation:
✅ You now sign all images using Cosign
✅ You verify images before deploy in CI/CD
✅ Signature ensures image was built by you and hasn’t changed

4. 🔓 Untrusted Images in CI/CD
Threat:

CI/CD pipelines deploy images that are:

Missing scans

Unsigned

Built outside your system

Mitigation:
✅ Pipelines enforce:

SBOM generation

CVE scan gating (--exit-code 1)

Cosign verification

Artifact retention for SBOMs

5. 🧾 Regulatory Compliance & SBOM Requirements
Threat:

No ability to prove what’s inside your software

Inability to respond to supply chain disclosures

Emerging Standards:

U.S. Executive Order (EO 14028)

NIST SP 800-218 (SLSA), SPDX/CycloneDX SBOM formats

FedRAMP, ISO 27001 supply chain mandates

Mitigation:
✅ You now produce and retain SPDX or CycloneDX SBOMs
✅ You could plug them into tools like:

DependencyTrack

Anchore Enterprise

OpenSCAP

✅ Security Wins So Far
Capability	Tool
CVE Detection	Trivy, Grype
SBOM Generation	Syft, Trivy
Image Signing	Cosign
Deployment Gates	CI/CD (GitHub/GitLab)
Image Verification	Cosign Verify
Base Image Enforcement	Manual grep or CI linting
🧠 Secure by Default Mindset
You’re beginning to build a “no deploy without validation” pipeline culture — key to DevSecOps.

Every artifact must:

Declare what’s inside (SBOM)

Prove it’s safe (CVE-free)

Prove who built it (signature)

You’re not just checking a box — you’re reducing your blast radius, shortening MTTR, and creating auditability.

📁 Week10/Overview.md
Title: 🏗️ IaC Fundamentals & Security – Terraform, CloudFormation, SAM

🎯 Goal
By the end of this week, you’ll:

Understand how to manage infrastructure as code (IaC) with Terraform, CloudFormation (CFN), and AWS SAM

Learn the security implications of provisioning via code

Scan IaC for misconfigurations and policy violations

Enforce gates to prevent insecure infrastructure from being deployed

Practice GitOps-style workflows for infrastructure changes

🧠 Why IaC Security Matters
Infrastructure is just as exploitable as code — and mistakes here are often invisible until it's too late.

Consider:

An S3 bucket opened to the public by accident

A security group allowing 0.0.0.0/0 on port 22

A Lambda deployed without encryption or least-privilege IAM

These mistakes often come from IaC drift, copy-paste reuse, or CI/CD misconfigurations.

📚 Core Concepts & Theory
1. 🧱 Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
IaC lets you define your cloud infrastructure using version-controlled, declarative files:

Tool	Format	Provider Support
Terraform	.tf HCL	Multi-cloud (AWS, GCP, Azure, etc.)
CloudFormation	YAML/JSON	AWS-native
AWS SAM	YAML	Serverless-focused (built on CFN)
Benefits:

Reproducibility

Audibility (version control)

Automation-ready for CI/CD

2. 🛑 IaC Security Risks
Misconfiguration	Impact
Open ports on public IPs	Remote exploitation
Overly permissive IAM roles	Privilege escalation
Unencrypted resources	Data exposure
Missing tagging/policies	Compliance violations
Many of these risks stem from:

Reusing insecure modules

Default values left unconfigured

Lack of drift detection between declared and deployed state

3. 🔍 Tools to Secure IaC
✅ Terraform Scanning
Tool	Use
tfsec	Static code analysis for Terraform
checkov	Broad IaC scanner (Terraform, CFN, Kubernetes)
terrascan	Open policy enforcement for Terraform and others
✅ CloudFormation Scanning
Tool	Use
cfn-lint	Syntax and policy checks
cfn-nag	Security best practices and misconfiguration alerts
✅ AWS SAM
Has built-in validation with sam validate

Deploys CloudFormation templates, so CFN tools apply

4. 🔐 GitOps for IaC
IaC should be managed via GitOps:

Infrastructure change requests are code-reviewed

CI/CD applies validated and scanned templates only

Changes are logged, auditable, and testable

Drift detection is built into workflow (e.g., terraform plan, aws cloudformation drift-detection)

🧩 How This Evolves the App
Your secure note/task manager now provisions infrastructure using reproducible code:

Upgrade	Benefit
Terraform-based VPCs, ECRs, RDS, IAM	Versioned and auditable infra
SAM-defined Lambda APIs	Structured and secured
CFN templates for ECS services	Controlled via change reviews
Scanners enforce compliance	Misconfigurations fail fast
✅ You’ll Be Able To…
Write and validate Terraform for cloud resources

Scan IaC for security issues before deploy

Use GitOps to manage infrastructure lifecycle

Detect drift between declared and real infra

Catch IAM, network, and storage misconfigurations early

🔜 Ready for the GuidedLab.md, where you’ll:

Create a secure VPC, ECS cluster, and supporting resources with Terraform

Define serverless APIs with SAM

Scan all infra code with tfsec, checkov, and cfn-nag

Add IaC validation into your CI/CD

📁 Week10/GuidedLab.md
Title: Secure Infrastructure as Code with Terraform, CFN & AWS SAM

🧪 Scenario
Your DevSecOps team is tasked with building infrastructure for the notes-service as part of the evolving note/task manager app. You’ll:

Provision a secure VPC, ECS Cluster, ECR repo, and IAM roles using Terraform

Define a serverless endpoint using AWS SAM

Create a CloudFormation template for static infrastructure (e.g. S3 bucket)

Scan all infrastructure code for misconfigurations using tfsec, checkov, and cfn-nag

🛠️ Part 1: Secure VPC + ECS Cluster using Terraform
🔹 Step 1.1: Create project structure
mkdir -p iac/terraform/notes-infra
cd iac/terraform/notes-infra
🔹 Step 1.2: Create main.tf
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
}

resource "aws_vpc" "secure_vpc" {
  cidr_block           = "10.0.0.0/16"
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = {
    Name = "notes-secure-vpc"
  }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "private_subnet" {
  vpc_id     = aws_vpc.secure_vpc.id
  cidr_block = "10.0.1.0/24"
  tags = {
    Name = "notes-private-subnet"
  }
}

resource "aws_ecr_repository" "notes_repo" {
  name                 = "notes-service"
  image_tag_mutability = "IMMUTABLE"
  image_scanning_configuration {
    scan_on_push = true
  }
}

resource "aws_ecs_cluster" "notes_cluster" {
  name = "notes-ecs-cluster"
}
🔹 Step 1.3: Initialize and apply
terraform init
terraform plan -out=tfplan
terraform apply tfplan
✅ You now have a secure ECS-ready VPC, subnet, and ECR repository with image scanning enabled.

🔍 Step 1.4: Scan Terraform code with tfsec and checkov
# Install
brew install tfsec
pip install checkov

# Run scans
tfsec .
checkov -d .
Look for alerts like:

Insecure CIDR blocks (e.g., 0.0.0.0/0)

Missing tags

IAM policies with *

Lack of encryption

✅ Fix any high-severity issues before continuing.

  SharedDataBucket:
    Type: AWS::S3::Bucket

      BucketName: devsecops-shared-data
      PublicAccessBlockConfiguration:
        BlockPublicAcls: true
        BlockPublicPolicy: true
        RestrictPublicBuckets: true
      VersioningConfiguration:
        Status: Enabled

  SharedReadOnlyRole:
    Type: AWS::IAM::Role
    Properties:
      AssumeRolePolicyDocument:
        Statement:
          - Effect: Allow
            Principal:
              Service: lambda.amazonaws.com
            Action: sts:AssumeRole
      Policies:
        - PolicyName: ReadOnlySharedBucket
          PolicyDocument:
            Statement:
              - Effect: Allow
                Action: s3:GetObject
                Resource: arn:aws:s3:::devsecops-shared-data/*

📁 Week10/Security.md
Title: Invisible No More: Securing the Cloud Layer with IaC Best Practices

🛡️ DevSecOps Principles in Action
This week, you secured not just your apps, but the infrastructure they run on.

Infrastructure misconfigurations cause:

Major data breaches (e.g. public S3 buckets)

Privilege escalation

Resource exposure

Compliance violations

Your IaC scanning and GitOps enforcement now act as the firewall at the code level.

🔍 Threats You’re Now Mitigating
1. ☁️ Misconfigured Networking or Public Access
Real Risk:

VPCs with public subnets

Security groups allowing unrestricted ingress

Public S3 buckets leaking data

Incidents:

Accenture 2021: public S3 bucket exposed client data

Tesla 2018: crypto mining container accessible via open dashboard

Mitigation:
✅ tfsec + checkov flagged wide-open ports
✅ cfn-nag flagged buckets without access control
✅ You enforced BlockPublicAccess and locked down subnets

2. 🔐 Overly Permissive IAM Roles
Real Risk:

Lambda functions with iam:PassRole or * permissions

Developers using production-level roles

Roles without Condition or Resource limits

Incidents:

Capital One 2019: IAM misconfig → SSRF into metadata service

Mitigation:
✅ IAM roles follow least-privilege
✅ Static analysis via checkov and cfn-nag blocked dangerous policies
✅ Shared roles are scoped with Resource: arn:aws:s3:::bucket/*

3. 🔁 Drift Between Code and Cloud
Real Risk:

Infra changes made manually outside of Git

Reconciliations missed in audits or rollbacks

Mitigation:
✅ You used terraform plan and drift-detection to identify drift
✅ Changes must go through code reviews (GitOps)
✅ CI/CD detects unauthorized configuration changes

4. 🪟 Insecure Defaults in CFN/SAM Templates
Real Risk:

Templates copied from online tutorials with:

Logging disabled

Publicly accessible APIs

Missing encryption

Mitigation:
✅ SAM + CFN templates validated using cfn-nag
✅ Enforced tracing, logging, and secure policies
✅ IAM and API Gateway scoped to least privilege and throttled

5. 🔍 Lack of CI/CD Guardrails
Real Risk:

IaC code gets merged without security review

Mistakes go straight into live cloud environments

Mitigation:
✅ Scanning tools (tfsec, checkov, cfn-nag) run in pipeline
✅ Builds fail on HIGH/CRITICAL severity findings
✅ Git tracks all changes — no “invisible infra changes”

🧠 Key Security Wins
Capability	Tool
Terraform misconfig detection	tfsec, checkov
CFN & SAM auditing	cfn-nag, sam validate
IAM least privilege enforcement	Policy reviews, scoped roles
Network hardening	Subnet + security group checks
GitOps for infra	terraform plan, sam deploy, version-controlled IaC
CI/CD enforcement	Pipelines fail on violations
🚀 App Evolution Summary
Service	Infra	Security Added
auth-service	ECS (Terraform)	Hardened VPC, ECS cluster, secure IAM
notes-service	Lambda + API Gateway (SAM)	IAM least-privilege, tracing, secure policies
Shared Infra	CFN S3 & IAM	Public access blocked, versioned, scoped roles
Your app now runs on secure-by-default, reproducible infrastructure — no console clicks, no guesswork, no invisible drift.

Title: ⚙️ Scripting & Automation with Bash & Python

🧠 Conceptual Overview
🔧 Why Scripting Matters in DevSecOps
Scripting is the glue between infrastructure, CI/CD, security scanning, monitoring, and deployment. Whether you're:

Automating a cloud backup

Generating a signed SBOM

Or sending audit logs to a compliance bucket

…chances are, a script is behind it.

Scripting done right enables:

Repeatability & reliability

Secure execution of privileged tasks

Team-wide operational consistency

Lightweight automation without heavyweight pipelines

🐚 Bash vs 🐍 Python
Feature	Bash	Python
Great for	Shell tasks, chaining commands, log rotation	Complex logic, API integrations, report generation
Strengths	Native to UNIX, cron-friendly	Libraries (e.g. boto3, requests), readable
Weaknesses	Harder to test/scale	Slower for one-liners
DevSecOps Use	Glue scripts, log rotation, backups	Report generators, scanners, validations
We'll use both in this module — in the right places and with security in mind.

🔐 Security Risks in Scripting
Common mistakes we’ll avoid:

Hardcoding secrets

Unsafe input parsing ($1, eval, os.system())

Over-permissioned scripts (running as root)

Logging sensitive info

Shell injection ($(user_input))

You’ll learn to:

Validate inputs

Sanitize environment variables

Use AWS SSM + .env for secure secrets loading

Log responsibly

Title: Secure Scripting: Bash & Python Automation for Cloud, CI/CD & Compliance

🧪 Scenario
You’ve been asked to:

Monitor the health of your ECS services

Generate SBOM reports from vulnerability scans

Fetch secrets from AWS SSM Parameter Store

Log archive and cleanup routines

You’ll build Bash and Python scripts that run locally and in CI/CD — with secure handling of secrets, inputs, logs, and IAM.

🧱 Project Structure
We’ll add this to your app repo:

scripts/
├── health/
│   └── check_ecs_health.sh
├── sbom/
│   └── trivy_to_html.py
├── secrets/
│   └── fetch_secrets.sh
├── logs/
│   └── archive_logs.py
.env.example
You’ll also prepare scripts/ci/ for jobs that can run in GitHub Actions or GitLab CI.

🐚 Part 1: Bash Script – ECS Service Health Check
🔹 Step 1.1: check_ecs_health.sh
#!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail

CLUSTER_NAME="${1:-}"
SERVICE_NAME="${2:-}"

if [[ -z "$CLUSTER_NAME" || -z "$SERVICE_NAME" ]]; then
  echo "Usage: $0 <cluster-name> <service-name>"
  exit 1
fi

STATUS=$(aws ecs describe-services \
  --cluster "$CLUSTER_NAME" \
  --services "$SERVICE_NAME" \
  --query "services[0].deployments[?status=='PRIMARY'].rolloutState" \
  --output text)

if [[ "$STATUS" == "COMPLETED" ]]; then
  echo "✅ $SERVICE_NAME is healthy in $CLUSTER_NAME"
else
  echo "❌ $SERVICE_NAME is not fully deployed. Status: $STATUS"
  exit 2
fi
✅ Security Practices:

set -euo pipefail → fail fast

Validates arguments

Doesn’t log secrets or tokens

🔹 Step 1.2: Run locally
chmod +x scripts/health/check_ecs_health.sh
./scripts/health/check_ecs_health.sh notes-cluster notes-service
🐍 Part 2: Python Script – SBOM Formatter
🔹 Step 2.1: Install Trivy and generate SBOM
trivy image --format cyclonedx --output results.json yourapp:latest
🔹 Step 2.2: trivy_to_html.py
import json, sys
from pathlib import Path

def generate_html(input_file: str):
    data = json.loads(Path(input_file).read_text())

    html = "<html><body><h1>SBOM Vulnerabilities</h1><ul>"
    for component in data.get("components", []):
        name = component.get("name", "unknown")
        version = component.get("version", "unknown")
        html += f"<li>{name} – {version}</li>"
    html += "</ul></body></html>"

    output_file = input_file.replace(".json", ".html")
    Path(output_file).write_text(html)
    print(f"✅ SBOM report saved to {output_file}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) != 2:
        print("Usage: python trivy_to_html.py results.json")
        sys.exit(1)
    generate_html(sys.argv[1])
✅ Security Practices:

No eval(), no shell injection

Validates file input

python scripts/sbom/trivy_to_html.py results.json
🔐 Part 3: Bash – Secure Secrets Fetch from AWS SSM
🔹 Step 3.1: fetch_secrets.sh
#!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail

SECRETS=("DB_PASSWORD" "API_KEY")

for secret in "${SECRETS[@]}"; do
  VALUE=$(aws ssm get-parameter \
    --name "/devsecops/${secret}" \
    --with-decryption \
    --query "Parameter.Value" \
    --output text)
  echo "export $secret='$VALUE'" >> .env
done

echo "✅ Secrets loaded into .env"
✅ Fetches encrypted parameters securely
✅ IAM permissions required: ssm:GetParameter, kms:Decrypt

☁️ Part 4: Python – Archive Logs to S3
import boto3, datetime, gzip, shutil
from pathlib import Path

logs_dir = Path("/var/log/myapp")
bucket = "devsecops-log-archive"
date = datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d")

session = boto3.Session()
s3 = session.client("s3")

for log_file in logs_dir.glob("*.log"):
    compressed = log_file.with_suffix(".gz")
    with open(log_file, 'rb') as f_in, gzip.open(compressed, 'wb') as f_out:
        shutil.copyfileobj(f_in, f_out)

    s3_key = f"logs/{date}/{compressed.name}"
    s3.upload_file(str(compressed), bucket, s3_key)
    print(f"✅ Uploaded {compressed.name} to s3://{bucket}/{s3_key}")
✅ Handles log rotation
✅ Compresses locally, uploads securely with Boto3
✅ Avoids uploading raw secrets

🧪 Bonus: CI/CD Integration
GitHub Actions example:

jobs:
  health-check:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - run: bash scripts/health/check_ecs_health.sh notes-cluster notes-service
Secrets fetch before pipeline step:

      - name: Load Secrets
        run: bash scripts/secrets/fetch_secrets.sh
✅ Your scripts are now portable, secure, and CI-ready.

Title: End-to-End Secure Automation for a Production Microservice Stack

🧪 Scenario
You're now part of the DevSecOps team managing a growing microservices-based app (auth-service, notes-service, notification-service) hosted on ECS and Lambda.

You're tasked with scripting a secure and portable daily automation routine that:

Checks health of all deployed services

Rotates old logs from each container

Generates and stores daily SBOM reports

Uploads all logs + reports to a secure S3 archive

Runs automatically via cron or CI/CD pipeline

You must ensure:

Secrets are never exposed

IAM roles are scoped correctly

Logging avoids sensitive data

CI jobs can run securely in both GitHub and GitLab environments

🎯 Requirements
Task	Language	Tooling
ECS health & Lambda checks	Bash	AWS CLI
SBOM generation	Python	Trivy
Log rotation & archive	Bash + Python	gzip, boto3
Secrets loading	Bash	AWS SSM
GitOps integration	Both	GitHub Actions / GitLab CI
🛠️ Tasks
✅ 1. Create a unified Bash script scripts/automation/daily_ops.sh
This script should:

Accept parameters (e.g. service name, region)

Call other scripts to:

Check service health (ECS + Lambda)

Rotate & archive logs

Generate SBOM and convert to HTML

Upload artifacts to a specific devsecops-prod-logs S3 bucket

Load secrets securely from SSM

The top of the script should look like:

#!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail

ENV="${1:-prod}"
REGION="${2:-us-east-1}"
The script must log actions to a timestamped file in logs/ (not to stdout).

✅ 2. Write a Python script scripts/sbom/sbom_daily_report.py
It should take an image name and output both .json and .html

Store results in sbom/YYYY-MM-DD/ folders

Upload to S3 automatically using boto3

Send an optional summary to Slack (mock or print)

✅ 3. Secure .env file loading
Create .env.example with:

AWS_REGION=
S3_ARCHIVE_BUCKET=
SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL=
Bash and Python scripts should read this securely using dotenv or source .env

In CI/CD: inject values via secrets manager (e.g., AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY, SLACK_TOKEN)

✅ 4. GitLab or GitHub CI/CD Integration
Create a pipeline job to run your daily_ops.sh:

GitHub Actions example:

jobs:
  daily-automation:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - run: |
          bash scripts/automation/daily_ops.sh prod us-east-1
        env:
          AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID: ${{ secrets.AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID }}
          AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY: ${{ secrets.AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY }}
GitLab CI example:

daily_ops:
  script:
    - bash scripts/automation/daily_ops.sh prod us-east-1
  only:
    - schedules
🔐 Security Requirements
No secrets hardcoded in scripts

Use IAM roles with least privilege (e.g., only allow upload to specific bucket)

Avoid using eval, os.system() or unsanitized variables

Set up .gitignore for .env, *.log, *.gz, and generated SBOM files

📦 Output Artifacts (Required)
Artifact	Format	Notes
Health Check Logs	logs/ecs_health_YYYY-MM-DD.log	Plaintext, timestamped
SBOM JSON	sbom/YYYY-MM-DD/*.json	CycloneDX
SBOM HTML	sbom/YYYY-MM-DD/*.html	For human consumption
Uploaded logs	s3://devsecops-prod-logs/...	Auto-versioned
Archived app logs	*.gz	Auto-uploaded
💡 Bonus Challenges
Add Slack notifications using Python (requests.post) to alert if health check fails

Encrypt log archives using AWS KMS before upload

Support different environments (dev, qa, staging, prod) with config files

✅ Completion Criteria
You’ve succeeded if:

All scripts are functional, reusable, secure

Logs, SBOMs, and outputs are archived to S3

📁 Week5/Security.md
Title: Scripting Security: Threat Surfaces, Mitigations, and DevSecOps Practices

🧠 Why This Matters
Scripting is powerful — but without care, it becomes one of the top sources of security breaches, especially in DevOps pipelines and automation tasks.

A misplaced $VAR, insecure file permissions, or a leaked log can expose secrets, credentials, or production data.

This section analyzes common attack surfaces in Bash and Python scripting, and how to prevent them in real-world DevSecOps pipelines.

⚠️ Common Scripting Threats
Threat	Description	Exploit Example
🧨 Shell Injection	Unsanitized inputs used in eval, exec, backticks	$(rm -rf /) injected via env var
🧪 Hardcoded Secrets	Static API keys, tokens, or DB credentials in scripts	Secrets committed to Git repo
🔓 Insecure File Handling	World-readable .env, log, or SBOM files	Leaked secrets or CVEs in logs
🦥 Improper Error Handling	Scripts silently failing or ignoring errors	Missed backup, broken deploy
🔁 Privilege Abuse	Scripts that assume or run as root	Escalation vector in compromised CI/CD
🗃️ Environment Leakage	Dumping entire env into logs	Secrets exposed in log collectors
🛡️ DevSecOps Security Practices
Here’s how your Week 5 scripts mitigate these risks:

✅ 1. Use set -euo pipefail in Bash
set -euo pipefail
Prevents:

Silent errors (-e)

Use of unset vars (-u)

Ignored broken pipes (-o pipefail)

📌 Why: Hard to troubleshoot broken automation when failures are masked.

✅ 2. Avoid eval, backticks, or shell expansion from user inputs
Instead of:

eval "aws ${user_input}"
Use:

aws ecs describe-services --cluster "$CLUSTER_NAME"
📌 Why: Prevents arbitrary command execution via user input or CI injection.

✅ 3. Externalize secrets – never hardcode
✅ Load from:

.env files (with .gitignore)

AWS SSM Parameter Store (via aws ssm get-parameter)

GitHub/GitLab Secrets

📌 Why: Hardcoded secrets lead to breaches, Git leaks, and audit failures.

✅ 4. Sanitize and validate all inputs
In Bash:

if [[ -z "$CLUSTER_NAME" ]]; then echo "Missing cluster"; exit 1; fi
In Python:

if not input_file.endswith(".json"):
    raise ValueError("Invalid file format")
📌 Why: Prevent malformed or malicious input from causing damage.

✅ 5. Secure file permissions
SBOMs should not be world-readable

Log archives must not include secrets

.env should have chmod 600

📌 Why: Files created in CI/CD are often globally readable if not explicitly locked down.

✅ 6. Use IAM-scoped roles
Grant ssm:GetParameter, kms:Decrypt, s3:PutObject only where needed

Use temporary credentials via CI roles or federated access

📌 Why: Scripts should never use long-lived credentials or over-privileged IAM roles.

✅ 7. Log responsibly
Instead of:

echo "Secret is $DB_PASSWORD"
Do:

echo "Fetched DB_PASSWORD from SSM"
📌 Why: Secrets in logs are a top CVE exploitation path (especially in CloudWatch, Papertrail, etc.)

🔐 Extra Defenses You Can Add
Technique	Description
sh -n or shellcheck	Validate Bash scripts statically
Python bandit	Linter for insecure Python practices
Audit logging	Log script invocations (not contents)
Encrypted S3 buckets	Secure backups + logs in transit and at rest
KMS encryption	Encrypt .gz, .json, .html files with customer-managed keys
🚧 Real-World Security Failure Scenarios
Scenario	Root Cause	Prevention
CI job accidentally commits .env with secrets	.env wasn’t gitignored	Add .env to .gitignore and use vault
Trivy report with CVEs gets exposed via public S3 bucket	No S3 bucket policy or permissions	Apply strict S3 bucket policy
Python log script fails silently during pipeline	No error handling in subprocess	Use try/except with logging
Log archive includes DEBUG output with token	Over-verbose logging	Sanitize logs and scrub sensitive info

📁 Week7/Overview.md
Title: 🏗️ CI/CD Pipelines Part II – AWS CodePipeline & CodeBuild
Focus: Building secure, production-grade CI/CD workflows using AWS-native tooling and integrating prior automation.

🧠 Conceptual Overview
🧭 Why AWS CodePipeline in DevSecOps?
CI/CD is a critical automation layer — and in AWS-native environments, using CodePipeline + CodeBuild can give you:

Tighter IAM-based security boundaries

Fully managed scalability with zero runners

Built-in integration with CodeCommit, ECR, S3, and CloudFormation

Direct control of source → build → test → deploy flows

Easier traceability and event-driven automation

But misconfigured CodePipelines can:

Leak secrets

Deploy unscanned images

Bypass security controls

This module teaches you how to secure CodePipeline from commit to deploy, integrating SBOM, secrets injection, and controlled IAM roles.

🧩 Evolving the App This Week
This week, you’ll:

Build a secure AWS CodePipeline for your notes-service

Use CodeBuild to:

Run unit tests

Run Trivy image scans

Generate SBOMs

Store SBOM + scan reports to S3

Deploy your app to ECS (or Lambda) if all checks pass

Use CloudWatch Logs and SNS alerts for pipeline observability

🔐 DevSecOps Learning Objectives
By the end of this week, you’ll be able to:

✅ Configure CodePipeline using YAML (via AWS CDK or console)
✅ Write secure buildspec.yml files for CodeBuild
✅ Inject and use secrets from AWS SSM securely
✅ Automate SBOM generation and Trivy scans inside CodeBuild
✅ Enforce security gates before deploy (scan must pass)
✅ Isolate build and deploy stages using scoped IAM roles
✅ Push artifacts securely to S3, ECR, or downstream stages

🧱 What You’ll Build
Component	Description
pipeline.yaml	Defines pipeline structure (CDK or CloudFormation)
buildspec.yml	Tells CodeBuild how to test, scan, generate SBOM
aws-secrets-inject.sh	Reuses secure scripting from Week 5
deploy-to-ecs.sh	Deployment logic gated by scan status
infra/pipeline/	Infra-as-code directory for the pipeline
📦 Pipeline Stages
Stage	Tool	Purpose
Source	GitHub / CodeCommit	Pull app source
Build	CodeBuild	Test + scan + SBOM
Approval	Manual / Scan check	Block unverified deploys
Deploy	ECS / Lambda	Trigger secure deploy
Notify	SNS / Slack	Optional alerts for failures
🧰 Tools & Services
Service	Use
CodePipeline	CI/CD orchestration
CodeBuild	Runs build, test, scan, SBOM
SSM Parameter Store	Secure secret injection
ECS or Lambda	Target deployment platform
S3	Artifact and report storage
CloudWatch Logs	Log pipeline and build output
CDK	Optional: Pipeline definition as code
🧠 Why CodePipeline Security Matters
Poorly secured pipelines often:

Expose environment variables in logs

Let unscanned code go to prod

Fail to isolate build credentials

Ignore IAM boundaries between build/test/deploy

This week, you’ll solve all of these by:

Using secretsManager or ssm:GetParameter for secrets

Applying IAM-per-stage security

Generating SBOM + Trivy scan gates

Storing build artifacts securely in S3

📁 Week7/GuidedLab.md
Title: Building a Secure, Automated CI/CD Pipeline with AWS CodePipeline & CodeBuild

🧪 Scenario
You're building the production-grade pipeline for notes-service.
It must:

Pull source from GitHub

Run tests and generate SBOM using CodeBuild

Enforce Trivy vulnerability scans

Store artifacts in S3

Deploy to ECS (or Lambda) only if scans pass

Securely handle secrets via SSM

Notify on failures via SNS (optional)

🔧 Pre-Requirements
✅ A working notes-service repo
✅ AWS CLI and credentials set up
✅ IAM permissions to create:

CodePipeline

CodeBuild projects

S3 bucket

ECS service or Lambda function

🧱 Directory Layout
Add to your repo:

infra/
├── pipeline/
│   ├── pipeline.yaml        # CloudFormation/CDK definition
│   ├── buildspec.yml        # CodeBuild instructions
│   └── deploy.sh            # Secure deploy script
scripts/
├── sbom/
│   └── trivy_to_html.py     # Reuse from Week 5
├── secrets/
│   └── fetch_secrets.sh     # Reuse from Week 5
.env.example
🛠️ Step-by-Step
✅ Step 1: Define buildspec.yml
This controls what CodeBuild does.
Create infra/pipeline/buildspec.yml:

version: 0.2

env:
  variables:
    IMAGE_NAME: "notes-service"
  secrets-manager:
    DB_PASSWORD: "notes/db_password"
    API_KEY: "notes/api_key"

phases:
  install:
    runtime-versions:
      nodejs: 18
      python: 3.11
    commands:
      - echo "🔐 Installing tools"
      - pip install trivy boto3
  pre_build:
    commands:
      - echo "🔍 Running tests..."
      - npm install && npm run test
  build:
    commands:
      - echo "🔐 Running vulnerability scan"
      - trivy image --format cyclonedx --output sbom.json $IMAGE_NAME
      - python scripts/sbom/trivy_to_html.py sbom.json
      - mkdir -p artifacts && mv sbom.* artifacts/
  post_build:
    commands:
      - echo "📦 Uploading to S3"
      - aws s3 cp artifacts/ s3://devsecops-artifacts/$IMAGE_NAME/ --recursive
artifacts:
  files:
    - artifacts/*
✅ Security Notes:

Secrets pulled from AWS Secrets Manager (not env vars)

No hardcoded creds

All logs are S3-uploaded, not printed

✅ Step 2: Create the S3 Artifact Bucket
aws s3 mb s3://devsecops-artifacts
Restrict access via bucket policy to:

CodePipeline

CodeBuild

Your IAM user

✅ Step 3: Deploy CodePipeline using CloudFormation or CDK
Option A: YAML (CloudFormation)
Create infra/pipeline/pipeline.yaml:

Resources:
  NotesBuildRole:
    Type: AWS::IAM::Role
    Properties:
      AssumeRolePolicyDocument:
        Statement:
          - Effect: Allow
            Principal:
              Service: codebuild.amazonaws.com
            Action: sts:AssumeRole
      Policies:
        - PolicyName: BuildAccess
          PolicyDocument:
            Statement:
              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - ssm:GetParameter
                  - secretsmanager:GetSecretValue
                  - s3:PutObject
                Resource: "*"

  NotesCodeBuild:
    Type: AWS::CodeBuild::Project
    Properties:
      Name: notes-service-build
      Source:
        Type: GITHUB
        Location: https://github.com/YOUR_USER/notes-service
      Artifacts:
        Type: CODEPIPELINE
      Environment:
        Type: LINUX_CONTAINER
        ComputeType: BUILD_GENERAL1_SMALL
        Image: aws/codebuild/standard:7.0
        PrivilegedMode: true
      ServiceRole: !GetAtt NotesBuildRole.Arn
      TimeoutInMinutes: 10
      BuildSpec: infra/pipeline/buildspec.yml

  Pipeline:
    Type: AWS::CodePipeline::Pipeline
    Properties:
      Name: notes-service-pipeline
      RoleArn: arn:aws:iam::YOUR_ACCOUNT_ID:role/CodePipelineServiceRole
      ArtifactStore:
        Type: S3
        Location: devsecops-artifacts
      Stages:
        - Name: Source
          Actions:
            - Name: GitHubSource
              ActionTypeId:
                Category: Source
                Owner: ThirdParty
                Provider: GitHub
                Version: "1"
              OutputArtifacts:
                - Name: SourceOutput
              Configuration:
                Owner: YOUR_USER
                Repo: notes-service
                Branch: main
                OAuthToken: YOUR_GITHUB_TOKEN
        - Name: Build
          Actions:
            - Name: BuildNotes
              ActionTypeId:
                Category: Build
                Owner: AWS
                Provider: CodeBuild
                Version: "1"
              InputArtifacts:
                - Name: SourceOutput
              OutputArtifacts:
                - Name: BuildOutput
              Configuration:
                ProjectName: !Ref NotesCodeBuild
Deploy:

aws cloudformation deploy \
  --template-file infra/pipeline/pipeline.yaml \
  --stack-name notes-pipeline \
  --capabilities CAPABILITY_NAMED_IAM
✅ Step 4: Secure Deployment Script
In infra/pipeline/deploy.sh:

#!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail

echo "Deploying $IMAGE_NAME to ECS..."

aws ecs update-service \
  --cluster notes-cluster \
  --service notes-service \
  --force-new-deployment

echo "✅ Deployed successfully"
Only execute this if scans pass.

✅ Step 5: Integrate Notifications (Optional)
You can add a failure notification via SNS:

  Notifications:
    Type: AWS::SNS::Topic
    Properties:
      TopicName: BuildFailures
Or in CodeBuild UI, configure notification rules.

✅ What You Now Have
Component	Secured?	Notes
buildspec.yml	✅	Secrets from Secrets Manager
CodePipeline stages	✅	Isolated, structured
SBOM & scans	✅	Artifacted and uploaded
IAM Roles	✅	Per-stage, scoped
Deploy	✅	Only after passing build

📦 Deliverables
Item	Format

Add Trivy logic like:

CRITICALS=$(trivy image --severity CRITICAL --exit-code 0 --format json -o trivy.json $IMAGE_NAME | jq '.Results[].Vulnerabilities[] | select(.Severity=="CRITICAL")' | wc -l)

if [ "$CRITICALS" -gt 0 ]; then
  echo "❌ Critical CVEs found: $CRITICALS"
  exit 1
fi
Also store:

aws s3 cp sbom.json s3://devsecops-staging-artifacts/$SERVICE/$BUILD_ID/

#!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail

VERSION="$1"
SERVICE="$2"

aws ecs update-service \
  --cluster notes-staging \
  --service "$SERVICE" \
  --force-new-deployment \
  --desired-count 2 \
  --deployment-configuration maximumPercent=200,minimumHealthyPercent=100

echo "✅ $SERVICE deployed version $VERSION to staging"
Integrate it only after successful scan.

ChatGPT said:
Understood — from here forward, every command or script in labs will come with:

Line-by-line breakdowns

What it does

Why it’s used

Its DevSecOps implications

Let’s take one of the key steps from the Challenge Lab and rewrite it fully explained:

🔍 Example from Trivy CVE Scan Enforcement
Original:
CRITICALS=$(trivy image --severity CRITICAL --exit-code 0 --format json -o trivy.json $IMAGE_NAME | jq '.Results[].Vulnerabilities[] | select(.Severity=="CRITICAL")' | wc -l)

if [ "$CRITICALS" -gt 0 ]; then
  echo "❌ Critical CVEs found: $CRITICALS"
  exit 1
fi
🔍 Annotated Version:
# Step 1: Run a Trivy vulnerability scan on the Docker image with severity filter
trivy image \
  --severity CRITICAL \           # Only look for CRITICAL vulnerabilities
  --exit-code 0 \                 # Don't exit with error yet — we’ll control that logic
  --format json \                 # Output in machine-readable JSON
  -o trivy.json \                 # Save results to a file
  "$IMAGE_NAME"                   # Docker image name passed as a variable
Why:
You want to run a scan on the container image you're about to deploy. But rather than failing automatically, you’ll parse the results and decide when to block the pipeline based on real thresholds.

# Step 2: Count the number of CRITICAL vulnerabilities using jq
CRITICALS=$(jq '.Results[].Vulnerabilities[] | select(.Severity=="CRITICAL")' trivy.json | wc -l)
What this does:

jq parses the JSON output file from Trivy

It filters to find all CVEs marked as "Severity": "CRITICAL"

wc -l counts how many matches are found

# Step 3: Enforce policy - block the build if CRITICAL vulnerabilities are found
if [ "$CRITICALS" -gt 0 ]; then
  echo "❌ Critical CVEs found: $CRITICALS"
  exit 1
fi
Why:
This is your DevSecOps policy gate. If any critical issues are found, the build fails immediately — preventing the image from progressing to ECS.

DevSecOps Impact:
This is shift-left security in practice — blocking known vulnerabilities before they hit runtime.

🔐 IAM for Secure CodePipeline & CodeBuild (Challenge Lab)
🎯 What We’re Securing
Your pipeline handles:

Secret fetching

SBOM generation

Artifact uploading

Staging deployments to ECS

Each CodeBuild project and CodePipeline stage must use principle of least privilege — only the minimum actions on the minimum resources needed.

📁 IAM Role: CodeBuildAuthServiceRole
Let’s create a secure IAM role for the auth-service CodeBuild job.

✅ CloudFormation Snippet (Annotated)
Resources:
  CodeBuildAuthServiceRole:
    Type: AWS::IAM::Role
    Properties:
      RoleName: codebuild-auth-service-role
      AssumeRolePolicyDocument:
        Version: "2012-10-17"
        Statement:
          - Effect: Allow
            Principal:
              Service: codebuild.amazonaws.com     # Only CodeBuild can assume this role
            Action: sts:AssumeRole

      Policies:
        - PolicyName: AuthServiceBuildPolicy
          PolicyDocument:
            Version: "2012-10-17"
            Statement:
              # ✅ Fetch only required secrets
              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - secretsmanager:GetSecretValue
                Resource:
                  - arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:111111111111:secret:auth-service/db*
              
              # ✅ Read-only access to specific SSM parameters
              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - ssm:GetParameter
                Resource:
                  - arn:aws:ssm:us-east-1:111111111111:parameter/auth-service/*

              # ✅ Allow SBOM/artifact upload only to auth-service bucket path
              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - s3:PutObject
                Resource:
                  - arn:aws:s3:::devsecops-staging-artifacts/auth-service/*
              
              # ✅ Allow Trivy to pull base images (if needed)
              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - ecr:GetAuthorizationToken
                  - ecr:BatchGetImage
                  - ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer
                Resource: "*"
🔍 What Each Block Does:
Block	Why It Exists
secretsmanager:GetSecretValue	Allows CodeBuild to securely inject secrets from Secrets Manager
ssm:GetParameter	Fetch secure config (e.g. feature flags, env settings) from Parameter Store
s3:PutObject	Upload scan results, SBOM, logs, and artifacts to service-specific S3 paths
ecr:*	Lets Trivy or CodeBuild pull base images for scanning or builds
🔐 Least Privilege Enforcement
This IAM role cannot deploy, read from other services' S3 paths, or fetch unrelated secrets

If compromised, the blast radius is isolated to one build job

Enforces one IAM role per service per CodeBuild

📁 IAM Role: CodePipelineServiceRole
This is the role used by the pipeline orchestration, not the builds.

  CodePipelineRole:
    Type: AWS::IAM::Role
    Properties:
      AssumeRolePolicyDocument:
        Statement:
          - Effect: Allow
            Principal:
              Service: codepipeline.amazonaws.com
            Action: sts:AssumeRole
      Policies:
        - PolicyName: CodePipelineAccess
          PolicyDocument:
            Statement:
              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - codebuild:StartBuild
                  - codebuild:BatchGetBuilds
                Resource: "*"

              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - s3:GetObject
                  - s3:PutObject
                Resource:
                  - arn:aws:s3:::devsecops-staging-artifacts/*

              - Effect: Allow
                Action:
                  - ecs:UpdateService
                  - ecs:DescribeServices
                Resource: "*"
🧠 DevSecOps Security Practices Recap
Practice	Explanation
🔐 Per-service IAM roles	Limits blast radius of misconfig or compromise
🔍 Explicit S3 path policies	Prevents one service from writing into another's directory
✅ Secrets pulled securely at runtime	No secrets in source code or buildspec
🔒 No deploy access in CodeBuild role	Prevents lateral movement via build jobs
📜 IAM policies as code (YAML/CDK)	Auditable and trackable via Git

You’ve now secured:

CodeBuild execution with minimum permissions

Artifact storage per service

Pipeline operations like starting builds and deployments

📁 Week7/Security.md
Title: CI/CD Pipeline Security – AWS CodePipeline & CodeBuild Hardening

🔐 Why Pipeline Security Matters in DevSecOps
CI/CD pipelines are high-value targets. If compromised, they can:

Deploy malware directly to production

Leak credentials or API keys to public logs

Skip critical vulnerability checks

Allow lateral movement across services

This week, we focused on building secure, least-privilege, auditable pipelines that minimize these risks.

🔍 What We Secured
Area	Security Mechanism
Secrets	Pulled at runtime from Secrets Manager or SSM, never stored in repo or logs
IAM Roles	Scoped per service and per stage — CodeBuild and CodePipeline have isolated permissions
Scan Enforcement	Pipeline blocks deployment if critical CVEs are detected via Trivy
SBOM & Artifacts	Stored in S3 per-service with versioning and path restrictions
Deployment	Controlled by post-scan approval stage, and run via secure scripts only
Logging	Enabled CloudWatch logs for build/test/scan/deploy stages
Notification (optional)	Alerting on pipeline failures via SNS or Slack for visibility
💣 Real-World CI/CD Threats Prevented
Threat	Mitigation in This Week
🔓 Leaked secrets in logs	Secrets pulled securely using secretsmanager:GetSecretValue
🎯 Compromised build container	IAM role scoped only to what it needs (no deploy access)
🚫 Unscanned image deployed	Trivy enforced via buildspec with fail-on-CVE gate
🎭 Cross-service artifact poisoning	S3 bucket write paths scoped to auth-service/ or notes-service/ only
🧪 Bypassed approvals	Manual or scan-based gate required before deploy
🪓 Lateral movement	CodeBuild cannot affect other builds or deploy other services
🕵️‍♀️ Lack of traceability	All events logged and tracked via CloudWatch + build logs
🔑 IAM Best Practices Used
✅ Per-service IAM Role Scoping
Each CodeBuild job gets its own IAM role, limited to:

Reading only its own secrets

Uploading its own artifacts

Pulling images from ECR (read-only)

🧠 This prevents one pipeline from being used to attack another service.

✅ Stage-specific Privileges
CodePipeline and CodeBuild have separate IAM roles:

codebuild-auth-service-role → fetch secrets, scan images, upload SBOM

codepipeline-master-role → orchestrate pipeline stages, trigger deploys

✅ No Wildcard Secrets Access
You should avoid policies like:

"Resource": "*"
Instead, use:

"Resource": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:111111111111:secret:auth-service/db*"
This restricts to only secrets tagged to auth-service.

🔐 Secrets Security Deep Dive
Technique	Why It Matters
AWS Secrets Manager	Provides versioning, rotation, encryption-at-rest
buildspec secrets-manager: syntax	Ensures secrets are never stored in plaintext on disk
Environment variable handling	Scripts must avoid echo $SECRET or writing secrets to logs
.env.example and dotenv validation	Used only for local development, not production builds
📦 SBOM, Trivy, and Artifact Hardening
✅ SBOM Artifacts
Generated per build using Trivy in cyclonedx format

Converted to HTML via custom Python

Uploaded to:

s3://devsecops-staging-artifacts/notes-service/{build-id}/sbom.json
🛡️ All files stored securely and versioned for audit purposes.

✅ CVE Scan Enforcement
Trivy exits with status code if any CRITICAL CVEs are found:

if [ "$CRITICALS" -gt 0 ]; then
  echo "❌ Blocking deploy due to critical vulnerabilities"
  exit 1
fi
✅ This enforces security policy as code — your pipeline won’t silently deploy vulnerable containers.

🔍 Logging & Observability
CodeBuild sends logs to CloudWatch Logs

You can subscribe to these logs for:

Anomaly detection

Post-mortem analysis

Integration into GuardDuty or SIEMs

🧠 DevSecOps Concepts Applied
Principle	Implementation
🔐 Least privilege	IAM roles scoped to service, stage, and action
🧱 Defense in depth	Secrets, scans, deploys, and IAM all enforced independently
🧪 Shift-left testing	Vulnerabilities are scanned before deploy
📜 Security as code	IAM, policies, buildspecs all codified in Git
📦 Software supply chain security	SBOMs generated, stored, and versioned for every build
✅ Your Security Wins This Week
CI/CD builds and deploys are now gated by security

Each microservice is isolated from others in terms of permissions

Every pipeline action is auditable and enforceable

Security failures are loud, visible, and logged

Title: Reflection – CI/CD Security with AWS CodePipeline & CodeBuild

🧠 What We Did
This week, we deepened our CI/CD pipeline capabilities by moving beyond GitHub Actions and GitLab CI to use AWS-native tools:

AWS CodePipeline for orchestration

AWS CodeBuild for building, testing, scanning

Integrated secure deploys to ECS staging

Enforced security gates using Trivy, SBOMs, and IAM isolation

🔍 Key Takeaways
1. Pipeline Security Is Non-Negotiable
Pipelines are the delivery engine for all software — and therefore a critical attack surface.
If they’re not secured:

You can accidentally ship malware

Secrets can leak into build logs

CVEs can go unaddressed into production

✅ This week we built pipelines that enforce security as a first-class citizen.

2. IAM Isolation Prevents Cross-Service Attacks
Giving each CodeBuild job its own scoped IAM role:

Limits the impact of any misbehavior or compromise

Prevents one service from accessing another’s secrets, buckets, or deploy targets

Makes it easier to audit and rotate credentials

Least privilege at the build stage is just as critical as in runtime.

3. Automated CVE Scanning Must Block Deployment
By integrating Trivy and scan enforcement logic:

You prevent vulnerable images from being deployed

Developers are alerted early — shift-left security in action

Every build outputs a machine-readable and human-readable SBOM

SBOMs + scan results = proof of software supply chain due diligence.

4. Security Must Be Continuous, Not a Checkbox
Each part of the pipeline enforces security:

Buildspec verifies dependencies and code quality

Trivy checks images for vulnerabilities

IAM roles restrict build context

SBOM is archived per build

Only passing builds are deployed

Security isn’t a final step — it’s woven into the CI/CD lifecycle.

🔄 What We Would Improve
Area	Opportunity
Deployment flexibility	Could introduce manual approvals, version pinning, and rollback support
Multi-account setup	Next step is to separate pipelines into dev/staging/prod accounts
Notifications	Slack/SNS/Teams alerts would improve developer feedback loop
Cost optimization	Some builds could be batched or containerized to reduce execution time

Principle	Application
🔐 Least Privilege	IAM per service and per stage
🧱 Defense in Depth	Secure secrets, scan enforcement, deploy verification
🧪 Shift-Left Security	Vulnerabilities caught during build phase
📜 Security as Code	IAM, policies, buildspec, deploy logic all version-controlled
📦 Supply Chain Security	SBOMs and scan logs stored for auditability

We’ll ensure secrets are:

Encrypted at rest and in transit

Never committed to source

📁 Week8/Overview.md
Title: Secrets Management with Vault, SOPS, and Sealed Secrets

🎯 Objective
In Week 8, we shift our attention to protecting application secrets — API keys, database credentials, tokens, and sensitive configuration. You’ll learn how to securely manage secrets across environments using:

🔐 HashiCorp Vault for dynamic secrets and runtime injection

🧾 SOPS for encrypting YAML/JSON files in Git

🔒 Bitnami Sealed Secrets for safe Kubernetes secret delivery

Your application will be refactored to consume secrets securely in both ECS and Kubernetes contexts.

🧠 Concepts & Theory
🔐 What Are Secrets?
In DevSecOps, secrets are sensitive values your app needs to operate:

Database connection strings

JWT private keys

API tokens (e.g., Stripe, SendGrid)

OAuth credentials

Encryption keys

Poor handling of secrets leads to:

💥 Data breaches

🪪 Identity spoofing

📦 Package tampering or repo poisoning

🔍 Secrets exposed in CI logs or Git history

🔐 Secret Management vs Secret Storage
Term	Description
Secret storage	Keeping secrets encrypted at rest (e.g. Secrets Manager)
Secret management	Includes lifecycle: rotation, access control, auditing, injection, and revocation
🧠 Why Store Secrets Securely in DevSecOps?
Secrets should never:

Be hardcoded in codebases

Be shared over Slack or email

Be visible in Git history

Be baked into Docker images

Instead, secrets should:

Be encrypted in Git (SOPS)

Be injected securely at runtime (Vault, SSM)

Be auditable and revocable

Follow least access policies

📚 Tool Comparison
Tool	Use Case	Strength
Vault	Dynamic secrets, identity-based access	Rotation, short-lived creds
SOPS	Encrypted config in Git	GitOps-friendly, PGP/KMS support
Sealed Secrets	Kubernetes-safe secret delivery	Git-compatible, encrypted secrets in YAML
AWS SSM / Secrets Manager	Native secrets for AWS infra	IAM integration, lifecycle support
🧩 This Week’s App Evolution
Your auth-service and notes-service will be refactored to:

🧊 Remove .env files in source control

🔐 Encrypt config files with SOPS

🧬 Load secrets dynamically from Vault (or SSM for ECS)

🗝️ Use Sealed Secrets for Kubernetes clusters

🔍 Use CI/CD to verify that secrets are never committed

🔄 App Refactor Summary
Before	After
.env in repo	.env moved to .sops.env.yaml encrypted
Dockerfile builds with secrets inside	Dockerfile reads secrets from env at runtime
Static secrets per environment	Vault provides short-lived dynamic secrets
K8s secrets in plain YAML	Encrypted with kubeseal for Git delivery
🛠️ Tools You'll Use This Week
Tool	Purpose
Vault	Secrets injection, identity-based access
SOPS	Git-friendly encryption for YAML/JSON env files
kubeseal	Encrypt K8s secrets for Git
dotenv-linter	Detect exposed secrets in code
truffleHog	Scan for hardcoded secrets
age or GPG	Key backends for SOPS encryption
🔐 DevSecOps Principles Reinforced
Principle	Applied As
🔐 Confidentiality by Design	Secrets stored encrypted at rest and in transit
🧱 Defense in Depth	Vault, SOPS, and sealed secrets layered for safety
🧪 Shift-left detection	Scan codebases for committed secrets during CI
🔍 Auditability & Revocation	Vault usage and SOPS keys are auditable
📜 Security as Code	Secrets are managed with Git-friendly workflows
