📁 Week8/GuidedLab.md
Title: Guided Lab – Secure Secrets Management in ECS and Kubernetes

🎯 Lab Objectives
In this guided lab, you'll:

🔐 Set up SOPS to encrypt .env or .yaml secrets for Git

🧪 Integrate TruffleHog into your pipeline to detect leaked secrets

⚙️ Configure Vault to dynamically generate secrets for services

🧊 Inject secrets securely in ECS (via SSM) and Kubernetes (via Sealed Secrets)

🔁 Refactor your auth-service and notes-service to load secrets securely at runtime

🛠️ Lab Prerequisites
AWS CLI & IAM permissions

Vault installed (brew install vault)

SOPS installed (brew install sops)

Age or GPG for encryption keys

Kubernetes cluster (for Sealed Secrets track)

ECS Cluster (for SSM/Vault track)

🧪 Part 1: Detect Existing Secrets in Codebase
🔍 1.1 Scan repo with TruffleHog
trufflehog filesystem --directory . --only-verified
What it does:

Scans your repo for high-entropy strings and known secret formats (e.g. AWS keys, Slack tokens)

--only-verified reduces false positives

❗ If any secrets are found, rotate them immediately and remove them from Git history using git filter-repo or BFG.

🔐 Part 2: Encrypt Secrets with SOPS (Git-Compatible)
🔑 2.1 Generate an encryption key (Age backend recommended)
age-keygen -o ~/.config/sops/age/keys.txt
Then add the public portion to your .sops.yaml config.

🗂️ 2.2 Create .sops.yaml config in your repo root
creation_rules:
  - path_regex: .*\.sops\.(yaml|json|env)$
    encrypted_regex: ^(data|stringData)$
    age: [age1qk6n3zrx...your_public_key_here]
    encrypted_suffix: .sops
This tells SOPS to auto-encrypt any file matching .sops.yaml, .sops.env, etc.

🔐 2.3 Encrypt your environment file
cp .env staging.sops.env
sops --encrypt --in-place staging.sops.env
What this does:

Encrypts secrets like DB passwords and API keys

Keeps file in Git, safely encrypted

Can be decrypted by CI/CD or local dev with your private key

🧪 2.4 Decrypt it securely during CI/CD (GitHub/GitLab)
sops --decrypt staging.sops.env > .env
Add this to your buildspec.yml or pipeline YAML for runtime use.

🧱 Part 3: Setup Vault for Dynamic Secrets
⚙️ 3.1 Start Dev Vault (for local test)
vault server -dev
For real infra, use Vault with AWS KMS auto-unseal in HA mode.

🔐 3.2 Enable KV and generate sample secrets
vault kv enable -path=secret kv-v2
vault kv put secret/auth-service DB_USER=svc_auth DB_PASS=topsecret123
🧬 3.3 Refactor Node.js backend to load secrets
Install Vault SDK or use a wrapper like node-vault:

npm install node-vault dotenv
Update config/secrets.js:

const vault = require("node-vault")({
  endpoint: process.env.VAULT_ADDR,
  token: process.env.VAULT_TOKEN
});

module.exports = async () => {
  const secrets = await vault.read("secret/data/auth-service");
  process.env.DB_USER = secrets.data.data.DB_USER;
  process.env.DB_PASS = secrets.data.data.DB_PASS;
};
🛡️ Part 4: ECS Integration (SSM or Vault)
✅ 4.1 Store secrets in Parameter Store (ECS-compatible)
aws ssm put-parameter \
  --name "/auth-service/db_user" \
  --type "SecureString" \
  --value "svc_auth" \
  --overwrite
Repeat for all secrets.

🔐 4.2 Attach IAM Role to ECS Task for Secure Fetch
IAM Policy:

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["ssm:GetParameter"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:ssm:us-east-1:123456789012:parameter/auth-service/*"
    }
  ]
}
Then, your container can fetch it using AWS SDK or env injectors.

🧩 Part 5: Kubernetes Integration (Sealed Secrets)
🔐 5.1 Install kubeseal CLI and controller
kubectl apply -f https://github.com/bitnami-labs/sealed-secrets/releases/download/v0.24.1/controller.yaml
🧾 5.2 Create and seal a secret
kubectl create secret generic auth-secret \
  --from-literal=DB_USER=svc_auth \
  --dry-run=client -o json > auth-secret.json

kubeseal --controller-namespace kube-system --format yaml < auth-secret.json > auth-sealed.yaml
Now you can safely commit auth-sealed.yaml to Git.

✅ Completion Checklist
 Secrets removed from .env and Git

 Encrypted secrets committed with SOPS

 Vault and/or SSM integrated for runtime secret access

 ECS task IAM roles scoped to secret path

 K8s secrets delivered via Sealed Secrets

 CI/CD scans for leaked secrets

CI/CD enforcement of TruffleHog and SOPS checks

Multi-service secret injection across namespaces and environments

📁 Week8/ChallengeLab.md
Title: Challenge Lab – End-to-End Secrets Hardening and Incident Response

🎯 Challenge Objective
You are tasked with simulating a real-world DevSecOps incident:

A developer has accidentally committed a secret to Git. You must:

Detect and rotate the leaked secret

Remove it from Git history

Integrate secret scanning into your pipeline

Migrate the secret into a secure management system (Vault or SOPS)

Prove that secrets are never leaked again

🧪 Scenario Setup
Your repo has a .env file with:

STRIPE_SECRET=sk_test_leaked123456
The .env was accidentally committed and pushed to GitHub/GitLab

CI/CD must now fail the build if this happens again

🛠️ Step 1: Simulate the Secret Leak
echo "STRIPE_SECRET=sk_test_leaked123456" > .env
git add .env && git commit -m "oops: added secret"
git push origin main
🚨 The secret is now in your public or private Git repo. This is a high-risk event.

🧪 Step 2: Detect the Secret in Git History
Run:

trufflehog git file://. --only-verified
You should see something like:

Found potential secret in file .env
Reason: High Entropy String
Verified: True
✅ This simulates your CI/CD pipeline catching the error before deployment.

🧽 Step 3: Remove the Secret from Git History
Option 1: With git filter-repo (recommended)

git filter-repo --path .env --invert-paths
Then:

git push --force
Option 2: With BFG Repo-Cleaner

bfg --delete-files .env
git reflog expire --expire=now --all && git gc --prune=now --aggressive
git push --force
⚠️ Everyone who cloned the repo must re-clone after this.

🔐 Step 4: Rotate the Secret (Critical)
If it was a real Stripe key, you’d rotate it immediately.

Example:

aws secretsmanager update-secret \
  --secret-id stripe-secret \
  --secret-string "sk_test_rotated456789"
Update Vault or SSM as well if you migrated the key.

🔏 Step 5: Migrate to Secure Storage
Option A: Use Vault

vault kv put secret/stripe STRIPE_SECRET=sk_test_rotated456789
Option B: Encrypt with SOPS

echo 'STRIPE_SECRET=sk_test_rotated456789' > stripe.sops.env
sops --encrypt --in-place stripe.sops.env
🔒 Step 6: Integrate Secret Scanning in CI
Add this to your GitLab .gitlab-ci.yml or GitHub Actions workflow:

- name: TruffleHog Secret Scan
  run: |
    trufflehog filesystem --directory . --only-verified
✅ Fails pipeline if secrets are committed again.

✅ Bonus: Add Git Hook to Prevent Future Leaks
.git/hooks/pre-commit:

#!/bin/bash
trufflehog filesystem --directory . --only-verified
if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then
  echo "❌ Potential secret detected. Commit aborted."
  exit 1
fi
Make executable:

chmod +x .git/hooks/pre-commit
📦 Bonus: Inject Secrets Across Multiple Services
Vault path structure:

secret/
  auth-service/
    DB_PASS=...
  notes-service/
    DB_PASS=...
ECS task roles:

auth-task-role → ssm:GetParameter on /auth-service/*

notes-task-role → /notes-service/*

Kubernetes sealed secrets for each namespace:

auth-namespace

notes-namespace

✅ Secrets are scoped by service AND environment, with zero shared credentials.

🧪 Completion Checklist
 Simulated leaked secret in Git

 Detected with TruffleHog

 Removed secret from Git history

 Rotated secret in Vault or AWS

 Migrated to secure secret storage

 Added CI pipeline secret scanning

 Created Git pre-commit hook

 Scoped secret access by service and namespace

📁 Week8/Security.md
Title: Security – Secrets Management Across the App Lifecycle

🔐 Why Secrets Management Matters
Secrets are the keys to your kingdom — if an attacker gets access:

They can exfiltrate data

Deploy unauthorized services

Laterally move between services

Trigger billing spikes, identity abuse, and data loss

In DevSecOps, secrets management is non-optional. We secure secrets across:

🧱 Infrastructure (databases, IAM)

📦 Codebases (API keys, service tokens)

🧪 Pipelines (build tools, scanners)

📲 Runtime (task roles, injected environment variables)

🧩 How the Application Evolved
🔄 Refactored Configuration
Previous	Now
.env in Git	Replaced with staging.sops.env encrypted via SOPS
Hardcoded process.env.*	Pulled from Vault or AWS Parameter Store
Shared config between services	Secrets scoped per service and environment
Docker builds with secrets baked in	Secrets injected securely at runtime
✅ Each service now consumes secrets securely, without knowing about other services.

🧬 Secrets per Service & Environment
Service	Environment	Storage	Delivery
auth-service	dev	SOPS + .env	Decrypted in dev container
auth-service	staging	Vault	Pulled via SDK
auth-service	prod	SSM Parameter Store	Injected via ECS task role
notes-service	dev	SOPS	Same flow
notes-service	staging	Vault	Same flow
notes-service	prod	K8s Sealed Secrets	Encrypted YAML deployed from Git
🔐 Vault Security Practices Applied
Control	Benefit
Policy-per-path	Prevents cross-service secret access
Short-lived tokens	Limits blast radius of token leaks
Token auth via CI/CD	CI pipelines authenticate and pull secrets only when building
KV v2 versioning	Track and roll back secrets if needed
🧠 For production, Vault should be auto-unsealed with AWS KMS and monitored with audit logs.

📁 SOPS Git Hygiene
Practice	Benefit
.sops.yaml in repo	Automatic enforcement of encrypted formats
Only .sops.env and .sops.yaml committed	Prevents accidental leakage of raw .env files
git-secrets hook + TruffleHog	Prevents commits of plaintext secrets
Encryption keys managed via Age or KMS	Git-agnostic and CI-compatible
🔐 Secrets are versioned and encrypted, but never accessible in plaintext from Git.

🛡️ ECS and Kubernetes Runtime Isolation
✅ ECS Tasks (Fargate)
IAM roles scoped per task definition

Secure string injection from SSM

App loads secrets at runtime using AWS SDK

No shared environment variables between services

✅ Kubernetes (K8s)
Secrets created as Sealed Secrets

Decrypted only by Sealed Secrets controller inside cluster

Services access only their sealed secret via namespace restriction

🚨 Secrets Lifecycle Risks Mitigated
Threat	Mitigation
Committing .env to Git	SOPS with .sops.env, .gitignore, and CI scan
Long-lived credentials	Vault dynamic secrets & expiring tokens
Cross-env access	Vault policies and IAM scoping per env
Leaked secrets in logs	Strict env var handling and CI linting
Secrets exposed in CI/CD	Pulled at runtime, not stored in buildspec or pipeline YAML
🧪 Secrets Enforcement in CI/CD
Tool	What It Does
TruffleHog	Scans committed code and history for secrets
dotenv-linter	Validates .env files don’t leak keys
SOPS CI step	Ensures encrypted files remain valid
Git hooks	Blocks bad commits at the source
SBOM tagging	Tracks secrets usage as part of the software bill of materials
📜 DevSecOps Principles Applied
Principle	Implementation
🔐 Least privilege	Secrets scoped per service, role, and namespace
📦 Confidentiality	Secrets encrypted with SOPS and sealed
🧪 Shift-left security	TruffleHog and dotenv-linter in pre-commit and CI
🔁 Auditable secrets	Vault KV versioning and encrypted Git history
🧱 Defense in depth	Secrets secure at rest, in transit, and runtime
✅ Security Wins This Week
🔐 No secrets committed to Git (enforced by hook + CI)

📁 Secrets stored encrypted per service/environment

⚙️ App evolved to dynamically consume secrets at runtime

📦 ECS and Kubernetes integrated securely

🧪 Secrets rotated and revocable

📜 Everything traceable and auditable

📁 Week8/Reflection.md
Title: Reflection – Secrets Management in DevSecOps

🧠 What We Did
This week, we tackled one of the most critical parts of DevSecOps: securing application secrets across the full software lifecycle. We:

🔐 Removed all plaintext secrets from our codebase

🧾 Used SOPS to encrypt secrets in Git for dev/staging

⚙️ Set up Vault for dynamic secrets in staging and prod

🛡️ Enforced IAM-scoped secret access in ECS

🔒 Integrated Sealed Secrets for Kubernetes

🧪 Added TruffleHog secret detection to CI/CD

🔁 Simulated a real-world leaked secret incident and recovery

🔍 Key Takeaways
1. Secrets Belong Outside the Codebase
Secrets should never live in .env or .yaml files inside a Git repo.

Instead, we now:

Encrypt secrets with SOPS for Git compatibility

Store runtime secrets in Vault or SSM

Inject them securely via CI/CD or runtime SDK

2. Dynamic Secrets are the Gold Standard
Vault provides:

Rotating credentials

Short TTLs (time-to-live)

Auditable access

Scoped permissions

This reduces the risk of long-lived credential abuse.

3. CI/CD Must Enforce Secret Hygiene
By using tools like:

trufflehog – secret detection in commit history

Git pre-commit hooks – early prevention

SOPS – Git-friendly secret encryption

We’ve built pipelines that fail fast and enforce clean repos.

4. App Now Adapts to Secure Config Loading
Before	After
Secrets hardcoded in .env	Pulled at runtime from Vault or SSM
Shared env vars	Scoped per service/environment
Secrets in build images	Injected securely at runtime
No tracking of secret changes	Full audit/version history via Vault
5. Sealed Secrets Make GitOps Safe on Kubernetes
We now use:

Encrypted K8s secrets

GitOps-compatible YAML files

Key rotation and public key sealing

Git is now safe for config and secret deployment.

🔄 What We Would Improve
Area	Opportunity
Vault production deployment	Should be HA, auto-unsealed, and monitored
Secrets rotation policy	Could add automatic expiration and alerts
Secret key lifecycle	Include rotation calendar and role-based approval
GitHub/GitLab secret scanners	Could integrate GitHub Advanced Security or GitLab Secrets Detection
🧩 DevSecOps Principles Reinforced
Principle	How It Was Applied
🔐 Confidentiality by Default	Encrypted secrets at rest and in transit
🧪 Shift-Left	TruffleHog in pre-commit and pipeline
🧱 Defense in Depth	SOPS + Vault + Sealed Secrets
🧾 Auditability	Vault versioning, CI logs, Git tracking
🧬 Scoping & Least Privilege	IAM per service/env, Vault policies, no shared secrets

📁 Week9/Overview.md
Title: Container Image Security & SBOMs

🎯 Objective
In Week 9, we focus on securing the containers your app runs in. You’ll learn how to:

Scan container images for vulnerabilities using Trivy, Grype, and Dockle

Generate and publish Software Bill of Materials (SBOMs)

Identify and mitigate risks in your base image and dependencies

Integrate security scans into CI/CD pipelines

Evolve your microservices to enforce image signing, immutable tags, and zero trust at the container level

🧠 Concepts & Theory
🐳 Why Container Security Matters
Containers encapsulate the runtime of your application. If you ship a container with:

A vulnerable OpenSSL version

An outdated Node.js or Python package

An exposed SSH server or debug port
→ You’ve now deployed a vulnerable system at scale.

🧬 What Is an SBOM?
A Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) is a list of all software components, libraries, and dependencies in your application or container image.

Why it matters:

Helps identify vulnerable components quickly

Required for compliance (e.g., US Executive Order 14028)

Enables real-time monitoring of security advisories

🔐 DevSecOps Goals This Week
Goal	Tool/Concept
Identify CVEs in base images	Trivy, Grype, Dockle
Create a container SBOM	Syft (CycloneDX, SPDX formats)
Block builds with critical vulnerabilities	GitLab/GitHub CI pipelines
Promote immutable image tags	Git SHA or version-tagged containers
Harden Dockerfiles	Minimal images, non-root users, COPY best practices
📦 App Evolution This Week
We’ll evolve the auth-service and notes-service Dockerfiles by:

🧼 Switching from node:alpine → distroless or scratch-based images (when possible)

🔐 Adding non-root users

📄 Generating SBOMs using syft and publishing them to the repo or artifact registry

🧪 Scanning container images during CI/CD

⛔ Blocking promotion of images with known CVEs

🔧 Tools You’ll Use This Week
Tool	Purpose
Trivy	Image and filesystem CVE scanner
Grype	High-fidelity CVE scanner
Dockle	Dockerfile linter (best practices, misconfigs)
Syft	SBOM generator
cosign	(optional) Sign and verify images
docker-slim	(optional) Minify image footprint
🛡️ DevSecOps Principles Reinforced
Principle	Applied As
🧱 Defense in Depth	Scanning every layer of the image and dependencies
🔍 Shift-left security	CVE scans before pushing images
📜 Auditability	SBOMs provide traceable component lists
📦 Immutable infrastructure	Use of image digests, not mutable tags
🐳 Supply chain trust	Signed, scanned, and reviewed container builds
📄 Deliverables This Week
Hardened Dockerfiles for all services

SBOMs committed to Git or uploaded to container registry

CVE scan logs integrated into CI

Critical CVEs blocked at build time

Optional: Signed container images

📁 Week9/GuidedLab.md
Title: Guided Lab – Scanning Containers and Generating SBOMs

🎯 Lab Objectives
By the end of this lab, you'll:

🔍 Scan Docker images with Trivy, Grype, and Dockle

📄 Generate and review SBOMs with Syft

🛡️ Harden Dockerfiles for both services

🧪 Integrate security scans and SBOMs into CI/CD pipelines

⛔ Block vulnerable images from reaching production

🧱 App Context
You will work with:

auth-service

notes-service

These services currently use standard node:alpine images and basic Dockerfiles. We will:

Replace the base image with hardened options

Scan for vulnerabilities in packages and base images

Produce SBOMs in CycloneDX/SPDX format

Commit SBOMs alongside the app for traceability

📦 Step 1: Scan Docker Images Locally
🔍 1.1 Install Scanners
brew install aquasecurity/trivy/trivy
brew install anchore/grype/grype
brew install goodwithtech/dockle/dockle
🐳 1.2 Build Your Images
cd auth-service
docker build -t auth-service:dev .

cd ../notes-service
docker build -t notes-service:dev .
🧪 1.3 Scan with Trivy
trivy image auth-service:dev
trivy image notes-service:dev
Output highlights:

Lists OS and app CVEs

Marks critical/high severity issues

Recommends fixed versions

🧪 1.4 Scan with Grype (for comparison)
grype auth-service:dev
🧽 1.5 Lint Dockerfiles with Dockle
dockle auth-service:dev
Check for:

Use of root user

Missing health checks

Exposed package managers (e.g. apt, apk left installed)

📄 Step 2: Generate an SBOM with Syft
🧰 2.1 Install Syft
brew install syft
📄 2.2 Generate SBOM for auth-service
syft auth-service:dev -o cyclonedx-json > sbom-auth-service.json
Other formats:

syft . -o table

-o spdx-json for SPDX support

🧬 2.3 Commit SBOMs to Git
mkdir -p sbom
mv sbom-auth-service.json sbom/
🔒 Commit SBOMs with every tagged release for full supply chain traceability.

🛠️ Step 3: Harden Dockerfiles
🧼 3.1 Before: Sample auth-service/Dockerfile
FROM node:alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY . .
RUN npm install
CMD ["npm", "start"]
🔐 3.2 After: Hardened Dockerfile
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev
COPY . .

# Runtime image
FROM node:20-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /app /app

# Drop privileges
RUN addgroup -S app && adduser -S app -G app
USER app

CMD ["node", "index.js"]
Improvements:

Multi-stage builds

Drops root user

Uses npm ci for deterministic builds

🔁 Step 4: Integrate Scanning into CI/CD
✅ GitHub Actions (example)
jobs:
  scan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3

      - name: Build Docker image
        run: docker build -t auth-service:dev .

      - name: Trivy Image Scan
        uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
        with:
          image-ref: auth-service:dev
          format: table
          exit-code: 1
          severity: CRITICAL,HIGH

      - name: Generate SBOM
        run: |
          syft auth-service:dev -o cyclonedx-json > sbom-auth-service.json
❗ exit-code: 1 fails the pipeline if vulnerabilities are found.

✅ GitLab CI (example)
scan_image:
  image: docker:latest
  services:
    - docker:dind
  before_script:
    - apk add --no-cache curl
    - curl -sSfL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aquasecurity/trivy/main/contrib/install.sh | sh
  script:
    - docker build -t auth-service:dev .
    - ./trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL,HIGH auth-service:dev
✅ Completion Checklist
 Scanned container images with Trivy, Grype, Dockle

 Hardened Dockerfiles with least-privilege users and best practices

 Generated SBOMs using Syft

 Committed SBOMs to version control

 Integrated scans into CI/CD pipelines

 Blocked builds with critical vulnerabilities

📁 Week9/ChallengeLab.md
Title: Challenge Lab – Supply Chain Attack Simulation and SBOM Enforcement

🎯 Scenario
You are responsible for maintaining the auth-service. A developer adds a dependency that includes a known critical CVE. Your job is to:

Detect the vulnerable image

Block it from being pushed to production

Generate and attach an SBOM to the image

Automate this as part of CI/CD

Document the SBOM in your Git repository

🧪 Step 1: Inject a Known Vulnerability
Inside auth-service, modify package.json:

"dependencies": {
  "express": "4.17.1",  // includes a known vulnerability
  ...
}
Now rebuild the image:

docker build -t auth-service:vuln .
🔍 Step 2: Scan the Image and Detect the CVE
trivy image auth-service:vuln
Look for:

CVE-2022-24999 | express@4.17.1 | High
Optionally verify with:

grype auth-service:vuln
✅ Your scanner should flag the CVE and suggest upgrading.

⛔ Step 3: Block the Vulnerable Image in CI/CD
Update your pipeline to fail if critical/high CVEs are found.

Example GitHub Actions:

- name: Trivy Image Scan
  uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
  with:
    image-ref: auth-service:vuln
    format: table
    exit-code: 1
    severity: CRITICAL,HIGH
Run CI: ✅ it should fail the pipeline due to the CVE.

📦 Step 4: Generate and Attach SBOM
Generate SBOM:

syft auth-service:vuln -o cyclonedx-json > sbom-auth-service.json
Push it to Git:

mkdir -p sbom && mv sbom-auth-service.json sbom/
git add sbom/sbom-auth-service.json
git commit -m "Add SBOM for auth-service:vuln"
📦 You now have a full manifest of every package in the container — required for auditing.

🔁 Step 5: Automate SBOM with Every Release
Update your CI/CD to generate and publish SBOMs:

- name: Generate SBOM
  run: |
    syft auth-service:latest -o cyclonedx-json > sbom/sbom-auth-service-latest.json
- name: Commit SBOM (GitHub example)
  run: |
    git config user.email "bot@company.com"
    git config user.name "CI Bot"
    git add sbom/
    git commit -m "Auto-generated SBOM for auth-service"
    git push
🔒 Bonus: Sign Your Container Image (Optional)
cosign sign --key cosign.key auth-service:latest
Then verify:

cosign verify --key cosign.pub auth-service:latest
🔐 Signed containers + SBOMs = full software supply chain integrity.

🧳 Bonus Scenario: CVE Disclosure and Remediation
Simulate this flow:

Step	Action
🔍 CVE Found	Trivy flags express@4.17.1 as vulnerable
⛔ Block Build	CI fails the merge or deployment
🔁 Developer Fix	Updates to express@4.18.2
🧪 Rescan	CVE no longer detected
✅ CI Passes	Image now safe to deploy
📦 SBOM Updated	Syft regenerates new dependency list
✅ Challenge Checklist
 Introduced a CVE in a real image

 Detected it using Trivy or Grype

 Blocked the CI/CD pipeline on CVE presence

 Generated and committed SBOM

 Integrated SBOM generation into CI/CD

 (Optional) Signed image for tamper protection

📁 Week9/Security.md
Title: Security – Container Image Hardening & Supply Chain Defense

🎯 Why Container Image Security Matters
Every container image is a snapshot of your app’s runtime. If it contains:

📦 A vulnerable package

🔧 Misconfigured Dockerfile

🐚 A shell left behind

👤 Root user privileges
→ It becomes an easy attack vector.

Modern breaches often target the software supply chain. If you don’t know what’s inside your containers, you can’t protect them.

🔍 Real-World Breach Examples
Incident	Root Cause
SolarWinds	Compromised build pipeline shipped signed malware
Docker Hub Malicious Images	Official-looking images with crypto miners
Log4Shell CVE-2021-44228	Vulnerability buried in a nested dependency
PyTorch Dependency Hijack (2022)	Package dependency replaced with malware
🔐 Vulnerability Classes We Mitigated
Class	Example	Solution
OS CVEs	Alpine or Ubuntu base image issues	Used Trivy/Grype to scan base images
App Dependency CVEs	express@4.17.1	CI scans block critical packages
Bad Dockerfile Practices	USER root, ADD . .	Refactored Dockerfiles with best practices
Hidden files in builds	.env, SSH keys	Used .dockerignore, multi-stage builds
Mutable tags	node:latest	Switched to digests or versioned tags
🧱 Image Hardening Techniques
Practice	What It Does
🧼 Multi-stage builds	Removes build-time tools and secrets
🔐 Non-root user	Prevents privilege escalation
🧱 Minimal base image	Reduces attack surface (distroless, scratch)
📁 .dockerignore	Prevents leaking dev/test files
🧪 Health checks	Helps detect when something breaks in prod
📜 SBOM Security Impact
A Software Bill of Materials allows you to:

📦 Know exactly what’s in each image

🚨 Cross-reference CVEs as they’re disclosed

📁 Maintain traceability over time

✅ Prove compliance in regulated environments (FedRAMP, SOC2)

Without SBOMs, response to supply chain vulnerabilities is slow and incomplete.

🔁 Immutable Images & Trust Chains
Technique	Purpose
node:18.20.2-alpine instead of node:latest	Ensures deterministic builds
Image digests (sha256:...)	Guarantees content integrity
cosign signatures	Verifies publisher identity
SBOM attached to release	Documents what was shipped and why
🧠 These techniques enforce "You build it, you own it"—a core DevSecOps principle.

🔄 DevSecOps Principles Applied
Principle	Implementation
🔍 Shift-left scanning	CVE detection during build, not in prod
🧱 Immutable infrastructure	Images locked to specific tags and digests
📜 Auditability	SBOMs committed and versioned
🔒 Defense in Depth	Dockerfile best practices + scanner + CI gates
🔐 Software provenance	Optional cosign signature verifies image trust
🧪 CI/CD Enforcement (What We Added)
Enforcement	Tool
🔎 CVE scanner on image builds	Trivy, Grype
🚫 CI fails on critical/high CVEs	exit-code: 1 in CI job
📄 SBOM generation on push	Syft
🧾 SBOM committed to Git	Audit trail
(Optional) 🔏 Image signed before push	Cosign
🧬 App Architecture Changes
Each microservice now:

Uses a multi-stage, hardened Dockerfile

Builds are scanned and signed before push

Produces SBOMs with each release

CI/CD includes blocking policies for insecure builds

📦 Result: A secure build artifact pipeline for all services.

Title: Reflection – Supply Chain Security with Container Scanning and SBOMs

🧠 What We Did
This week, we moved from infrastructure security into software supply chain defense — one of the most targeted and overlooked attack surfaces in modern DevOps.

We:

🔍 Scanned Docker images using Trivy, Grype, and Dockle

📄 Generated and committed SBOMs (Software Bill of Materials)

🔍 Key Takeaways
1. If You Don’t Scan It, You Can’t Trust It
Our container images previously shipped unverified software stacks — including Node.js, Python dependencies, and system libraries.

Now:

We scan all layers (base image + app)

We fail the pipeline on high/critical CVEs

We publish SBOMs alongside each release

→ We know what we’re shipping, and can react to security advisories immediately.

2. Hardened Dockerfiles are Essential
Before:

Running as root

Using node:latest

No .dockerignore

No health checks

After:

Multi-stage builds

Minimal base images

Non-root users

Explicit COPY and entrypoints

Slim image size = smaller attack surface

3. SBOMs Create a Traceable Chain of Custody
We now generate SBOMs using syft in CI and store them:

In the Git repo alongside the release

Optionally in the container registry (as artifacts or annotations)

This lets us:

📜 Answer: “What was in that container?” 3 months later

⚠️ Cross-reference new CVEs against old releases

✅ Comply with supply chain regulations (e.g. EO 14028, FedRAMP)

4. The App Is Now Part of a Secure Software Factory
Our evolving microservices now:

Are built using CI jobs with security gates

Publish metadata (SBOMs, scan logs)

Enforce immutable tags and content verification

Are safer to release into production with minimal manual review

🔄 What We’d Improve

Image signing enforcement	Make signed images required in staging/prod
Vulnerability suppression workflow	Add a pipeline step for approved CVE ignores
SBOM storage	Push SBOMs to a registry like AWS ECR or GitHub Releases
Alerts on new CVEs	Integrate Trivy or Grype with Dependabot-style notifications
Docker base image audit schedule	Automatically rebuild images on a schedule to pick up patches

Principle	Example
🔐 Shift-left	Scan images during build, not in prod
🧾 Auditability	SBOMs + commit trail = proof of integrity
📦 Immutable Infrastructure	Enforced versioning, tag locking
🧱 Defense in Depth	CI scanners + hardened images

Here's the .md-formatted Overview.md for Week 10 – Infrastructure as Code (IaC) & Security.

📁 Week10/Overview.md
Title: IaC Fundamentals & Security with Terraform, CDK, CloudFormation, and SAM

🎯 Objective
In Week 10, you will:

Understand the foundational and advanced principles of Infrastructure as Code (IaC)

Explore the IaC tools powering modern DevSecOps pipelines: Terraform, AWS CDK, CloudFormation, and AWS SAM

Implement defensive infrastructure configurations

Scan IaC code for misconfigurations and CVEs

Detect drift, prevent privilege escalation, and enforce least privilege

Begin evolving the infrastructure behind your app (e.g. auth-service, notes-service) using IaC

🧠 Concepts & Theory
💡 What is Infrastructure as Code?
IaC is the practice of managing infrastructure (servers, networks, IAM, databases) using versioned, machine-readable definitions — just like code.

Core principles:

Principle	Description
🔁 Idempotence	Running IaC repeatedly produces the same result
📝 Versioning	Changes to infrastructure are tracked in Git
🔍 Reviewability	Infrastructure can be peer-reviewed via pull requests
✅ Automatable	CI/CD pipelines apply infrastructure with control gates
🧪 Testable	IaC can be statically analyzed and tested pre-deploy
🏗️ The Tools We'll Explore
Tool	Language	Use Case	Why It Matters
Terraform	HCL	Cloud-agnostic IaC	Most widely adopted IaC tool
AWS CDK	TypeScript/Python	AWS-native IaC using real code	Enables abstraction & reusable logic
CloudFormation	YAML/JSON	AWS-native IaC	Integrated with AWS services
SAM	YAML	IaC for serverless apps	Focused on Lambda/APIGW/DynamoDB
🔐 Why IaC Security Is Critical
When IaC goes wrong, it leads to:

Misconfiguration	Risk
Open 0.0.0.0/0 in security groups	Unauthorized external access
IAM roles with * permissions	Privilege escalation
Hardcoded secrets in Terraform	Credential leaks
Drifted resources	Inconsistent security posture
Untracked changes via console	Shadow infrastructure
🔧 Tools for Securing IaC
Tool	Purpose
tfsec / tflint	Terraform static analysis
checkov	Multi-tool IaC scanning (Terraform, CFN, CDK)
cfn-lint	CloudFormation best practices
cdk-nag	CDK security compliance checks
driftctl	Drift detection between declared vs real infra
aws-nuke	Cleanup unused or orphaned resources
📦 App Evolution This Week
Our secure note/task app will evolve as follows:

Component	IaC Change
Auth Service	VPC, ECS Service, IAM Role via Terraform & CDK
Secrets	Stored in AWS Secrets Manager via CDK or Terraform
CI/CD Infra	CodePipeline + CodeBuild via CDK
Audit Logging	Add CloudTrail + CloudWatch infra declarations
IAM Role Policies	Minimized permissions per service with CDK validation
Staging/UAT Isolation	Environment-based infra modeled and deployed via IaC
🧰 Key Features in AWS CDK
Feature	Why It Matters
Constructs	Reusable components for services (e.g. EcsService, Vpc)
Stacks	Group related resources (e.g., AppStack, NetworkStack)
Environments	Isolate dev/qa/prod infra programmatically
Aspects	Add rules like security checks (cdk-nag) across stacks
Outputs	Export values (e.g. ARNs, URLs) between stacks
Context	Pass shared config or global values into builds
🛡️ DevSecOps Principles Reinforced
Principle	Application
🔍 Shift-left	Scan IaC in PR before deployment
🧱 Defense in Depth	Secure networking, IAM, VPCs, roles
🧾 Auditability	All changes committed and versioned in Git

Title: Guided Lab – Secure Infrastructure with Terraform and AWS CDK

🎯 Lab Goals
By the end of this guided lab, you will:

✅ Terraform (for VPC, ECS, IAM)

✅ AWS CDK (TypeScript) (for CI/CD pipeline, secrets, and environment separation)

🧱 PART 1 – Provisioning Infrastructure with Terraform
📁 1.1 Project Structure
infra-terraform/
├── main.tf
├── vpc.tf
├── ecs.tf
├── iam.tf
├── outputs.tf
├── variables.tf
├── environments/
│   ├── dev.tfvars
│   ├── staging.tfvars

⚙️ 1.2 Initialize the Terraform Project
terraform init
This pulls the AWS provider and sets up the backend. (You can configure S3/DynamoDB for remote state later.)

🌐 1.3 VPC & Subnets – vpc.tf
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16"
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = {
    Name = "main-vpc"
  }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "public_a" {
  vpc_id     = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block = "10.0.1.0/24"
  availability_zone = "us-west-2a"
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
}
✅ This gives us one public subnet; you can extend to private + NAT for secure service communication.

🐳 1.4 ECS Cluster and Service – ecs.tf
resource "aws_ecs_cluster" "secure_cluster" {
  name = "secure-cluster"
}

resource "aws_ecs_task_definition" "auth_service" {
  family                   = "auth-service-task"
  network_mode             = "awsvpc"
  requires_compatibilities = ["FARGATE"]
  cpu                      = "256"
  memory                   = "512"

  container_definitions = jsonencode([
    {
      name      = "auth"
      image     = "123456789.dkr.ecr.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/auth-service:latest"
      essential = true
      portMappings = [
        {
          containerPort = 3000
          hostPort      = 3000
        }
      ]
    }
  ])
}

resource "aws_ecs_service" "auth" {
  name            = "auth-service"
  cluster         = aws_ecs_cluster.secure_cluster.id
  task_definition = aws_ecs_task_definition.auth_service.arn
  desired_count   = 2
  launch_type     = "FARGATE"
  network_configuration {
    subnets         = [aws_subnet.public_a.id]
    assign_public_ip = true
  }
}
🔐 Fargate with awsvpc isolates each task in its own ENI (Elastic Network Interface).

🛡️ 1.5 IAM Roles – iam.tf
resource "aws_iam_role" "ecs_task_exec" {
  name = "ecs-task-execution-role"
  assume_role_policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Action    = "sts:AssumeRole"
      Effect    = "Allow"
      Principal = {
        Service = "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com"
      }
    }]
  })
}

resource "aws_iam_role_policy_attachment" "ecs_task_execution" {
  role       = aws_iam_role.ecs_task_exec.name
  policy_arn = "arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/service-role/AmazonECSTaskExecutionRolePolicy"
}
🚫 DO NOT use AdministratorAccess. Always scope roles to the minimum required.

🔍 1.6 Scan with tfsec, checkov, tflint
tfsec .         # Detect misconfigurations and insecure defaults
checkov -d .    # Broader compliance scan (SOC2, HIPAA, etc.)
tflint          # Lint and enforce style, naming, etc.
Sample tfsec finding:

[MEDIUM] Resource 'aws_security_group_rule' allows ingress from 0.0.0.0/0
🚀 1.7 Deploy to AWS
terraform plan -var-file="environments/dev.tfvars"
terraform apply -var-file="environments/dev.tfvars"

🔰 2.2 Initialize CDK Project

cdk init app --language=typescript
🔐 2.3 Secrets Stack – lib/secrets-stack.ts
import * as cdk from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import * as secrets from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-secretsmanager';

export class SecretsStack extends cdk.Stack {
  constructor(scope: cdk.App, id: string) {
    super(scope, id);

    new secrets.Secret(this, 'AuthServiceSecret', {
      secretName: 'auth-service-db-secret',
      generateSecretString: {
        secretStringTemplate: JSON.stringify({ username: 'auth_user' }),
        generateStringKey: 'password',
        excludePunctuation: true
      }
    });
  }
}
💡 This secret can now be injected into your ECS task definition securely via IAM permissions.

🏗️ 2.4 CodePipeline + CodeBuild – pipeline-stack.ts
import * as pipelines from 'aws-cdk-lib/pipelines';

const pipeline = new pipelines.CodePipeline(this, 'SecurePipeline', {
  pipelineName: 'SecureAppPipeline',
  synth: new pipelines.ShellStep('Synth', {
    input: pipelines.CodePipelineSource.gitHub('your-org/your-repo', 'main'),
    commands: ['npm install', 'npm run build', 'npx cdk synth'],
  }),
});

🧪 ECS Task Definition with Secrets Injection (CDK)
// lib/auth-service-stack.ts

import * as ecs from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-ecs';
import * as secrets from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-secretsmanager';
import * as iam from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-iam';
import * as ec2 from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-ec2';
import * as ecs_patterns from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-ecs-patterns';

export class AuthServiceStack extends cdk.Stack {
  constructor(scope: Construct, id: string, props?: cdk.StackProps) {
    super(scope, id, props);

    const vpc = ec2.Vpc.fromLookup(this, 'VPC', { isDefault: true });

    const cluster = new ecs.Cluster(this, 'Cluster', { vpc });

    const secret = secrets.Secret.fromSecretNameV2(
      this,
      'ImportedSecret',
      'auth-service-db-secret'
    );

    const taskRole = new iam.Role(this, 'TaskRole', {
      assumedBy: new iam.ServicePrincipal('ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com'),
    });

    secret.grantRead(taskRole);

    const fargateService = new ecs_patterns.ApplicationLoadBalancedFargateService(this, 'FargateService', {
      cluster,
      cpu: 256,
      memoryLimitMiB: 512,
      taskImageOptions: {
        image: ecs.ContainerImage.fromRegistry('node:20-alpine'),
        containerPort: 3000,
        secrets: {
          DB_SECRET: ecs.Secret.fromSecretsManager(secret),
        },
        taskRole,
      },
      desiredCount: 2,
    });
  }
}
✅ Explanation:

The secret is passed securely into the container as an environment variable (DB_SECRET)

Only the taskRole is granted permission to read the secret — enforcing least privilege

🏗️ CodePipeline + CodeBuild (CDK)
Add to lib/pipeline-stack.ts:

import * as codepipeline from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-codepipeline';
import * as codepipeline_actions from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-codepipeline-actions';
import * as codebuild from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-codebuild';
import * as s3 from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-s3';

const artifactBucket = new s3.Bucket(this, 'ArtifactBucket', {
  versioned: true,
  removalPolicy: cdk.RemovalPolicy.DESTROY,
});

const sourceOutput = new codepipeline.Artifact();
const buildOutput = new codepipeline.Artifact();

const project = new codebuild.PipelineProject(this, 'BuildProject', {
  buildSpec: codebuild.BuildSpec.fromObject({
    version: '0.2',
    phases: {
      install: {
        commands: ['npm install'],
      },
      build: {
        commands: ['npm run build', 'npm run lint'],
      },
    },
    artifacts: {
      files: ['**/*'],
    },
  }),
});

new codepipeline.Pipeline(this, 'Pipeline', {
  artifactBucket,
  stages: [
    {
      stageName: 'Source',
      actions: [
        new codepipeline_actions.GitHubSourceAction({
          actionName: 'GitHub',
          owner: 'your-org',
          repo: 'your-repo',
          oauthToken: cdk.SecretValue.secretsManager('github-token'),
          output: sourceOutput,
          branch: 'main',
        }),
      ],
    },
    {
      stageName: 'Build',
      actions: [
        new codepipeline_actions.CodeBuildAction({
          actionName: 'CodeBuild',
          project,
          input: sourceOutput,
          outputs: [buildOutput],
        }),
      ],
    },
  ],
});
🌍 CDK Context for Multi-Environment Awareness
📄 cdk.json

{
  "context": {
    "envs": {
      "dev": {
        "account": "111111111111",
        "region": "us-west-2"
      },
      "staging": {
        "account": "222222222222",
        "region": "us-west-2"
      }
    }
  }
}
📄 bin/main.ts

const envName = process.env.ENV || 'dev';
const envConfig = app.node.tryGetContext('envs')[envName];

new AuthServiceStack(app, `AuthService-${envName}`, {
  env: {
    account: envConfig.account,
    region: envConfig.region,
  },
});
💡 Use ENV=staging cdk deploy to deploy into specific environment accounts with isolated stacks.

🔐 Fine-Grained IAM Role for ECS Tasks
Instead of giving ECS access to all logs/secrets, use inline IAM policy:

taskRole.addToPolicy(
  new iam.PolicyStatement({
    actions: ['secretsmanager:GetSecretValue'],
    resources: [secret.secretArn],
  })
);
📉 Drift Detection with driftctl
Install:

brew install driftctl
Run in Terraform root:

driftctl scan --from tfstate+s3://my-state-bucket/path/to/terraform.tfstate
🚨 Detects resources in AWS that are not in Terraform, helping prevent shadow infrastructure and drift.

💾 Terraform Remote State Backend
📄 backend.tf

terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "my-secure-terraform-state"
    key            = "dev/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-west-2"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"
    encrypt        = true
  }
}

📁 Week10/ChallengeLab.md
# 🧪 Week 10 – Challenge Lab: Securing and Remediating IaC in Production

## 🎯 Challenge Objectives

Your goal is to simulate a real-world DevSecOps incident where:

- Misconfigured infrastructure is discovered via static scans and drift detection
- Secrets were accidentally exposed in ECS container logs
- Overly permissive IAM roles are remediated
- Infrastructure is refactored to enforce secure-by-default patterns

---

## 🔧 Scenario Setup – Simulated Misconfiguration

### ✅ Step 1: Introduce Drift

Go to the AWS Console or use the AWS CLI and manually update a resource **outside of Terraform/CDK**.

Example:
```bash
aws ecs update-service \
  --cluster secure-cluster \
  --service auth-service \
  --desired-count 0
💡 This simulates unauthorized or accidental changes by a team member or system.

🔍 Step 2: Detect Drift Using driftctl
Run this from your Terraform root:

driftctl scan \
  --from tfstate+s3://my-secure-terraform-state/dev/terraform.tfstate \
  --deep
Expected Output:

Found 1 deleted resource outside of Terraform
[DELETED] aws_ecs_service.auth-service
✅ Fix:

terraform apply -var-file="environments/dev.tfvars"
🔐 Why: Drift can indicate security incidents, privilege misuse, or poor Git discipline. Always monitor infra with IaC-aware tools.

🧪 Scenario 2: Secret Appears in Logs
🔥 Problem:
A team member added a line to Dockerfile for debugging:

RUN echo "DB_PASSWORD=${DB_PASSWORD}" >> /var/log/app.log
It leaked into CloudWatch logs via ECS.

✅ Challenge:
Detect the leak via aws logs

Rotate the secret in AWS Secrets Manager

Re-deploy ECS service with new secret version

Steps:

aws secretsmanager rotate-secret --secret-id auth-service-db-secret

# Trigger ECS redeploy to refresh task definition
aws ecs update-service \
  --cluster secure-cluster \
  --service auth-service \
  --force-new-deployment
🔐 Best Practice: Rotate secrets immediately after suspected exposure. Add log-sanitizers to pipelines.

🛑 Scenario 3: Overly Permissive IAM Role
Problem:
The ECS task role has this in your iam.tf:

policy = jsonencode({
  Version = "2012-10-17",
  Statement = [{
    Effect = "Allow",
    Action = "*",
    Resource = "*"
  }]
})
✅ Task:
Replace * with only the needed permissions

Validate the refactor with tfsec, tflint, and checkov

Example Fix:

actions = [
  "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
  "logs:CreateLogStream",
  "logs:PutLogEvents"
]
resources = [
  aws_secretsmanager_secret.auth.arn,
  "arn:aws:logs:*:*:*"
]
🔒 Scenario 4: Block Insecure Deployments
Goal:
Prevent a deploy when:

Secrets are hardcoded

Public subnets expose sensitive services

IAM has wildcard permissions

✅ Task:
Add tfsec + checkov + cdk-nag as pre-commit or CI pipeline jobs

Fail the pipeline if security violations are found

Example for GitLab CI:

security_scan:
  stage: test
  image: aquasec/tfsec
  script:
    - tfsec .
  allow_failure: false
📍 DevSecOps Tie-in: This is "shift-left" security — catching violations before deployment.

✅ Completion Checklist
Task	Verified
Detected and fixed drift with driftctl	✅
Rotated secret after simulated log leak	✅
Refactored IAM role with least privilege	✅
Added security scanners to prevent insecure deploys	✅
Redeployed app with rotated secret and hardened infra	✅
🧠 Reflection Prompts
Include your answers in Reflection.md:

What signs might indicate infrastructure drift before you detect it explicitly?

How does IAM misconfiguration increase blast radius?

How would you build pipeline stages to halt deployment if tfsec/checkov fail?

What would you log and alert on in production to catch these issues early?

🚀 Bonus Challenge
Enable Terraform Sentinel or OPA to enforce:

All IAM policies must be scoped by resource and not use "*"

No public subnets in prod environment

All secrets must originate from aws_secretsmanager_secret

📁 Week10/Security.md
# 🔐 Week 10 – Security: Infrastructure as Code (IaC) with Terraform, CDK, and SAM

---

## 🔍 Core DevSecOps Security Concepts

| Concept | Description | Relevance |
|--------|-------------|-----------|
| Principle of Least Privilege (PoLP) | Only give services the exact permissions they need | Reduces blast radius in case of a breach |
| Immutable Infrastructure | Avoid modifying infra manually; always redeploy via code | Ensures repeatability, auditability, and prevents config drift |
| Infrastructure Drift Detection | Detect and reconcile changes made outside of IaC | Prevents out-of-band changes or shadow infrastructure |
| Secrets Management | Store secrets in managed vaults (not in code or env files) | Protects credentials, tokens, and sensitive data |
| Environment Isolation | Each environment should have its own infra, secrets, and roles | Reduces accidental cross-environment data leakage |

---

## ✅ Terraform-Specific Security Practices

### 🔐 Secret Handling

- **✅ Use `aws_secretsmanager_secret`** instead of `variable` blocks
- **❌ Don’t use `plain_text` env vars** or write secrets in `*.tfvars`

### 🔍 Static Security Scans

- **`tfsec`**: Static analysis with cloud-provider-specific checks
- **`checkov`**: Full IaC security policies and benchmarks (CIS, NIST, SOC2)
- **`tflint`**: Detects syntax and logic errors

```bash
tfsec . && checkov -d . && tflint
✅ Least Privilege IAM
Instead of:

resource "aws_iam_policy" {
  actions = ["*"]
  resources = ["*"]
}
Use:

actions = [
  "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
  "logs:PutLogEvents"
]
resources = [aws_secretsmanager_secret.my_secret.arn]
✅ AWS CDK Security Practices
✅ SecretsManager Integration
Inject secrets at runtime using ecs.Secret.fromSecretsManager()

Never define .env or config files with secrets in your CDK code

Avoid toString() or .value calls on secrets — use directly as SecretValue

🧪 CDK Security Scanning
Use cdk-nag for best practices:

Aspects.of(this).add(new AwsSolutionsChecks());
Enforces:

No public access on S3

Encryption on resources

Valid IAM trust relationships

✅ SAM-Specific Security Notes
Define secrets in Parameters and inject them via Environment.SecretsManager

Prefer AWS-managed Policies blocks over custom wildcard policies

Use Metadata to mark parameters as sensitive (useful in scans)

Example:

Environment:
  Variables:
    DB_SECRET: !Ref MySecret
🛡️ CI/CD Security Enforcement
Tool	Where	Why
tfsec, checkov, tflint	Pre-commit & CI/CD pipelines	Block insecure changes before deploy
OIDC + short-lived tokens	CDK/Terraform deploy roles	Prevent key leaks and stale access
driftctl or infracost	CI/CD or cron	Detect unplanned drift or cost spikes
Secrets rotation logic	CodeBuild step or Lambda	Enforce periodic rotation + audit logs
🔐 Common Misconfigurations & Their Fixes
❌ Insecure Practice	✅ Secure Alternative
IAM: "*" on all resources	Specific actions and resources
Secrets in terraform.tfvars	Use SecretsManager + data sources
docker run -e SECRET=value	Use ECS or Kubernetes secret injectors
Console-based infra edits	Use IaC tools only + detect drift
Overuse of default VPC	Create and secure isolated VPCs per env
Shared state across environments	Separate S3 backends for dev, staging, prod
🧠 DevSecOps Mental Models
“If it’s not in Git, it doesn’t exist.”

“Code is the boundary between intention and reality.”

“Static scanners enforce discipline. Drift detection enforces truth.”

“Every IAM wildcard is an unguarded door.”

# 🧠 Week 10 – Reflection: Infrastructure as Code & Security

---

## 🌱 What I Learned

This week solidified the idea that **IaC is not just about provisioning infrastructure**, but about ensuring **repeatability, security, and visibility** in cloud environments. I learned to:

- Deploy ECS, VPCs, subnets, IAM, and Secrets via **Terraform and CDK**
- Securely inject secrets into ECS containers using **SecretsManager**
- Implement **multi-environment awareness** in CDK with context handling
- Detect unauthorized changes via **`driftctl`**
- Add **static analysis and security gates** to my IaC lifecycle
- Use **IAM roles with scoped-down permissions** to enforce least privilege
- Separate environments and use remote state storage to prevent conflicts
- Think of infrastructure as part of my secure application lifecycle

---

## 🔄 How the App Evolved

The `auth-service` component of our app is now:

- Deployed using **Fargate**, with **no public IP**, and inside an **isolated VPC**
- Injected with secrets from **SecretsManager** — not using `.env` files
- Run via **secure IAM roles** that only allow `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` and logging
- Positioned for future integration into a full **CI/CD pipeline** (CodePipeline via CDK)

This evolution reflects **real-world enterprise standards** where security, reproducibility, and isolation are critical.

---

## ⚠️ Key Challenges & Takeaways

| Challenge | Takeaway |
|----------|----------|
| Switching between Terraform and CDK | Important to understand trade-offs — CDK is great for tight AWS integration and abstraction, Terraform is better for cloud-agnostic workflows |
| Secrets handling in ECS | Misuse of environment variables is risky — SecretsManager + IAM + runtime injection is preferred |
| IAM least privilege | Requires careful attention and testing — but drastically improves security posture |
| Drift detection | Driftctl is invaluable in identifying shadow changes not tracked in Git |

---

## 🧠 Real-World Reflection Questions

### ❓ How would I design secure, environment-specific infrastructure for multiple clients?

- Separate VPCs and S3 buckets per tenant
- Use IAM boundaries and SCPs to limit blast radius
- Tag resources for cost and security tracing
- Use encrypted tfstate backends with isolated DynamoDB locks

---

### ❓ What practices would help my team avoid drift and insecure deployments?

- Enforce IaC-only deployments (no console editing)
- Run `driftctl` on schedule or before `terraform plan`
- Integrate `tfsec`, `checkov`, and `cdk-nag` in every PR pipeline
- Rotate secrets on schedule and monitor for changes

---

### ❓ What DevSecOps mindset shifts did this week reinforce?

- Security must start **before** deployment — during design
- IAM and secrets are code artifacts — treat them like application logic
- Every resource change should be traceable to a Git commit
- If someone can modify infra outside of Git, you don’t have full control

---

## ✅ I Now Feel Confident In:

- Creating, securing, and deploying cloud infra with Terraform/CDK
- Applying DevSecOps principles to infrastructure components
- Building secure deployment pipelines that can evolve with my application

---

## 🧭 What’s Next?

Next week I’ll shift focus from ECS to **Kubernetes security** — starting with:

- RBAC (Role-Based Access Control)
- Network Policies
- Namespace isolation
- Service Accounts and workload identity

Kubernetes introduces **more flexibility but more complexity** — and that’s where security principles really shine.

# 🧱 Extended Guided Lab – Terraform Modules in a Real-World Project

## 🎯 Objective

Refactor your flat Terraform infrastructure into reusable, secure **modules** for:

- VPC networking
- ECS Cluster + Service
- SecretsManager
- IAM roles

You’ll also learn how to:
- Use `locals`, `outputs`, and `data` sources across modules
- Apply security scanning to modules
- Structure your repo like a real-world IaC platform team

---

## 🗂️ Step 1: Real-World Terraform Project Structure

```bash
infra/
├── main.tf
├── variables.tf
├── outputs.tf
├── environments/
│   ├── dev/
│   │   └── terraform.tfvars
│   └── staging/
│       └── terraform.tfvars
├── modules/
│   ├── vpc/
│   │   ├── main.tf
│   │   ├── variables.tf
│   │   └── outputs.tf
│   ├── ecs/
│   ├── iam/
│   └── secrets/
└── backend.tf
🌐 Step 2: VPC Module
📁 modules/vpc/main.tf

resource "aws_vpc" "this" {
  cidr_block           = var.cidr
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = {
    Name = "${var.env}-vpc"
  }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  count                   = length(var.public_subnet_cidrs)
  vpc_id                  = aws_vpc.this.id
  cidr_block              = var.public_subnet_cidrs[count.index]
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
  availability_zone       = element(data.aws_availability_zones.available.names, count.index)

  tags = {
    Name = "${var.env}-public-subnet-${count.index}"
  }
}

data "aws_availability_zones" "available" {}
📁 modules/vpc/variables.tf

variable "env" {}
variable "cidr" {}
variable "public_subnet_cidrs" {
  type = list(string)
}
📁 modules/vpc/outputs.tf

output "vpc_id" {
  value = aws_vpc.this.id
}

output "public_subnets" {
  value = aws_subnet.public[*].id
}
🧱 Step 3: ECS Module
📁 modules/ecs/main.tf (partial)

resource "aws_ecs_cluster" "this" {
  name = "${var.env}-cluster"
}

resource "aws_ecs_task_definition" "app" {
  family                   = "${var.env}-auth-task"
  network_mode             = "awsvpc"
  requires_compatibilities = ["FARGATE"]
  cpu                      = "256"
  memory                   = "512"
  execution_role_arn       = var.execution_role_arn

  container_definitions = jsonencode([
    {
      name      = "auth-service"
      image     = var.image
      cpu       = 256
      memory    = 512
      essential = true
      secrets   = [
        {
          name      = "DB_PASSWORD"
          valueFrom = var.secret_arn
        }
      ]
    }
  ])
}
📁 modules/ecs/variables.tf

variable "env" {}
variable "image" {}
variable "execution_role_arn" {}
variable "secret_arn" {}
variable "public_subnets" {
  type = list(string)
}
🔐 Step 4: IAM Module
📁 modules/iam/main.tf

resource "aws_iam_role" "ecs_task_exec" {
  name = "${var.env}-ecs-task-exec"
  assume_role_policy = data.aws_iam_policy_document.assume_role.json
}

data "aws_iam_policy_document" "assume_role" {
  statement {
    actions = ["sts:AssumeRole"]
    principals {
      type        = "Service"
      identifiers = ["ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com"]
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_role_policy_attachment" "exec_policy" {
  role       = aws_iam_role.ecs_task_exec.name
  policy_arn = "arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/service-role/AmazonECSTaskExecutionRolePolicy"
}
📁 modules/iam/outputs.tf

output "execution_role_arn" {
  value = aws_iam_role.ecs_task_exec.arn
}
🔑 Step 5: Secrets Module
📁 modules/secrets/main.tf

resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret" "auth" {
  name        = "${var.env}/auth/db"
  description = "Auth service DB password"
}
📁 modules/secrets/outputs.tf

output "secret_arn" {
  value = aws_secretsmanager_secret.auth.arn
}
🧪 Step 6: Assemble It All in main.tf
📁 infra/main.tf (partial)

module "vpc" {
  source              = "./modules/vpc"
  env                 = var.env
  cidr                = "10.0.0.0/16"
  public_subnet_cidrs = ["10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"]
}

module "iam" {
  source = "./modules/iam"
  env    = var.env
}

module "secrets" {
  source = "./modules/secrets"
  env    = var.env
}

module "ecs" {
  source            = "./modules/ecs"
  env               = var.env
  image             = "myrepo/auth-service:latest"
  public_subnets    = module.vpc.public_subnets
  execution_role_arn = module.iam.execution_role_arn
  secret_arn         = module.secrets.secret_arn
}
✅ Step 7: Scan and Secure
tfsec ./modules
checkov -d ./modules
tflint ./modules

Modules improve reuse, security, and team collaboration

Avoid putting secrets, passwords, or large inline JSON in root files

Group modules by functionality (network, compute, secrets, IAM)

# 🔧 Advanced CDK – CrossStack, Secrets, and Secure Constructs

## 🎯 Objective

You’ll build a **modular CDK project** that deploys a secure ECS service with:

- 🔐 Secrets injection via AWS Secrets Manager
- 📦 CrossStack resource sharing (VPC, roles, secrets)
- 🧱 Reusable CDK constructs
- ✅ Security scanning with `cdk-nag`

---

## 🗂️ Step 1: Real CDK Project Structure

```bash
cdk-app/
├── bin/
│   └── app.ts                # Entry point
├── lib/
│   ├── network-stack.ts      # VPC + Subnets
│   ├── secrets-stack.ts      # SecretsManager
│   ├── iam-stack.ts          # IAM roles
│   └── ecs-stack.ts          # ECS service + Task
├── constructs/
│   ├── ecs-service.ts        # Custom construct
├── cdk.json
└── package.json
🔁 Step 2: Bootstrap and Install Dependencies
npm install -g aws-cdk
cdk init app --language typescript

npm install @aws-cdk/aws-ecs @aws-cdk/aws-ec2 \
            @aws-cdk/aws-iam @aws-cdk/aws-secretsmanager \
            constructs cdk-nag
🌐 Step 3: Create VPC Stack
📁 lib/network-stack.ts

import { Stack, StackProps } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import { Vpc } from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-ec2';
import { Construct } from 'constructs';

export class NetworkStack extends Stack {
  public readonly vpc: Vpc;

  constructor(scope: Construct, id: string, props?: StackProps) {
    super(scope, id, props);

    this.vpc = new Vpc(this, 'AppVPC', {
      maxAzs: 2,
      natGateways: 1
    });
  }
}
🔐 Step 4: Secrets Stack
📁 lib/secrets-stack.ts

import { Stack } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import { Secret } from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-secretsmanager';
import { Construct } from 'constructs';

export class SecretsStack extends Stack {
  public readonly dbSecret: Secret;

  constructor(scope: Construct, id: string) {
    super(scope, id);

    this.dbSecret = new Secret(this, 'DbSecret', {
      secretName: 'auth-service/db-password'
    });
  }
}
🔐 Step 5: IAM Stack
📁 lib/iam-stack.ts

import { Stack } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import { Role, ServicePrincipal, ManagedPolicy } from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-iam';
import { Construct } from 'constructs';

export class IAMStack extends Stack {
  public readonly executionRole: Role;

  constructor(scope: Construct, id: string) {
    super(scope, id);

    this.executionRole = new Role(this, 'TaskExecRole', {
      assumedBy: new ServicePrincipal('ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com'),
      managedPolicies: [
        ManagedPolicy.fromAwsManagedPolicyName('service-role/AmazonECSTaskExecutionRolePolicy'),
        ManagedPolicy.fromAwsManagedPolicyName('SecretsManagerReadWrite')
      ]
    });
  }
}
📦 Step 6: ECS Service as a Reusable Construct
📁 constructs/ecs-service.ts

import { Construct } from 'constructs';
import {
  Cluster, FargateTaskDefinition, ContainerImage, FargateService
} from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-ecs';
import { Secret } from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-secretsmanager';
import { Role } from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-iam';
import { SubnetType, Vpc } from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-ec2';

interface ECSProps {
  vpc: Vpc;
  role: Role;
  secret: Secret;
}

export class ECSServiceConstruct extends Construct {
  constructor(scope: Construct, id: string, props: ECSProps) {
    super(scope, id);

    const cluster = new Cluster(this, 'Cluster', { vpc: props.vpc });

    const taskDef = new FargateTaskDefinition(this, 'TaskDef', {
      executionRole: props.role,
    });

    taskDef.addContainer('AppContainer', {
      image: ContainerImage.fromRegistry('node:18'),
      memoryLimitMiB: 512,
      secrets: {
        DB_PASSWORD: props.secret.secretValue,
      }
    });

    new FargateService(this, 'Service', {
      cluster,
      taskDefinition: taskDef,
      assignPublicIp: false,
      vpcSubnets: { subnetType: SubnetType.PRIVATE_WITH_EGRESS }
    });
  }
}
🧱 Step 7: Compose It All in bin/app.ts
📁 bin/app.ts

import { App } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import { NetworkStack } from '../lib/network-stack';
import { IAMStack } from '../lib/iam-stack';
import { SecretsStack } from '../lib/secrets-stack';
import { Stack } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import { ECSServiceConstruct } from '../constructs/ecs-service';

const app = new App();

const network = new NetworkStack(app, 'NetworkStack');
const iam = new IAMStack(app, 'IAMStack');
const secrets = new SecretsStack(app, 'SecretsStack');

new Stack(app, 'AppStack').node.addDependency(network);

new ECSServiceConstruct(app, 'ECSConstruct', {
  vpc: network.vpc,
  role: iam.executionRole,
  secret: secrets.dbSecret
});
🔒 Step 8: Enforce Security with cdk-nag
📁 bin/app.ts (add at the top)

import { Aspects } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
import { AwsSolutionsChecks } from 'cdk-nag';

Aspects.of(app).add(new AwsSolutionsChecks());
Then run:

cdk synth
cdk deploy
You’ll get security warnings like:

❌ Public access enabled on S3 (if added)

❌ No logging on load balancers

✅ Encrypted SecretsManager secrets

# 🧠 Reflection: Advanced CDK Constructs & Secure Infrastructure as Code

---

## 🌱 What I Learned

This section pushed my understanding of AWS CDK beyond the basics. I gained deep insight into:

- Building **modular infrastructure** using reusable CDK constructs
- Structuring **multi-stack projects** to represent real-world cloud architectures
- Securely injecting secrets into ECS tasks using **Secrets Manager**
- Assigning **least-privilege IAM roles** to task definitions
- Composing loosely coupled infrastructure components (VPC, ECS, IAM, Secrets)
- Scanning my CDK code using `cdk-nag` to meet **CIS/NIST standards**
- Using **context variables** to define and isolate environments like `dev`, `qa`, and `prod`

---

## 🔄 How the App Evolved

- Our `auth-service` now runs in an ECS Fargate cluster provisioned with **secure CDK constructs**.
- Secrets (like DB credentials) are no longer hardcoded — they are pulled securely from **AWS Secrets Manager** at runtime.
- The ECS task now runs under a **minimal-privilege IAM role**, created and attached automatically.
- The application lives in an **isolated VPC**, deployed from a **reusable NetworkStack**.
- The entire infrastructure is deployed using code that passes **security posture checks** thanks to `cdk-nag`.

This mirrors the exact type of architecture used in large, security-sensitive AWS deployments.

---

## ⚠️ Key Challenges & Takeaways

| Challenge | Takeaway |
|----------|----------|
| Composing cross-stack resources (VPC, secrets, IAM) | Use constructs or export stack outputs for clean separation of concerns |
| SecretsManager permissions | IAM must include `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` scoped to specific ARNs |
| Managing environment context via `cdk.json` | CDK context handling is powerful but requires planning |
| Understanding `constructs` vs `stacks` | Constructs encapsulate functionality for reuse — critical in large orgs |

---

## ❓ Real-World Reflection Questions

### 🔐 How would I scale CDK across multiple teams?

- Create shared `constructs` and publish them to an internal npm registry
- Use **stacks per domain** (network, compute, identity) to avoid coupling
- Leverage **CDK Pipelines** for promoting across `dev → staging → prod`
- Enforce security policies using `cdk-nag` + GitHub PR checks

---

### 🧠 When is CDK preferable over Terraform?

| Scenario | Use CDK? |
|----------|----------|
| Full AWS-native workflows | ✅ Yes (tight SDK integration) |
| Multi-cloud (GCP, Azure) support | ❌ No (prefer Terraform) |
| Strong TypeScript/DevX requirement | ✅ Yes (dev-focused teams) |
| Large shared modules between teams | ✅ Yes (constructs are composable) |

- Building secure ECS deployments using **AWS CDK**
- Applying the **principles of modularization and isolation** to infrastructure
- Injecting and securing application secrets without hardcoding
- Writing **DevSecOps-ready IaC** that’s reviewable, repeatable, and auditable
- Scanning CDK code using **automated security policies**

# 🔧 Advanced IaC with AWS SAM – Secure Serverless API for Auth Service

---

## 🎯 Objective

Use **AWS SAM** to define a secure, environment-aware **serverless auth API** that includes:

- 🔐 Secrets from AWS Secrets Manager
- 🔧 IAM roles and policies
- 🛡️ Parameter protections and sensitivity markers
- 🔁 API Gateway routing
- 📦 Lambda packaging and build
- 🧪 Deployment automation

---

## 🗂️ Recommended Directory Structure

```bash
auth-api/
├── template.yaml
├── .aws-sam/
├── events/
│   └── test-event.json
├── src/
│   └── auth/
│       ├── app.py
│       └── requirements.txt
├── env.json
├── samconfig.toml
└── README.md
🔧 Step 1: Initialize the Project
sam init
Choose: 1 - AWS Quick Start Templates

Runtime: Python 3.11

Template: Hello World Example

Name it: auth-api

🔐 Step 2: Annotated template.yaml
AWSTemplateFormatVersion: '2010-09-09'
Transform: AWS::Serverless-2016-10-31
Description: >
  Secure Auth API using Lambda + API Gateway + Secrets

Globals:
  Function:
    Timeout: 10
    Runtime: python3.11
    Tracing: Active
    Environment:
      Variables:
        LOG_LEVEL: info

Parameters:
  Env:
    Type: String
    Default: dev
    Description: "Environment (dev/staging/prod)"

Resources:

  AuthSecret:
    Type: AWS::SecretsManager::Secret
    Properties:
      Name: !Sub "${Env}/auth/db"
      Description: Auth DB password

  AuthFunction:
    Type: AWS::Serverless::Function
    Properties:
      FunctionName: !Sub "${Env}-auth-fn"
      CodeUri: src/auth/
      Handler: app.lambda_handler
      Policies:
        - AWSSecretsManagerGetSecretValuePolicy:
            SecretArn: !Ref AuthSecret
      Environment:
        Variables:
          SECRET_ARN: !Ref AuthSecret

  AuthApi:
    Type: AWS::Serverless::Api
    Properties:
      Name: !Sub "${Env}-auth-api"
      StageName: v1
      Cors:
        AllowMethods: "'POST,OPTIONS'"
        AllowHeaders: "'Content-Type'"
        AllowOrigin: "'*'"

  AuthApiResource:
    Type: AWS::Serverless::Function
    Properties:
      FunctionName: !Sub "${Env}-auth-api"
      CodeUri: src/auth/
      Handler: app.lambda_handler
      Events:
        ApiRequest:
          Type: Api
          Properties:
            Path: /login
            Method: POST
            RestApiId: !Ref AuthApi

Outputs:
  ApiUrl:
    Description: "Invoke URL"
    Value: !Sub "https://${AuthApi}.execute-api.${AWS::Region}.amazonaws.com/v1/login"
🔒 Step 3: Add Sensitive Marker to Parameters (Optional)
Metadata:
  AWS::CloudFormation::Interface:
    ParameterGroups:
      - Label:
          default: "Environment Settings"
        Parameters:
          - Env
    ParameterLabels:
      Env:
        default: "Deployment Environment"
This allows tools like CloudFormation Guard or sceptre to enforce policies.

🧠 Step 4: src/auth/app.py – Secure Auth Logic
import json
import boto3
import os

secrets_client = boto3.client('secretsmanager')

def get_secret():
    secret_arn = os.environ['SECRET_ARN']
    response = secrets_client.get_secret_value(SecretId=secret_arn)
    return response['SecretString']

def lambda_handler(event, context):
    try:
        db_password = get_secret()
        return {
            "statusCode": 200,
            "body": json.dumps({
                "message": "Auth successful",
                "db_password": db_password  # ⚠️ Replace with token in prod
            })
        }
    except Exception as e:
        return {
            "statusCode": 500,
            "body": json.dumps({"error": str(e)})
        }
🧪 Step 5: Local Testing
sam build
sam local invoke AuthFunction --event events/test-event.json

Configure for Environment
📁 env.json

{
  "Env": "dev"
}
Deploy:
sam deploy --guided
Follow prompts to create a deployment bucket, save samconfig.toml, and set default parameters.

🛡️ Step 7: Add Security Tools (Optional)
cfn-nag: cfn_nag_scan --input-path template.yaml

CloudFormation Guard (cfn-guard):

Write rules to enforce:

Environment parameters must be present

No public S3 buckets or wildcards in IAM policies

📦 Step 8: Package for CI/CD
sam package \
  --template-file template.yaml \
  --output-template-file packaged.yaml \
  --s3-bucket my-deployment-artifacts

sam deploy \
  --template-file packaged.yaml \
  --stack-name auth-api-stack \
  --capabilities CAPABILITY_IAM

# 🧠 Reflection & Tool Comparison – SAM vs CDK vs Terraform in DevSecOps

---

## ✅ Reflection: SAM Serverless Infrastructure

Working with AWS SAM gave me a strong foundation in building **secure, API-driven serverless applications**. Here's what I now understand:

- How to **inject secrets from Secrets Manager** into Lambda functions
- Structuring a `template.yaml` file with **secure IAM policies**, parameters, and outputs
- Using `sam local` to simulate function execution and API Gateway integration
- Applying **DevSecOps principles**: parameterization, least privilege IAM, no hardcoded values, separation of concerns
- Scanning CloudFormation templates with `cfn-nag` or `cfn-guard` for policy violations

---

## 🔄 Tool Decision Flow – CDK vs Terraform vs SAM

| Use Case | Recommended Tool | Why |
|----------|------------------|-----|
| 🛠️ Multi-cloud IaC | **Terraform** | Broad provider support, mature module ecosystem |
| ☁️ AWS-native IaC with full SDK power | **CDK** | Code-driven, object-oriented IaC, great for large AWS teams |
| ⚡ Event-driven apps (APIs, Lambdas) | **SAM** | Optimized for Lambda, integrates with API Gateway & CloudWatch |
| 🧱 Infrastructure with shared constructs | **CDK** | Reusable constructs, rich programming model |
| 💼 Regulated IaC with prebuilt scans | **Terraform** | Easy to integrate with tools like tfsec, checkov |
| 🔐 Secrets + Lambda with min config | **SAM** | Minimal YAML, automatic IAM, fast for small serverless apps |
| 🧪 Local testing before deploy | **SAM** | `sam local` for Lambda & API Gateway simulation |
| 🧩 Complex ECS, Fargate apps | **CDK** or **Terraform** | CDK is easier for service composition; Terraform offers multi-cloud repeatability |

---

## 🧩 DevSecOps-Specific Comparison

| Feature | Terraform | CDK | SAM |
|--------|-----------|-----|-----|
| Secrets handling | 🔒 Strong (via `aws_secretsmanager_secret`) | 🔒 Very strong with `SecretValue` & constructs | 🔒 Built-in |
| IAM Role definition | ✅ Granular | ✅ Fine-grained (with L2 constructs) | ✅ Auto-generated or manual |
| Cross-stack references | ✅ via outputs and `data` | ✅ via strong typing & object reuse | ⚠️ Limited |
| Secure Defaults | ❌ Must define explicitly | ✅ L2 constructs often default to secure settings | ✅ Very secure by default |
| Built-in security scanning | ✅ tfsec, checkov | ✅ cdk-nag | ⚠️ Needs external tools like `cfn-nag` |
| Language | HCL | TypeScript, Python, Java | YAML (some Python for logic) |
| CI/CD integration | ✅ Easy with GitHub, GitLab, Jenkins | ✅ With CDK Pipelines or GitHub | ✅ Supports CodePipeline, GitHub |

---

## 🤔 When Would I Use Each?

### ✅ Terraform
- You’re building for **multi-cloud**, or using **modular IaC at scale**
- Want **tool-agnostic** security scanning and compliance workflows
- Team prefers **declarative**, predictable syntax

### ✅ CDK
- You need to compose **custom reusable infrastructure patterns**
- You want strong **TypeScript/Python programming ergonomics**
- You're building a **large AWS-native platform**

### ✅ SAM
- You want to **rapidly deploy serverless APIs**
- You need **minimal YAML** to get started with **secure defaults**
- You care about **fast local testing** of Lambda/API Gateway

---

## 🔐 DevSecOps Best Practices Recap (All Tools)

| Practice | Description |
|---------|-------------|
| Use environment variables and parameterization | Avoid hardcoded secrets or region-specific logic |
| Inject secrets via AWS Secrets Manager | Never store secrets in source code or `env` files |
| Scan IaC templates early | Use `tfsec`, `checkov`, `cdk-nag`, `cfn-guard`, `kics` |
| Assign least privilege IAM roles | Use managed policies and restrict by resource where possible |
| Separate environments logically | Use `workspace`, `context`, or `parameter overrides` per environment |
| Store IaC in version control | Enable auditability and change tracking |
| Use automation pipelines | Deploy and scan from GitHub Actions, GitLab CI/CD, or CodePipeline |

---

- Build secure, serverless infrastructure with **SAM**
- Compose reusable constructs with **CDK**
- Deploy cloud-native apps using **Terraform modules**

# 🔧 Week 11 – Kubernetes Security I: RBAC, Network Policies & Admission Controls

---

## 🎯 Goal

By the end of this module, you'll understand how to **secure access to Kubernetes clusters** using:

- Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)
- Network segmentation using NetworkPolicies
- Admission control using built-in and custom plugins

All within a **real-world microservices application**, as we deploy parts of our evolving app (e.g. `auth-service`) into a **secure Kubernetes namespace**.

---

## 📚 Core DevSecOps Concepts

| Concept | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| 🔐 RBAC | Fine-grained control over **who can do what** in the cluster |
| 🌐 Network Policies | Control **who can talk to whom** over the network |
| 🧩 Admission Controllers | Gate incoming resource requests (e.g. prevent privileged pods) |
| 🔄 Principle of Least Privilege | Only give the permissions needed — no more, no less |
| 🧱 Namespaces & Isolation | Organize resources and isolate environments securely |
| 📜 Service Accounts & Boundaries | Workload identity tied to permissions, not users |

---

## 🧠 Why This Matters

Modern Kubernetes clusters can become **attack surfaces** if:

- RBAC is overly permissive
- Workloads run as root or with `hostNetwork: true`
- Anyone can deploy insecure manifests
- Pods can talk to other services or databases without restriction

This week gives you the **core tools to defend your cluster**, enforce compliance, and practice **Zero Trust principles inside Kubernetes**.

---

## 🧱 How the App Evolves This Week

| Area | Evolution |
|------|-----------|
| Auth Service | Now deployed into Kubernetes with a dedicated namespace (`auth-dev`) |
| RBAC | Only DevSecOps engineers can deploy to this namespace |
| ServiceAccount | Each microservice gets a unique identity |
| NetworkPolicy | `auth-service` can only talk to the `db` service, not other pods |
| Admission | Pod creation is blocked if it lacks labels or tries to run as root |

---

## 🛠️ Tools You'll Use

- `kubectl`, `kustomize`
- `RBAC` YAMLs (Role, RoleBinding, ClusterRole, ServiceAccount)
- `NetworkPolicy`
- `Admission Controllers` (built-in + preview for OPA/Gatekeeper)

Optional (for deeper visibility):
- `k9s`, `kubescape`, `kubewarden`

---

## 📦 Learning Outcomes

✅ Define secure namespaces with scoped permissions  
✅ Apply RBAC roles to users, teams, and service accounts  
✅ Enforce ingress/egress policies using `NetworkPolicy`  
✅ Restrict workload privileges using admission policies  
✅ Build deployable, secure Kubernetes manifests  
✅ Enable auditability and compliance on resource creation  

---

## 🔐 Real-World Use Case

Your org runs multiple environments (dev, staging, prod) in a shared cluster.  
You want:

- Developers to **deploy only into their own namespaces**
- Sensitive services to **only talk to their dependencies**
- A system that **rejects dangerous manifests** (e.g., running as root)

This week sets the foundation for **policy-as-code**, **runtime security**, and **zero trust enforcement** in Kubernetes.

---

## 🔄 Coming Up Next

🔒 Week 12 will continue building on this foundation with:

- ECS Security Best Practices  
- Runtime Isolation  
- Cross-environment policies  
- Secure sidecars and service mesh (if enabled)

---

## ✅ Prerequisites

- Docker Desktop with Kubernetes enabled **OR** access to a K8s cluster (EKS, GKE, or Minikube)
- `kubectl` installed and configured
- Kustomize (for managing manifests)
- Helm (optional but useful later)
